## Women’s work and family-building patterns during the First Demographic Transition: A longitudinal study of Derbyshire, 1881-1911

## Emma Grace Diduch

### Corpus Christi College December 2025

This thesis is submitted for the degree of Doctor of Philosophy

i

PREFACE

This thesis is the result of my own work and includes nothing which is the outcome of work done in collaboration except as declared in the preface and specified in the text. It is not substantially the same as any work that has already been submitted, or is being concurrently submitted, for any degree, diploma or other qualification at the University of Cambridge or any other University or similar institution except as declared in the preface and specified in the text. It does not exceed the prescribed word limit for the relevant Degree Committee.

Versions of three chapters of this thesis have been published as open-access journal articles or released as working papers prior to submission for examination. A version of Chapter 3 was published as ‘The record linking glass ceiling. Applying automated methods to the census and women’s marriage records, 1881-1911’ in Historical Life Course Studies (Diduch, 2024). A version of Chapter 4 was released as a working paper in the Cambridge Working Papers in Economic and Social History series (Diduch, 2025a). A version of Chapter 5 was published in The History of the Family (Diduch, 2025b). These papers have all been edited for inclusion in the thesis.

ii iii

#### ABSTRACT

This thesis examines the impact of women’s employment on marital fertility during the First Demographic Transition in late nineteenth-century Derbyshire by creating a novel dataset of linked census, civil registration, and archival records from between 1881 and 1911. Previous research on fertility decline in England and Wales has been limited by the cross-sectional or retrospective nature of available data. The fertility survey included in the 1911 census established that marital fertility was falling from the 1870s, and that births per couple differed substantially between social classes and occupational groups. Previously, these characteristics have been measured only for husbands and not wives, reflecting the biases of historical sources. The recent release and digitization of individual-level data allow more detailed examination of diversity within these broad categories.

My study of Derbyshire provides a comparative perspective on marriage and fertility in districts with textile mills, coal-mining and agricultural districts, and growing urban centres. In addition to demographic data from the census enumerators’ books, I collect material from the archive of the Strutt cotton spinning mills in Belper which provides granular information on employment, including job titles, wages, and hours worked. By working with a sample of records from one county, I create a sufficiently large dataset which I use to demonstrate new and adapted methodologies which have wider applications.

In the first chapter, I present new automated methods for linking both women and men across censuses by incorporating indexes to marriage registers to capture women’s transitions from maiden to married names. I reduce the gender gap in record linking, creating a more representative sample, and retrieve information about women’s pre-marital households and occupations. In addition to enabling a life-cycle perspective on women’s work, the linked data also augment the fertility information contained in the 1911 census. The next chapter of the thesis describes the reconstruction of complete birth histories for married women using imputed birthdates for children who are not observed with their mothers because they had died or left the household before the census date. This information is crucial for evaluating changes in the timing as well as the total number of births.

In the next chapter, I use the linked data and reconstructed birth histories to define childbearing sequences for three cohorts of women. I characterize these sequences into groups using cluster analysis and demonstrate that both husbands’ and wives’ occupations before marriage had significant effects on the likelihood of following a low fertility trajectory. I argue that the combined effects of couples’ occupational histories are important for explaining the low levels of fertility found in textile districts, which were defined by a high degree of assortative mating between

iv

textile workers as well as high levels of women’s labour force participation. I also find, contrary to some hypotheses about the vertical diffusion of birth control, that women formerly employed as domestic servants had the largest families of any female occupational group. I discuss these results within a theoretical framework concerning the ability of couples to negotiate and act on changing fertility goals, conditioned on their relative ages, social backgrounds, and contributions to the family wage economy.

In the final three chapters, I examine the transitions to marriage and to each birth using cure models, also known as split population models, to analyse the factors related to the timing and occurrence of each event separately. These models distinguish between groups which took relatively longer to marry or had longer birth intervals from those who were more likely to remain unmarried or who stopped childbearing at a particular parity. Previous research suggests that postponing first births was an important characteristic of textile districts, but scholars have struggled to demonstrate this conclusively with existing data. I find that the differences in average birth intervals between female occupational groups are significant but only partly explained by the likelihood of continuing in work after marriage. In contrast to their adoption of family limitation within marriage, I also find that female textile workers were advantaged on the marriage market within textile districts. I examine these dynamics more closely using the Strutt factory records to identify family employment groups and to contextualize the age, sex, and wage structures of the workforce. This research reveals the intersections of labour markets, marriage markets, and the family economy through their effects on demographic outcomes.

“One is bound to ask whether couples or regions, as it were, had babies.” (Levine, 1992, p. 327)

CHAPTER 1: INTRODUCTION

A bird’s-eye view of the Demographic Transition 
Eighty years ago, the first formulations of what would come to be known as Demographic Transition 
Theory were concerned with the future as much as with the past (Davis, 1945; Notestein, 1945). It is 
telling that Notestein’s article providing a long view of global population trends appeared in a book 
titled Food for the World and its central motivation was to provide population projections and 
inform policies for economic recovery and development in the post-war decades. Notestein 
predicted that the global population would reach 3 billion by the year 2000. In fact, the world 
population would reach that point by 1960 and double again by the end of the millennium, without 
the Malthusian consequences for living standards and mortality anticipated by Notestein and Davis 
(United Nations, 2024). 

Historical demographers are still grappling with the causes and consequences of the First Demographic Transition. Coupled with improvements in infant mortality and adult life expectancy, the Demographic Transition represented a profound and irreversible shift in the contours of human life. Scholars like Notestein and Davis and later Ansley Coale (1973), observing contemporary population dynamics in the second half of the twentieth century, turned to history for an explanation, setting off massive data collection projects to describe these transitions for as many societies as possible and to determine when mortality and fertility began to decline, and from what levels. In the process, demographic theories have often reproduced assumptions about the role of individual agency in responding to structural change, viewing populations as either hyper-rational economic actors or passive, fatalist subjects. The historical agency of women in particular has been neglected, in part due to the biases and limitations of many historical sources (Mackinnon, 1995). The recent, growing accessibility of digitized historical datasets has shifted attention from the nation or region to the individual and community, allowing analysis of not just where but who started the trend of deliberate family limitation.

Though mistaken in the speed and scale of future population growth, the writings of Notestein and Davis were hugely influential in their articulation of several stylized facts regarding long-term demographic change. The basic argument, on which many others built, was that the economic and social modernization associated with the Industrial Revolution led to unprecedented population growth in Europe, primarily through the reduction of mortality. After a lag, fertility also

declined in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries because the new individualist, urban societies made large families disadvantageous, and numerous births were no longer necessary to counteract the drag of infant and child mortality. The populations of Europe, it was asserted in the mid twentieth century, had reached the end of their demographic transition and achieved a ‘low pressure’ homeostasis (Friedlander, 1969). As economic development advanced in non-European countries, both a period of population growth and an ultimate fertility decline would be inevitable.

The empirical basis for early work on the Demographic Transition was limited, consisting of the few long-run series of birth and death rates which existed for select countries in Northwestern Europe and of associations between inter-war demographic rates and levels of economic development. The lack of more substantial historical data was not a problem for the prevailing methodology of ‘reading history sideways’ which assumed that historical levels of fertility and mortality among post-transition populations could be inferred from the levels observed in contemporaneous pre-transition populations (Thornton, 2001). Countries whose family systems and demographic patterns were less like Western Europe were taken to represent lower rungs on the developmental ladder. This perspective aided family planning and development programs which hoped to speed up the global transition towards European-style modernism with minimal fuss.

The Princeton European Fertility Project was the first large-scale attempt to test the mechanisms of Demographic Transition Theory by collecting fertility, mortality, and socioeconomic data at the province-level for multiple historical populations (Coale & Watkins, 1986). The indexes of fertility used by the project were developed to identify the period at which a substantial proportion of the population were deliberately limiting their family size by preventing additional pregnancies after a desired final birth. This type of parity-dependent fertility control, also referred to as ‘stopping’ behaviour, described the tendency of couples to begin practising birth control only after reaching their intended final birth, and was considered to be the defining feature of the fertility transition. In contrast, pre-transitional populations were defined by ‘natural fertility’ which was not under deliberate control as regards total family size, but could still vary substantially between populations according to ages at marriage and the intervals between births (Friedlander et al., 1999). Even deliberately lengthened birth intervals, described as ‘spacing’ behaviour or parity-independent fertility control, were considered compatible with the existence of natural fertility, because such behaviour was not altered according to the number of children born (Henry, 1961).

Instead of identifying the necessary thresholds of economic indicators which triggered the onset of historical fertility decline, the European Fertility Project discovered that there was no consistent relationship between a country’s socioeconomic development and the beginning of its demographic transition. Marital fertility fell from around 1870 in almost all of their study areas, even

in places where mortality remained relatively high (Coale & Watkins, 1986). At the same time, the estimation of additional demographic measures of nuptiality revealed new dimensions of fertility and population growth. The even longer-term series constructed from parish registers by Wrigley and Schofield showed that much of the population growth in England after the Industrial Revolution was driven by an increase in fertility rather than a decrease in mortality, and in particular by a decrease in average ages at first marriage (Wrigley & Schofield, 1981).

On the other hand, the Princeton data revealed alternative religious and linguistic geographic groupings, in which populations with shared identities followed similar demographic patterns despite experiencing different socioeconomic contexts (Lesthaeghe & Surkyn, 1988). Having made the negative case for a unitary, modernisation view of demographic transition, the project contributed to a growing emphasis on cultural factors which affected the adoption of new fertility behaviours. This perspective was reflected in the interpretation of social class differences in marital fertility revealed by the 1911 Census of England and Wales. The ‘professional classes’ appeared to be exceptional in their early fertility decline, compared with the persistence of large families among the working classes and particularly among coal miners (Haines, 1989; Stevenson, 1920). Both contemporary comment and historical studies suggested that the process of national fertility decline in Britain was defined by an innovation of new behaviour among the middle and upper classes, related to Victorian family values and aspirations to new standards of living, which gradually diffused down the social class hierarchy (Banks, 1954).

How and why did fertility decline? In the wake of the results of the European Fertility Project, revisions to Demographic Transition Theory resulted in a broad contrast between innovation-diffusion models on one side and socioeconomic adjustment models on the other (Carlsson, 1966). These schools of thought do not necessarily imply that cultural vs economic explanations of fertility decline are contradictory but rather place different emphasis on these factors as necessary or sufficient conditions for demographic change. The ‘New Home Economics’ model developed by Becker and the ‘Easterlin synthesis’ of supply and demand for children were examples of adjustment models which incorporated more nuanced formulations of economic decision-making (Guinnane, 2011). Caldwell’s use of a life-cycle perspective regarding the flow of resources from parent to child and back again also illustrates an attempt to synthesise economic and demographic theories (Caldwell, 2005).

In Becker’s model, fertility decline involved a trade-off between the quantity and quality of children, child ‘quality’ being primarily conceived in economic terms regarding returns to investment in education and human capital (Becker, 1960, 1993). Easterlin’s framework of the supply and demand for children acknowledged that cultural contexts could influence consumption tastes,

desired family sizes, and the costs of contraception, but ultimately emphasized the same forces of modernisation (mortality decline, urbanisation) as in classic Demographic Transition Theory, operating through different pathways (Easterlin, 1975; Easterlin & Crimmins, 1985). One of the assumptions of these microeconomic models is that the high levels of fertility seen in the past and in contemporary societies reflect high demand for children, stemming from both cultural and economic incentives for large families. This assumption is undercut by evidence, both from quantitative analysis and qualitative surveys, regarding the uncertain economic benefits of children (Cleland & Wilson, 1987). It is difficult to prove that, even in the context of high levels of child labour, children in pre-transitional societies were a net positive addition to their families, compared with the costs of their consumption and the opportunity costs of their parents’ time. Even in a modern context of widespread access to contraception, surveys of desired family size can reveal high levels of unwanted fertility which are not inevitably implemented into family limitation (Bongaarts, 2025).

A useful framework for the historical fertility transition which balances ideational and contextual factors was articulated in Coale’s three preconditions for fertility decline (1973). For fertility to decline, couples must see reduced fertility as advantageous; fertility must be ‘within the calculus of conscious choice,’ meaning that reproduction is something that couples believe is within their control; and finally, couples must have knowledge of or access to effective contraception. Already a succinct formulation of these processes, Coale’s preconditions have been further summarised as requiring that couples be ‘Ready, Willing, and Able’ to limit their fertility (Lesthaeghe & Vanderhoeft, 2001). The differences between competing theories of fertility decline can then be seen in terms of which of the three preconditions they emphasize as the precipitating factor.

There was no substantial change in contraceptive technology before the onset of the historical fertility transition. Traditional methods of birth control (including abstinence, withdrawal, and abortion) were widely known before the transition and remained prevalent well into the twentieth century; thus, ability to regulate fertility is often assumed to have been relatively constant and sufficient for fertility decline to occur (Fisher & Szreter, 2003; Santow, 1995). Economic adaptation theories assume that couples were also sufficiently willing to adjust their fertility to maximize their utility and simply lacked ‘readiness’ until economic circumstances changed and the advantages of smaller families became apparent (Easterlin, 1986). Diffusionist theories suppose some change in ability or perhaps knowledge of effective birth control, such as through the publication of contraceptive pamphlets, but particularly emphasise changes in the willingness of couples to accept family limitation as culturally acceptable behaviour and in cultural constructions of ideal family size (Beach & Hanlon, 2023; David & Sanderson, 1987). For sustained, deliberate fertility

change to take place, all three preconditions must be met, with the last one to be obtained acting as a ‘bottleneck’ on the speed of transition (Lesthaeghe & Vanderhoeft, 2001).

Although the European Fertility Project emphasized the simultaneity of fertility declines across the continent, studies of both exceptional places, such as the early fertility decline in France (Wrigley, 1985), and exceptional groups, such as elite and upper-class couples (Livi-Bacci, 1986), have contributed to an alternative theory of multiple fertility declines, operating concurrently or independently according to the configurations of readiness, willingness, and ability which characterized a time, place, or population (Garrett et al., 2001; Greenhalgh, 1990; Szreter, 1996). Since it is couples, not regions, that have babies (Levine, 1992), describing the patterns and causes of these multiple transitions requires disaggregated data. An advantage of the ready, willing, and able framework is its dynamic inclusion of cultural, social, and economic factors of demographic change; the challenge of implementing research within this framework is the availability of data to measure all three preconditions at once (Hacker, 2016).

Instead, many studies have focused on demonstrating when and how one of the preconditions for fertility decline was met, to prove either that it was the missing piece of the puzzle which triggered the transition or that it was a long-standing characteristic in a population which lacked one or both other preconditions (Brée & de la Croix, 2019; Dribe & Scalone, 2010; Szreter & Garrett, 2000). One area where this has manifested is in critiques of the concept of ‘natural fertility’ in pre-transitional demographic regimes and its implications for either willingness or ability to control reproduction (Friedlander & Okun, 1995). Natural fertility is easily misinterpreted as representing peak human reproductive potential, partly because of its association with the Coale Trussel indexes of fertility used in the European Fertility Project which took the high birth rates experienced by the Hutterite religious community as a natural fertility standard (Coale & Treadway, 1986).

Very few European populations reached this standard even before the fertility transition due to both higher ages at marriage and longer intervals between births (Wilson et al., 1988). However, behaviours which are not clearly intended to reduce fertility or related to the number of children already born – for example, delayed marriage, extended breastfeeding to preserve infant health, or cultural taboos against post-partum sexual intercourse – are compatible with natural fertility because they do not imply that fertility is within the ‘calculus of conscious choice’ in the same way as when couples demonstrate a concrete preference for smaller families by altering their sexual behaviour to prevent the second, third, or fourth birth. The concept of natural fertility is deliberately conservative, because it aims to identify the least ambiguous signs of deliberate changes to fertility within marriage, in the absence of evidence for the motivations behind other sources of fertility

variation. New data and methods allow these assumptions to be relaxed as researchers can better assess patterns of birth spacing as well as stopping before, during, and after the fertility transition.

Within theoretical frameworks favouring ideational change as the bottleneck condition of fertility decline, the contrast between natural fertility and parity-dependent family limitation implies a radical cultural shift towards a new ‘numeracy in children’ in late-nineteenth-century society (van de Walle, 1992). The case for discontinuity draws on evidence from modern surveys of desired family size in which some women in countries with high fertility refuse to name a specific number of hypothetical children, appearing to adopt a fatalistic acceptance of however many children come along. Against this view are studies that emphasize continuity before and after the fertility transition by demonstrating the deliberate spacing or postponement of births, for example in response to crises like harvest failures, as well as long-run evidence of contraceptive knowledge and practices which imply a historic willingness to control fertility (Bengtsson & Dribe, 2006; Friedlander & Okun, 1996; Santow, 1995). Other studies provide examples of fertility transitions, particularly outside Europe, which were achieved through substantially increased birth intervals (Anderton & Bean, 1985; Timæus & Moultrie, 2020).

A revised characterization of the historical fertility transition therefore emphasizes the intensification of contraceptive efforts within marriage and an increasing (but not necessarily new) parity-dependent pattern. A revisionist view also cautions against ascribing motives to historical behaviour based on ‘modern’ rationality (Johnson‐Hanks, 2002); in the context of uncertain household fortunes, contraceptive methods with high failure rates, and high infant mortality, couples may not have started married life with a clear plan of how many children they would have and may have adjusted both fertility desires and intentions over the years (Seccombe, 1990; Timæus & Moultrie, 2008). The contraceptive confidence hypothesis argues that longer birth intervals are part of a rational strategy to reduce a woman’s time at risk of an unwanted pregnancy, whereas more reliable birth control methods allow women to cluster births closer together and be reasonably certain of avoiding further pregnancies before reaching menopause (Ní Bhrolcháin, 1988).

Gender at work and in marriage Although often depicted as competing, ideational and adjustment theories of fertility decline are not necessarily mutually exclusive, nor do they represent a strictly chronological development within demographic research (see for example Dribe & Scalone, 2014). Theories about historical fertility change have also always been informed by observations from contemporary population trends. Continuing demographic changes in high-income countries in the last several decades, including below-replacement-level national fertility rates and changing patterns of marriage and cohabitation,

have been dubbed a ‘Second Demographic Transition’ (Lesthaeghe, 2020). The mission of theory has become to explain why people do not have more children rather than focusing on how they were first persuaded to have fewer. In this context, an emerging theme – long present in the literature but gaining more theoretical attention – concerns the importance of gender.

Women’s labour force participation has long been recognized as an important determinant of fertility levels (Crafts, 1989; Garrett, 1990; Moore et al., 2021; Tilly et al., 1976). During the First Demographic Transition, marital fertility was generally lower in areas where substantial numbers of married women were in the industrial workforce. The relative costs of childbearing are higher for working women because they face a choice between the opportunity costs of disruptions to their earnings or the direct costs of obtaining alternate childcare. While this economic mechanism may be the primary link between women’s work and fertility, historical demographers have also suggested that women who worked outside the home were more exposed to new ideas about family size and knowledge about birth control (Gittins, 1982; McDonald & Moyle, 2018). On the other hand, married women’s work in some contexts in late-nineteenth-century Europe was associated with higher levels of marital fertility, especially when demand for child labour was also high (Dupree, 1995; Janssens, 1998).

Despite significant cross-sectional associations between married women’s labour force participation and marital fertility, the onset of fertility decline in England and Wales was accompanied by an unprecedented shift of married women out of the labour force, in contrast to the situation after the post-Second World War baby boom (Hewitt, 1958; Tilly & Scott, 1978). Married women’s work continued to be significant only in certain defined industries such as textiles, and the wives of textile workers reported the lowest marital fertility of the working classes in 1911 (Stevenson, 1920). In the wake of the Second Demographic Transition, scholars have proposed a U shaped relationship between women’s work and fertility over time, reflecting qualitative changes in the relationship between these factors (Goldin, 2025; Goldscheider et al., 2015). Reconciling these changing relationships requires careful attention to the culturally embedded meanings of gender.

In settings where mothers are likely to work outside the home, but gender norms mean that childcare and household tasks are unevenly distributed between partners, mothers face a ‘dual burden’ of paid and unpaid labour which contributes to lower fertility desires (McDonald, 2000; Raybould & Sear, 2021). Couples who distribute household labour more equitably may be more likely to desire and achieve larger families. The converse of this cooperative model of fertility is one which emphasizes unequal power dynamics within marriage under patriarchal systems and the bargaining processes which occur within the family economy (Folbre, 1983; Seccombe, 1990). Working-class women seeking contraceptive advice at the turn of the twentieth century in England

sometimes expressed the disconnect between their own desires to limit the burdens of pregnancy and childcare and their husbands’ unwillingness to give up their ‘married pleasure’ (Davey, 1988).

The potential for conflict between spouses concerning family limitation is an important focus for feminist critiques of demographic theory and research (Folbre, 2016; Janssens, 2007a). While one of the criticisms of the European Fertility Project was the theoretical and empirical distance between populations and couples, an ongoing problem has been how to observe processes inside the household, where individuals contribute to and make decisions within the family unit (Mackinnon, 1995). Data which do not effectively differentiate between husbands and wives may perpetuate assumptions about homogeneity within households and the position of men as decision makers and heads of household (Janssens, 2007b; Testa et al., 2014; Thomson et al., 1990; Watkins, 1993). Since the costs and benefits of childbearing are different for women and for men, why should we assume that men and women had identical attitudes and desires during the fertility transition?

Oral histories and surveys offer some insight into sexuality and contraception in the past, but do not reach back to the onset of the fertility transition. The sensitive subject matter can also produce conflicting results; for example, the 1946 Family Census in Great Britain found that women who married in the 1910s reported an average of 2.8 total births, but the majority of this cohort did not report using any form of birth control (Langford, 1991). One interpretation is that these early patterns of family limitation were achieved largely through abstinence or abortion (Cook, 2005). On the other hand, oral histories from working-class couples who married in the inter-war period emphasize withdrawal as the preferred method of contraception (Fisher & Szreter, 2003). Although questions about the prevalence of different types of birth control are unlikely to be settled with the available evidence, this debate is important in part because of the gendered construction of methods: abortion and abstinence are classified as ‘female’ methods through which women attempted to control their own fertility, whereas withdrawal and the use of condoms are male behaviours (Davey, 1988).

Qualitative evidence from twentieth-century Britain suggests that there were class differences in contraceptive behaviour which reflected not only increasing access to modern appliance methods of birth control among the middle classes but also differences in the relations between spouses regarding communication and knowledge about birth control. Fisher and Szreter’s collection of oral histories and Davey’s analysis of correspondence with prominent birth control advocate Marie Stopes both suggest that middle-class couples were more likely to take joint responsibility for contraception and discuss plans for using either male or female birth control methods (Davey, 1988; Szreter & Fisher, 2010). The sources disagree, however, about which spouse was more likely to take initiative on family limitation among the working classes.

Although both analyses emphasize the use of withdrawal among couples of all classes, the impression gleaned from Fisher and Szreter’s oral histories, as well as those collected from an iron mining community by Williamson (2000), is that working-class women were more ignorant about sex and likely to leave family planning to their husbands. The working-class women writing to Marie Stopes similarly cited an ignorance of the ‘secrets’ of birth control but revealed their own initiative in family planning both by seeking this expert advice and by more often mentioning abstinence as their previous method of contraception. Working-class women were also integrated in folk traditions of medicine which made it easier for them to seek abortions (Moore, 2013). If there was a lag between women’s desires for limited fertility and the onset of fertility decline, the missing factor could be either lack of motivation among men to reduce fertility or lack of agency among women to negotiate with their husbands (McDonald & Moyle, 2018; Seccombe, 1995). A woman who had her own earnings or otherwise had greater control over the household budget might have more power, both financial and symbolic, in the resolving of such conflicts (Agarwal, 1997; Bourke, 1994; Lundberg & Pollak, 1994).

Another assumption of traditional demographic transition theory was that industrialisation led to a breakdown of traditional family systems of household production, extended kinship, and paternal authority (Thornton, 2001). The historical evidence undercuts these assumptions on two counts: first, Hajnal’s work on the Northwestern European Marriage pattern proved that nuclear households were dominant in this region since at least the early modern period, and secondly, detailed studies of households and occupations during the nineteenth century have argued that the family economy persisted through the transition to the factory and became the family wage economy (Hajnal, 1965; Tilly, 1979). Social norms emphasizing familial obligations meant that children contributed their earnings to the household budget and parents could exercise considerable control over their offsprings’ transitions between education, work, and marriage (Alter, 1988; Hareven, 1975).

The elusive mechanisms of fertility decisions thus have to be nested within increasing layers of organisation, from the couple and family to the local community to the regional or national picture – what Greenhalgh calls ‘whole demographies’ (Greenhalgh, 1995). The desire for individual level data to regain the specificity lost by aggregation should not go too far the other way and divorce individuals from their contexts. Another model of fertility outcomes which tries to capture the dynamic nature of the decision-making process is the Traits-Desires-Intentions-Behaviours (TDIB) Framework proposed by Miller and colleagues (Miller, 1994; Miller et al., 2004). The TDIB framework is useful both because it acknowledges that individuals may experience conflicting realities – such as

a gap between fertility desires and intentions (Badolato, 2025) – and also because it illustrates how external factors can influence different stages of the process.

The first step in this framework is the formation of fertility desires, influenced by both innate traits (drawing on evolutionary demography) and social and cultural values. Desires for children are then translated into fertility intentions in the context of perceived constraints, including household budgets or access to contraception. Finally, intentions to conceive or prevent conception are translated into behaviours which may require the cooperation of a partner. Although developed in the context of the Second Demographic Transition, the TDIB framework has strong echoes of the Ready, Willing, and Able model of fertility decline. Coale’s three preconditions could be reframed as the processes which translate traits into desires (readiness), desires into intentions (willingness), and intentions into behaviours (ability). By encompassing both static, measurable characteristics and the flexible relationships between them, this framework illustrates how singular factors – like a desire for fewer children – do not inevitably lead to singular outcomes.

The gap between theory and evidence 
The feedback loop between the formulation of theory, the collection of evidence, and the 
reformulation of theory is often disrupted. One of the defining challenges for the field of historical 
demography is lack of data. We cannot ask a couple on the street in 1870, ‘how many children would 
you like to have?’ and instead must indirectly infer motivations from observed trends. We often rely 
on statistical series which were created for different, historically specific purposes, such as parish 
registers or decennial censuses. On the other hand, we have an advantage in knowing what 
happened to fertility in the long run, both in terms of changes over time and outcomes within the 
life course. If we ask a survey respondent about their intention to have children in 2025, we may 
have to wait twenty years to find out what happened. 

Data availability aside, the gap between theory and evidence is also methodological, as researchers attempt to operationalise theoretical frameworks into testable hypotheses. This challenge may take the form of variable selection and definition, as research in both modern and historical settings faces the problem of summarizing complexity in an informative way. If we are interested in the association between social class and family size, we first have to agree on how to define class categories. This is often done using coded versions of occupational descriptors, organized into a broad hierarchy of ‘skill.’ One could argue that a more accurate measurement of social class would reflect the combined effects of income, occupation, residence, education, and family background, but it is a rare data source which captures all these variables for every individual. A classification system which works in the cross-section may break down over time, as the population composition within classes changes. On the one hand, shared disciplinary norms for

variable definitions and analytical approaches can ensure that results from different studies are directly comparable; on the other hand, these standards will not be appropriate for every dataset and may, with repeated or uncritical use, end up obscuring more than they reveal.

Detailed local case studies are often the most successful in illuminating the missing links between the proposed causes of demographic change and their effects, in part because they are able to implement a blend of quantitative and qualitative methodologies (Alter, 1988; Hareven, 1975; Parr, 2004). The Schneiders’ study of rural Sicily is often cited for its clear description of distinct fertility declines operating among the nobility, the artisan classes, and the peasantry (Schneider & Schneider, 1984). These methods and results cannot easily be translated to other contexts, not least because they involved oral histories from individuals who are now long deceased. In addition, both the limited survival of archival sources and the effort it takes to collect them mean that micro-historical demographic studies often have restricted temporal ranges. The need to balance levels of geographic and temporal scale is another challenge for demographic analysis. Much has been gained through comparison of results from different case studies, but the quantitative and qualitative differences between settings make overarching synthesis difficult.

The theories and evidence described above emphasize how demographic processes are embedded in time – both historical time and individual time (Hareven, 1977). Given the distinction between ‘stopping’ and ‘spacing’ behaviours during the Demographic Transition, a crucial question is not just whether, but when couples had their next child. To effectively analyse marriage and fertility timelines, covariates of interest must also be time-stamped; for example, it would be misleading to measure the impact of education on fertility using observations of terminal degrees which may have been obtained after the onset of childbearing (Hoem & Kreyenfeld, 2006). The best data for assessing childbearing patterns at the micro-level are longitudinal, consisting of multiple observations for the same individual over the course of their lifetime. Longitudinal population microdata have become a gold standard for demographic research, and both their creation and their interpretation bring new challenges.

Motivation of this study My contribution to this literature is rooted in a desire to bring new data to old questions. Studies of fertility decline in England and Wales have relied heavily on the 1911 Census, first in the form of statistical summaries and abstracts from the Registrar General’s Report, and later in the form of individual-level samples (Crafts, 1989; Garrett et al., 2001; Haines, 1989; Szreter, 1996). This is because the census’s survey of marital fertility is the only source for this period which records the fertility experienced by individual couples over their reproductive lives rather than the total births

reported in an individual year. The release and digitisation of the full-count census data after a hundred year embargo was a huge step forward for historical demographic research, but access to individual-level data has not completely erased the biases and limitations of these sources (Schürer & Higgs, 2020a). In particular, the 1911 census recorded the cumulative childbearing experiences of couples over their whole marriages but measured their places of residence, occupations, and household structures at a single point in time, often long after couples were at risk of having their children. This data limitation is both a source of measurement error and an obstacle for analysing fertility in a life course framework which integrates these changes in occupation, migration, or household structure into event history models.

In this thesis, I have enhanced the census data through record linking, and recent developments in data and methods mean that this can be done on a larger scale than ever before. Having identified the same individuals in records spanning several decades, I am able to analyse marriage and fertility patterns in a life course perspective which emphasizes the relative timing of transitions in the individual and familial life cycle and the accumulation of transitions across the life course (Hareven, 1978). The challenge for my research has been to achieve this level of observation for both women and men. When a woman took her husband’s surname at marriage, her life course was broken into two sections which could be observed separately but not together. Marriage certificates, which would represent the missing links between these identities, are not available to researchers on a national scale. Instead, I have used indexes to the original marriage registers to identify married women’s maiden names.

My study begins with the 1881 census and ends in 1911. It focuses exclusively on data from the county of Derbyshire, in the East Midlands of England. My motivations for choosing a geographically limited setting were threefold: first, I wanted to limit the logistical challenge of developing a record linking methodology incorporating marriage indexes; secondly, I wanted to have the benefits of both larger scale and microhistorical perspectives on fertility decline; and finally, I wanted to augment the linked census data further with other primary sources which would have to be manually collected and transcribed. Derbyshire did not have the same population growth or concentration of manufacturing as the areas around Manchester to the north, but the county grew from a population of under 400,000 in 1881 to almost 550,000 in 1911. It provides an opportunity for a comparative study of marriage and fertility among mining communities, textile manufacturing towns, rural parishes, and a few urban centres. There are important ways in which this setting differs quantitatively and qualitatively from other contexts, including the scale of urbanisation and movement of migrants within and without the county, but the population of Derbyshire reflects

many of the important demographic, socioeconomic, and cultural changes occurring on a national (and international) scale in this period.

The presence of textile manufacturing in the county was a crucial criterion, given the potential importance of women’s labour force participation in general, and textile factories in particular, during the fertility transition (Atkinson, 2012; Garrett, 1990; Hewitt, 1958; Ittmann, 1992). Record linking between censuses and marriage indexes results in reconstructed occupational histories for women both before and after marriage, at ten-year intervals. The linked census data provide valuable insight into the lives of women employed as domestic servants or dressmakers and of men working as coal miners, agricultural labourers, or white-collar professionals, which is more informative than the static picture presented by a single census. To obtain more granular information about women’s work and the context of textile manufacturing, I collected material from the archive of the Strutt cotton spinning mills in Belper which spans the same period as the census data (W. G. and J. Strutt and Co., 1751). These sources reveal the ages at which women and men began work in the mills, tasks performed, wages received, and family relationships between mill hands.

These data are bounded temporally by the missing 1871 census on one side (which has not been fully digitised by the commercial partner for the Integrated Census Microdata Project) and the only recently released 1921 census on the other. I thus begin in medias res, since I am not able to directly observe marriages and births from before the Demographic Transition or the earliest years of fertility decline in the 1870s. Nevertheless, the 1911 Census provides information about the fertility of earlier marriages, and my linked data follow several cohorts of women from early adulthood to the end of their childbearing careers. By overcoming data restrictions and methodological obstacles, this project is an important movement towards viewing women in historical demography as individuals with both a past and a future. In the rest of this introduction, I narrow focus from the Demographic Transition as a global phenomenon to the specific paths of fertility decline in England and Wales, in the textile industry, and in Derbyshire.

The fertility transition in England and Wales The crude birth rate in England and Wales (live births per thousand people) peaked in the 1810s, plateaued after 1830, and began its steep decline in 1877 (Wrigley & Schofield, 1981). This statistic paints a very sketchy picture of the fertility transition, since changes in the crude birth rate may result from a combination of changes in the population age structure, marital fertility, nonmarital fertility, age at marriage, proportions married, mortality, and migration. Nonmarital fertility was also in decline in the second half of the nineteenth century, and average ages at first marriage rose

slightly from around 25.5 for women and 26.1 for men in 1851 to around 26.2 and 27.6 in 1911, respectively (Schürer et al., 2018). Variations in nuptiality and illegitimacy in response to economic cycles are associated with pre-industrial demographic regimes (Adair, 1996), whereas the Demographic Transition was associated with new changes to fertility within marriage which were the greatest driver of total fertility decline. For a clearer picture of marital fertility, scholars turn to the 1911 census. In a section on the census schedule headed ‘Particulars as to Marriage,’ married women were asked to state the duration of their current marriage, the total number of children born alive to that marriage, the number of children still alive, and the number of children who had died.

These data reveal that the average number of children born per married woman fell from 7.5 for women who married aged 20-24 in the 1850s to 5.1 children born to women who married at the same ages in the 1880s (Woods, 2000). Using the occupations which men reported in the same census, the Registrar General’s Report categorized couples into a social class hierarchy, starting with the upper and middle classes in Class I and ending with unskilled labourers in Class V. In the same marriage cohorts, the wives of upper- and middle-class men experienced a decline in total marital fertility from 6.7 to 3.8 births per woman, while the wives of unskilled workers decreased their marital fertility from 7.8 to 5.8 births. These patterns were taken by T.H.C. Stevenson, Superintendent of Statistics in the General Register Office, as evidence for the innovation of low fertility behaviour among the middle and upper classes and gradual diffusion down the class ladder (Stevenson, 1920).

Historical demographers have long recognized these data as flawed in several ways: a husband’s observed occupation in 1911 is just one slice of a potentially varied occupational trajectory up to that point and may be very different from his occupation in the early years of marriage if the couple had been married for some time. Perhaps more importantly, the census provides little to no insight into the occupations and backgrounds of wives, leading to a fixation on husbands as being the dominant forces behind fertility change. In addition, the social and occupational classes devised for the Registrar General’s report were motivated by the a priori assumption of an orderly social gradient of fertility, leading to the separation of three large, anomalous occupational groups – miners, textile workers, and agricultural labourers – from the rest of the working classes (Szreter, 1996).

While, in general, children per couple increased further down the social hierarchy, the residual occupational categories in the Registrar General’s scheme illustrated the diversity of experience within the working classes. Miners displayed unusually high fertility given their relatively high wages and social status (up to 8.2 births per woman in the early marriage cohorts), and male

textile workers had the smallest families of the working classes. These groups have often been taken as opposite ends of a spectrum, one end illustrating the dominance of the male breadwinning family model in mining families and the other reflecting high levels of married women’s labour force participation in textile factories (Friedlander, 1973; Garrett, 1990). Stevenson interpreted the low fertility of male textile workers (4.5 births per woman in the latest marriage cohort) as being almost entirely the result of their wives’ employment (Stevenson, 1920).

Garrett’s study using a limited sample of linked census records from the textile town of Keighley provides an important illustration of why assessing the relationship between women’s work and fertility in a cross section is misleading: the married women in her sample who worked in the worsted industry appeared to have lower net fertility, but were also likely to leave textile employment after their first or second birth. Fertile women were thus selected out of the sample of working women, while those remaining included a mix of women who were only recently married, were actively limiting their fertility, or had lost more children to childhood diseases (see also Garrett, 1998; Garrett & Reid, 1994). Crafts (1989) similarly argued that an ‘excess of childlessness’ and lower fertility for marriages of short duration in areas with higher women’s labour force participation reflected substantial spacing of first and second births among working women. Greater birth spacing early in marriage while women were working could be an important component of low fertility among textile couples, but is it enough to explain the whole difference between them and the rest of the working classes?

Without longitudinal data, it has been difficult (if not impossible) to satisfactorily assess theories linking women’s work and fertility by establishing relationships within the individual life course as opposed to identifying cross-sectional correlations which are vulnerable to omitted variable bias. Discussion of the relationship between women’s work and fertility has generally been restricted to a few visible industries, including both the cotton and woollen textile industries and the employment of married women in the Staffordshire potteries where working patterns allowed them to maintain relatively higher fertility (Dupree, 1995). Changes to factory legislation and mechanisation also affected the character of the textile workforce, in terms of gender, age, and marital status, across the nineteenth century (Hewitt, 1958). In a wider context, women’s work in the nineteenth century was highly contingent on life-cycle factors: the majority of women worked before marriage, withdrew from the workforce during their peak childbearing years, and held their labour ‘in reserve’ to support their families as needed through casual or seasonal work and by taking in lodgers (Tilly & Scott, 1978).

Different explanations have been put forth for the gradients in marital fertility observed in the rest of Stevenson’s class hierarchy. Banks (1954, 1981) provided a detailed social history of

marriage and fertility among the middle and upper classes which emphasized parents’ changing attitudes towards the education of their children for professional careers and potential social mobility which would be hampered by large families. This was a version of the ‘quality-quantity’ trade-off, but one which was embedded in cultural as well as economic conceptions of ‘quality.’ Pooley (2013) also used qualitative sources to study changing norms for parent-child relationships which required both more money and more time spent on offspring, raising the ‘perceived relative costs of childrearing’ among elite society (Szreter, 1996).

Class or occupation also interacted with environmental factors to affect fertility, both via disparities in the risks of infant and child mortality and via the composition of local social networks. Garrett et al. (2001) used both the 1911 census and samples from earlier censuses to estimate age specific and total marital fertility rates by both class and district. Their analysis recognized that there were important compositional differences in the geographic distribution of social classes, most notably among the three occupational classes in the Registrar General’s scheme – textile workers, miners, and agricultural labourers. In addition, these data showed that other social classes and occupational groups living in the same environments behaved similarly to their neighbours. Industrial, agricultural, and white-collar environments were defined by different occupational structures but also by different social norms, often stemming from the majority socio-occupational group.

This conclusion echoes Szreter’s (1996) critique of the Registrar General’s social class system as an explanatory framework for fertility variation, given the large amount of variability within social classes. Couples were, in Szreter’s analysis, embedded in ‘communication communities’ which could be locally determined or could span geographic boundaries through commercial, social, or kinship networks, including through the circulation of books and newspapers. These networks determined the social norms of class (e.g. Banks, 1981) as well as knowledge and attitudes regarding the acceptability of birth control. Szreter offered modified support for Banks’ theory of social diffusion by highlighting the low fertility of working-class occupational groups which had the greatest social contact with the middle and upper classes, particularly male domestic servants; however, he more forcefully argued that the childbearing patterns of different groups were affected by distinct social and economic forces, creating multiple fertility transitions.

Using the full-count census data, Jaadla et al. (2020) found widening social class differences in recent net fertility from 1881 onwards, as measured by the number of children under five observed in the household. Their analysis also found that migrants (both those moving within Great Britain and from abroad) generally had lower net fertility than non-migrants – except for non migrant wives of textile workers. Since recent net fertility at the time of the census could be affected

by duration of marriage, infant mortality, or birth spacing, this study suggested (but could not directly prove) that migrants had lower fertility because they had postponed marriage and childbearing, while couples who had children at younger ages were less likely to migrate later. A general consensus has emerged that the Demographic Transition in England and Wales involved substantial spacing of births as well as increased birth stopping (perhaps in different combinations according to different contexts), but most of these observations have been based on indirect estimates and aggregate measurements, such as age-specific marital fertility rates, rather than analysis of individual birth intervals (Garrett et al., 2001; Woods, 2000).

Age-specific fertility rates are the basis for the Coale-Trussel indices of fertility M (the level of ‘natural fertility’ in a population, affected by average birth intervals) and m (the deviation of age specific fertility curves from the Hutterite standard) (Coale & Trussell, 1974). Although these measures have been used to indirectly measure spacing and stopping respectively, they can be affected by changes in childbearing which make this distinction less clear. Rather than resulting in a convex pattern of age-specific fertility, increased stopping among younger women at lower parities can create flatter fertility curves; meanwhile, failed attempts to stop at higher parities can result in longer birth intervals (Ewbank, 1989). The reconstructed data on individual mothers and births which I describe below allow me to re-examine the role which these patterns of both spacing and stopping played in the fertility transition.

Research questions 
My initial research questions responded to two gaps in the literature on fertility decline in England 
and Wales and were informed by developments in historical demographic research in other 
European and North American contexts. Firstly, I wanted to address the role of married women’s 
work, and women’s labour force participation more generally, in shaping marriage and fertility 
patterns in the late nineteenth century. The collection of factory workforce records allow me to 
explore women’s work in the textile industry specifically, but the linked census data also provide 
insight into other female occupations, including the most common employment for single women, 
domestic service. These data required a more complete break from the Registrar General’s social 
class scheme of fertility than has been implemented in previous studies because that framework is 
not transferrable to the study of female occupations. The strength of this approach is that my 
classification of both female and male occupational groups will be data-driven and specific to the 
contexts of late nineteenth- and early twentieth-century Derbyshire; the limitation is that the results 
will not be directly comparable to those of other studies. On the other hand, this approach also 
reveals the power of compositional differences within broad categories which have wider 
implications for studies using standardised classification schemes. 

Secondly, and stemming directly from these questions about the impact of women’s employment on fertility, I wanted to assess the relative roles of stopping, spacing, and starting (via age at marriage) during the fertility transition in Derbyshire. My initial hypotheses based on the results of previous studies were that women employed in the textile industry would be older at marriage and have longer intervals to their first and second births. However, the longer-term trajectories of childbearing among textile workers, as well as among women with other occupational histories, are not governed by such clear expectations, since it has not previously been possible to explore these patterns in the available data. An important methodological question of my research was the extent to which the linked census data and 1911 fertility survey could be used to successfully reconstruct complete birth histories in order to analyse birth spacing and stopping directly.

With these data, my research approach is in line with the general trend of historical demography in Europe which has benefitted from the creation of national and regional databases of longitudinal population microdata (Mandemakers, 2025). Some countries – notably Sweden, Belgium, and the Netherlands – have longitudinal data from population registers which contain a level of detail about both demographic events and individual migration that can probably never be attained through census record linkage. Researchers using these data have addressed the role of birth spacing and compared social class gradients in different communities during their fertility transitions (Dribe et al., 2017) and have applied and adapted new event history methods (Alter, 2019; Alter & Oris, 2025; Bras & Schumacher, 2019). My data and methods are in direct conversation with these emerging literatures and provide new perspectives from a different historical setting.

The Belper factory data and a micro-history of the surrounding community was initially intended to be the central focus of the thesis, with contextual analysis of data from the rest of the county. The balance has instead been flipped, for both positive and practical reasons. Firstly, the linked census data and reconstructed birth histories have proved enormously rich sources for research on occupational differences in marriage and fertility, and there are many possibilities for future work on occupations, marriage, and childbearing. In addition, the factory records proved to be both too dense to collect in their entirety and too patchy to enable the construction of complete employment histories for more than a few years. By focusing mostly on the linked census data, I have been able to compare female textile workers and textile manufacturing districts to other occupations and environments and use the results of this broader analysis to inform a more refined set of questions which I address using the sampled factory records.

The contributions of this research are first exploratory: who or what type of couple was controlling their fertility in late nineteenth-century Derbyshire? When did the fertility transition

begin in these communities? How did couples choose to restrict the number of births – by spacing, stopping, or a combination of birth control strategies? The descriptive results regarding fertility patterns by age, parity, and occupation are important because they have not previously been visible with available data for Britain. In a more limited capacity, this project also seeks to contribute to explanatory frameworks – why did some couples have small families and others have large ones? Why did women’s work have the impact it did on fertility? What was the bottleneck condition of the fertility transition – readiness, willingness, or ability? These questions are much harder to answer, involving a degree of inference about individual agency in the absence of more qualitative material, but are important for interpreting the significance of the quantitative results.

In Chapters 2, 3, and 4, I introduce the census data, define occupational categories, and describe the process of building my longitudinal dataset and reconstructing complete birth histories. Chapter 5 analyses levels and trends of marital fertility in Derbyshire in a life course framework by using sequence analysis to identify childbearing typologies, encompassing starting, spacing, and stopping effects. Chapter 6 uses a split-population cure model to analyse the factors associated with individual parity transitions, including the transition from marriage to the first birth. I also apply this model to study the timing of marriage and likelihood of remaining single in Chapter 7. Finally, Chapter 8 describes the workforce of the Strutt cotton spinning mills in Belper, synthesising and comparing the information available from the census and from the factory archives, and explores the impact of child labour and family employment on marriage and childbearing patterns. I summarise the results and discuss their contribution in the context of theoretical frameworks in Chapter 9.

CHAPTER 2: THE INTEGRATED CENSUS MICRODATA (I-CEM)

Coverage and variables 
The first individual-level census in England and Wales was taken in 1841. From 1851 onwards, the 
census schedules collected more complete information about individuals and households, including 
relationship to the head of household, marital status, age, occupation, and place of birth (Higgs et 
al., 2021). The surviving household schedules and census enumerators’ books held by The National 
Archives are kept under embargo for a hundred years after their creation to protect the privacy of 
individuals. When I started this project, the 1921 census had recently been released to the public but 
had not yet been fully transcribed and processed for the census microdata collection. The 1921 
census also does not include the marriage and fertility questionnaire conducted in 1911, which 
makes that census a natural end point for my research. 

In addition, the 1871 census was only partially transcribed and so was not included in the original partnership to enrich and standardise the available census data for academic research purposes. The Integrated Census Microdata (I-CeM) series now provides researchers access to anonymised data from 1851 to 1861 and 1881 to 1921 (Schürer & Higgs, 2020a). For this project, I use the special license version of the data which includes names and addresses, extracting all records from the Registration County of Derbyshire in the 1881, 1891, 1901, and 1911 censuses (Schürer & Higgs, 2020b). While the population of the county grew, its composition was shifting over this period of demographic transition; age pyramids show evidence of the fertility decline taking place between 1881 and 1911 (Figure 2.1).

The I-CeM documentation provides detailed information about coverage and variable definitions (Higgs et al., 2021); here I will review some of the main variables which were used in the construction and analysis of my dataset, where there are potential errors or biases related to these variables, and where I have derived new or edited variables. The I-CeM data contain both the original string fields as transcribed from the census enumerators’ books or household schedules and coded or standardised versions of these variables. In most cases, the coded variables are preferable because they have been checked for errors (such as a ‘wife’ whose marital status is ‘single’) and verbose strings have been collapsed into consistent categories.

My research particularly relies on the I-CeM enriched household identifiers and standardised relationships within the household, both during the record linking process to identify family groups in adjacent censuses and during analysis to add parent, spouse, or offspring characteristics to individual records. These variables include HID (household identifier), PID (person identifier within household), SPOUSE (PID location of married couple in household, if present), FATHER (PID location

of father in household, if present), and MOTHER (PID location of mother in household, if present). For other household members, relationship to head of household may include ‘servant’, ‘lodger’, ‘grandchild’, etc. As mentioned above, the 1911 census includes the additional variables MARYEAR (year of marriage, estimated based on the duration of marriage, YEARSMAR), CHILDTOT (children ever born alive), CHILDALIVE (children surviving), and CHILDDEAD. I used a version of the 1911 fertility file which has been further cleaned and standardised by members of the Cambridge Group for the History of Population and Social Structure.

Even with extensive processing to correct mistakes in enumeration or transcription, the technical accuracy of the census data can vary widely between individuals. Variables which are subject to measurement error include age, since respondents were asked to record their age at last birthday rather than exact birth dates. An individual’s precise year of birth is thus expected to be within a two-year window, depending on the timing of census night relative to their birthday. In addition, census data often reveal age heaping, or the tendency of innumerate or illiterate respondents to round their ages to a number ending in five or zero, and other reporting errors made by heads of household recording information for other household members (Hwang & Squires, 2024; Lee & Lam, 1983).

Source: I-CeM; Derbyshire Registration County.

Figure 2.1 
Age pyramids by sex and year, Derbyshire 1881-1911 

Random errors are also introduced in the enumeration and transcription process, including misspellings or illegible handwriting. For the censuses before 1911, the I-CeM data are based on the census enumerators’ books, which were transcriptions made from the original schedules filled out by household heads, as these are the only records which survive. Although the sex of individuals should be a more precise and consistent variable, the way that this information was recorded in the original census schedules could lead to mistakes in enumeration or transcription. Instead of directly reporting gender, the age field was divided into two columns for males and females. In the I-CeM data this variable has been compared with first name and relation to household head to correct for errors. Other errors may be more systemic – for example, long-distance migrants were less likely to record their precise parishes of birth than those who were still living close to where they were born.

Derived variables

Occupational groups 
Occupations present a particular problem for researchers using census data. Responses to the 
question about an individual’s regular employment could vary in detail from a complete description 
of the workplace (‘confectioner master emp. 5 men, 2 boys, 6 waitresses’) to the vague titles of 
‘labourer’ or ‘factory hand’ with no indication of industry or position. Grouping these occupational 
titles by industry, field, or skill is a herculean task with no clear solution. The I-CeM data include 
several occupational coding schemes, including the internationally comparable HISCO codes, the 
Hollerith codes used to tabulate the 1911 census, and the OCCODE matrix developed specifically for 
I-CeM. OCCODE reduces many millions of unique strings into 797 standard occupational labels which 
are consistent across this period. These codes can be used to reconstruct the larger occupational 
categories used in the Registrar General’s reports, or to create a bespoke occupational typology for a 
particular research question (Schürer, 2024). 

Since my analysis focuses on women’s work, a new occupational typology was needed to highlight patterns of female employment in addition to testing existing theories regarding male occupational groups. I began with several occupational categories which derived both from the Registrar General’s report and from other groups of interest, including textile workers (both male and female), agricultural labourers, coal miners, white-collar or professional occupations, and domestic servants. I adopted a wide definition of ‘professional’ occupations to include any OCCODE category which I judged to reflect a combination of education, authority, or lack of manual labour (so, for example, the overlooker on the factory floor would not be included in the white-collar category, but the factory bookkeeper and clerk would be). This approach allowed me to highlight some female occupational categories – including governesses, teachers, and nurses – which were

associated with women from less working-class backgrounds or could represent the opportunity for an independent career. A full list of the OCCODE labels and final categories for both men and women can be found in Table A.1.

The initial occupational groups left large numbers of women and men in the residual ‘other’ category. By reviewing common OCCODEs and the original character string occupational descriptions from among this group, I identified several other occupations which were frequent enough to deserve their own categories – for women these groups were dressmakers and other milliners, and for men these groups were transport workers (primarily related to the railway), shopkeepers and other small-scale commercial occupations, and iron and metal workers, whom I combined with coal miners to form a ‘heavy industry’ group. In addition, since it is difficult to determine whether men describing themselves as ‘labourers’ were agricultural labourers or other unskilled workers, I combined them with the farming occupations to create an agriculture-labouring group.

In this process, I also discovered cases where the assigned OCCODEs were clearly wrong – either because the original strings were confused for another occupational description or for no clear reason (for example, domestic servants coded as OCCODE 30, clergymen of the United Free Church). There were also cases in which individuals reported an occupation which had not been coded by the I-CeM team. Having identified common codes and strings associated with these errors, I recoded selected occupations using a few string filtering conditions to keep the original OCCODE for those cases where it was accurate and to replace it with a general code for one of my other occupational categories if it was not (a list of recoded occupations is in Table A.2). As far as possible, I separated residential or day domestic servants from farm servants and other types of agricultural labour, but this depends on the level of detail with which occupations were originally described.

Registration subdistricts Census geographies are another challenge for the researcher. The Registrar General’s reports on each census and on births, deaths, and marriages in the intercensal periods presented data organised by registration county, district, and subdistrict. The boundaries of these units changed over time, some more than others; for example, the Registration District of Derby was split into two subdistricts of Derby and Osmaston in 1911. The I-CeM data also allocates individuals to parishes, which can vary widely in size and population – the largest parish in Derbyshire in 1881 was St. Werburgh in the city of Derby, with 25,927 inhabitants, and the smallest was the parish of Nether Haddon in rural Bakewell, with a population of 11. In my analyses of marriage and fertility, I rely primarily on the Registration Subdistricts (RSDs) to define contextual variables for each census year related to local occupational structure, population density, and sex ratios.

For this environmental typology, I follow the definitions used by Reid et al. (2018) to classify the RSDs in each census into eight groups: agriculture, semi-rural, professional, semi-professional, mining, textile, transport, and other urban (the conditions for each type are specified in Table A.3). To simplify comparisons, I combine the agriculture and semi-rural districts into one rural group and combine the professional, semi-professional, transport, and other urban districts into one urban group. These groups do not imply that textile production only occurred in the textile districts, or that there were no market towns in rural districts; I use the environmental typology to summarise the aggregate occupational structures of a district which contextualises the occupation of an individual residing in that area, whether they were part of the majority group or of a more varied population. RSDs may change types from census-to-census due to changing population composition. In most analyses below, I use the RSD type at an individual’s first observation in the linked census data.

I-CeM also contains several different birthplace variables since the census schedules asked for parish or town as well as county of birth and this was subject to different interpretations by individual householders and enumerators. The original place of birth strings from the transcribed census schedules have been preserved and the I-CeM enrichment process has attempted to assign these place names to standardised parishes of birth. Standardised locations may not always be accurate or available for every census entry and this imprecision may increase with the distance between place of birth and place of enumeration (Day, 2022). Instead, a more consistent record of birthplace is an individual’s standardised county of birth, but as a less focused measure it also produces more fuzzy estimates of lifetime migration and has less discriminatory power for record linking, as described below.

Other data cleaning 
In order to facilitate census-to-census record linking, I use the reported ages in each census to 
calculate implied years of birth (rather than adding or subtracting ten years of age from one census 
in each pair). I also process names using a cleaning algorithm provided as part of the ABE census 
linking replication package (Abramitzky et al., 2020). This algorithm separates first and middle names 
(where this has not already been done in I-CeM), reformats all names in consistent lowercase and 
removes special characters (such as hyphens or apostrophes), and uses lookup tables to replace 
common nicknames and abbreviations (Geo. for George, Liz for Elizabeth). Some linking methods use 
extensive name coding to account for differences in spelling or nicknames, but this results in less 
precise grouping of individuals with similar sounding names, which can contribute to linking errors 
(Ruggles et al., 2018; Vick & Huynh, 2011). The name cleaning process is not perfect and cannot 
account for all possible variations in spelling; however, the record linking methodology is designed to 
address these kinds of discrepancies. 

Derbyshire: context and composition 
The county of Derbyshire is sandwiched between two areas which were famous for their textile 
industries in the nineteenth century – the cotton manufacturing centres of Lancashire and Cheshire 
to the north and the hosiery and lace industries of Nottingham and Leicester to the southeast. These 
textile areas are highlighted in dark green in Figure 2.2. In fact, the first water-powered cotton 
spinning mills in the world were built in Derbyshire in the late eighteenth century. Early industrialists 
including Richard Arkwright, patentee of the water spinning frame, took advantage of the natural 
power of the Derwent River and reservoirs of labour from the wives and children of local miners, 
farmers, and nail makers (Fitton & Wadsworth, 1958). The development of steam engines for 
powering factory machinery in the first decades of the nineteenth century meant that the industry 
was no longer limited by the geography of rivers, and the better-connected commercial centres in 
Lancashire and Yorkshire soon outpaced the Derbyshire industry. Despite its smaller relative size, 
textile manufacturing remained a significant presence in the Derbyshire landscape for the next 150 
years. 
Source: I-CeM and Reid et al. (2018). 
Note: Registration Subdistrict classifications described in Table A.3. 
Figure 2.2 
Map of Registration Subdistricts in England and Wales by type, with inset showing the county of 
Derbyshire, 1881 

Occupational structure 
The cotton spinning mills in Belper were established in the late eighteenth century by Jedediah 
Strutt, a sometime hosiery manufacturer and early partner of Richard Arkwright (Fitton & 
Wadsworth, 1958). The Strutt factory complex, growing to five mills in Belper and a bleaching and 
dyeing operation in nearby Milford by the early nineteenth century, remained in operation well into
