the twentieth, helped by a merger with other small firms in 1897 to form the English Sewing Cotton 
Company. The surviving archive of W. G. & J. Strutt and Co. (renamed when Jedediah’s sons took 
over the business) is held by the Derbyshire Record Office in Matlock and includes several series of 
employment records for the period covered by the sampled census data. The content and analysis of 
the factory records are described in detail in Chapter 8. Combined, the textile manufacturers in 
Derbyshire employed 21% of adult single women over the age of fifteen in the 1881 census (Figure 
2.3). 

The eastern districts of the county sat on the southern end of the Yorkshire coalfield. There was also substantial employment of men in iron foundries and lead mines. My ‘industry’ occupational category represents 30% of adult men in the 1881 census. In contrast to its significant employment of young single women, the textile industry only accounted for 7% of adult men’s employment. The sex ratio of employment in other textile areas of England and Wales could be very different, such as in the spinning districts of Lancashire where up to half of adult men were so employed (Morgan, 2013). This suggests that the types of textiles made or technologies used by the mills in Derbyshire either created less demand for adult male labour or had been adapted to different labour supplies due to competition from other local industries. The historic mills along the Derwent River likely differed from those in the growing districts of Hayfield and Glossop where the county bordered Lancashire, which really formed part of the greater Manchester textile area. A substantial proportion of men in the county were involved in agriculture (a fifth of adults in 1881) while professions including doctors, lawyers, and clerks made up about 6% of adult male employment.

The largest single source of employment for single women in Derbyshire was domestic service. Around 30% of adult single women in the 1881 census were enumerated as domestic servants. Nationally, 36.6% of adult single women in England and Wales, or 48% of working single women, were in service. In Derbyshire, the proportion in service dropped to 23% of single women in 1911, partly as a result of a slight decline in overall recorded labour force participation rates: 72% of single women in Derbyshire returned an occupation in the 1911 census, compared with 77% of women in the 1881 census. These patterns are almost identical to national rates of single women’s labour force participation. However, employment in domestic service also shrank in relative terms,

and domestic servants as a share of working single women in Derbyshire declined from 40% in 1881 to 32% in 1911. The share of working women employed in the textile industry remained more stable across the period at around 25%. Between 6% and 8% of all women in Derbyshire (or 10% of working women) were dressmakers, and similar proportions were employed in white-collar and professional roles.

About 20% of children in the county between ten and fifteen years old were recorded with an occupation in the 1881 census (excluding those reporting an occupation as ‘scholar’ or ‘student’). The textile industry was the largest employer of children in the county, accounting for almost 30% of children who were working half- or full-time in 1881. Child labour in the textile industry will be explored further in Chapter 8. Even in childhood, working patterns were gendered: up to 40% of working girls were in service and another 40% were in textiles. A fifth of working boys were in the textile industry, another fifth in agriculture, but the largest occupational group for boys, even at this

Source: I-CeM; Derbyshire Registration County. 
Note: For women, ‘other’ includes agriculture and labourers, industry, shops, transport, and other 
occupations not listed here; for men, ‘other’ includes dressmaking, service, and other occupations not 
listed here. Occupational categories based on OCCODE as described in Table A.1. ‘aglab’ = farmers, 
agricultural labourers, and general labourers. 

Figure 2.3 Occupational distribution in Derbyshire by age, sex, marital status, and year

age, was coal mining and other heavy industries (27% in 1881). Despite changing child labour regulations in 1901 which raised the minimum age for half-time workers from eleven to twelve, the proportion of all children aged 10-15 reporting an occupation was not much lower in the 1911 census. The apparent persistence of child labour could be as much a product of underreporting of occupations among younger children in earlier censuses as of changes in the age structure of child workers in later censuses.

A notable shift in employment patterns in Derbyshire, and in England and Wales as a whole, over this period is the decline in the share of married women returning an occupation in the census. In 1881, 15% of married women in Derbyshire were working, and a third of working wives were in the textile industry (nationally, these proportions were 14% and 25%, respectively). A quarter of all female textile workers in this census were married. In 1911, only 9% of married women in Derbyshire were enumerated with an occupation. Married women who were working were still more likely to be in the textile industry than other occupations, but only 15% of female textile workers were married. Widowed women were more likely than married women to report occupations in every census, but their labour force participation rates also seem to have been in decline, falling from 70% in 1881 to around 50% in 1911. An important question is to what extent these patterns reflect a true change in the economic activities of married and widowed women, and to what extent they reflect changing enumeration practices.

After the 1881 census, there was a national decline in the number of women enumerated as ‘occupation’s wife’, that is, engaged in the same work as their husbands (You, 2020). More generally, the instructions on the census schedules said that household heads should record women’s occupations if they were ‘regularly employed from home’ (Higgs, 1987). The census data provide limited insight into casual, seasonal, or part-time work for this reason, and thus may be especially biased as a measurement of married women’s labour, since mothers and wives would be the most likely to do these types of work which could be more easily combined with childbearing and domestic labour (Burnette, 2025; Hatton & Bailey, 2001). This pattern could also be exacerbated by the attitudes of household heads and enumerators who undervalued women’s work, as has been suggested by comparison of census data with wage records (McGeevor, 2014; Miller, 1984). On the other hand, women who worked in the textile industry were highly visible, and their occupations are largely recorded accurately in the census (Lown, 1990; Wilkinson, 2010).

The declining share of married and widowed women working in the textile industry in Derbyshire between 1881 and 1911 suggests that this trend was not driven entirely by enumeration practices. As discussed later, the textile workforce was contracting and shifting towards younger, single women. The overall decline in widowed women’s labour force participation is intriguing, and

explaining this phenomenon would require more detailed exploration of patterns of work by age, kin proximity, and the number of co-resident working children. The important insights from this aggregate, cross-sectional analysis are that women’s work was highly contingent on life-cycle dynamics, and either demand or supply (or both) of married women’s labour were shifting in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries (Horrell & Humphries, 1995). Measuring the true extent of these shifts depends on longitudinal data and additional sources of information on employment and wages, as will be shown.

Environment and migration In 1881, 42% of the population of Derbyshire were enumerated in rural registration subdistricts, 23% were in urban or semi-urban areas, 16% in textile districts, and 19% in mining districts. In 1911, the share of the population living in urban subdistricts had grown to 40%. These changes were partly a result of population movement between districts and partly the result of growth and change within districts. Only two RSDs were classified in 1881 in the ‘urban’ category of the environmental typology (Buxton, a ‘professional’ subdistrict, and the city of Derby); in 1911, three other districts had become urban or semi-urban – Belper, Chesterfield, and Osmaston (a new district within Derby). Although the Strutt mills in Belper were operational throughout this period, Belper shifted from being classified as a textile district in 1881 to be ‘other urban’ in 1891 and ‘semi-professional’ in 1911. This is partly because the environmental typology defined by Reid et al. (2018) is based on the occupational structure of all working people between the ages of 15 and 64. The workforce of the Belper mills was largely female: almost 45% of working women in Belper were still employed in the textile industry in 1911, but textiles employed only 6% of working adult men in the district.

Sixty percent of the inhabitants of Belper reported having been born in the parish, and 80% were born in Derbyshire. Overall, lifetime migration rates in the county were somewhat higher: 35% of inhabitants in 1881 were born in another county, and 44% in 1911. The next most common birthplaces were Nottinghamshire (5.6%) and Staffordshire (4.6%). Defining migrants in this way is somewhat arbitrary, given that some may have moved just a few miles over the county border and others from entirely different parts of the country. Migration within the county can be explored using linked census data, and migrants into the county can be identified, but migration out of the county will be unobserved in the selected sample. Out of all individuals in England and Wales who were enumerated with a birthplace in Derbyshire in 1881, 58.6% were living in their county of birth. Outmigrants were mostly found in neighbouring counties: 18.9% of Derbyshire-born people were enumerated in either the West Riding, Nottinghamshire, or Lancashire in 1881.

This brief exploratory analysis of the Derbyshire census data illustrates both continuity and change at the turn of the twentieth century. The dominant occupations in the county in every census

between 1881 and 1911 were domestic service and textiles for women and heavy industry and agriculture for men. The most visible changes in this period are the shift in population distribution from rural to urban districts and the decline in married women’s labour force participation. These patterns reflect similar trends at the national level. On the other hand, the 1911 census reveals a more fundamental change occurring in this period in the number of children born to marriages in the 1880s and 1890s. Figure 2.4 illustrates the number of children ever born to couples who married between the ages of 20 and 34, as reported in the 1911 fertility survey by husband’s occupational group; marriages of shorter duration would not have reached their final parity, but differences between occupational groups in the rate of childbearing appear to have emerged within a few years of marriage. Although not following precisely the same occupational classifications, these patterns conform with the Registrar General’s findings for the national census returns, as the wives of miners and others in heavy industry reported the highest fertility, and wives of professional men reported the lowest.

Couples in which the husbands worked in the textile industry also show a steep decline in the number of children ever born between those who married in the 1870s and those who married

who married aged 20-34.

around 1890. As we have seen, the textile industry in Derbyshire was more important for its employment of women than of men. To demonstrate whether this fertility decline was driven by the occupations of these men’s wives (if men in textiles were likely to marry women in the same industry) or their own occupations, longitudinal data are necessary to reconstruct both male and female occupational histories. The following chapters describe how these data were constructed.

CHAPTER 3: LINKING INDIVIDUALS BETWEEN CENSUSES AND MARRIAGES

Introduction1

Until recently, women have been largely absent in large-scale historical record linking projects. This absence has been not so much an oversight as a methodological challenge. In times and places where women traditionally change surnames upon marriage, it is usually impossible to track these changes through a single data source like the decennial census. Not only will there be an upper limit to the number of women who can be linked between any two censuses, but the unknown outcomes of marriages and remarriages might also undermine confidence in the veracity of these links. Following women over extended periods must involve additional civil registration sources, which have not been digitized and made publicly available to the same extent as national census data. The difficult process of linking women to their marriage records may also introduce new sources of error or bias. To address these challenges, I developed an adapted methodology for linking both women and men in England by combining census records with indexes to the marriage registers.

Complete civil registration records for this period are not available in the UK, although some studies have made use of limited samples in combination with other data sources (Akgün et al., 2020; Arthi et al., 2022; Newman & Smith, 2023; Reid et al., 2002). Indexes of births, deaths, and marriages which list events by district and quarter of the year have been transcribed and made publicly available but do not contain the level of detail found in the full certificates. Crucially, the marriage indexes do not list bride and groom together but rather connect individuals with pages in the original registers, and women are listed only by their maiden names. Each page in the register usually contains two marriages, so the linking process must allocate spouses to the correct partners. Combining indexes of marriage with direct census-to-census links improves match rates for women in their twenties by up to twenty percentage points. I begin by comparing the performance of deterministic, probabilistic, and household-based methods between the 1881 and 1891 censuses, before describing the process of linking couples to records of their marriage, and finally assessing the bias and accuracy of my results.

Approaches to automated census linking The central challenge of many historical record linking projects concerns the trade-off between the number and the accuracy of links, especially when scaling up from small, manually linked samples to automated linking of entire populations. A growing number of studies of different record linking

1 A version of this chapter was published as Diduch, E. (2024). The record linking glass ceiling. Applying automated methods to the census and women’s marriage records, 1881–1911. Historical Life Course Studies, 14, 126–143. https://doi.org/10.51964/hlcs19189.

approaches concentrate on these trade-offs to optimize the balance of sensitivity and specificity in linked data. Maximizing the number of links (i.e. avoiding missed links, or false negatives) comes with the risk of including more erroneous links, or false positives. Several recent articles have compared the performance of three record linking approaches: rule-based or deterministic linking, probabilistic linking, and machine learning algorithms.

These comparisons consistently find that probabilistic linking offers a good balance between match rates and accuracy without requiring large, hand-linked training datasets which are both costly to produce and less replicable for other researchers (Abramitzky et al., 2021; Bailey, Cole, Henderson, et al., 2020; Massey, 2017; Wen et al., 2022). In addition, other census linking projects have moved towards a household-based approach to incorporate additional linking variables based on the people with whom an individual is living at each census date (Antonie et al., 2020; Fu et al., 2014; Helgertz et al., 2022). These approaches include links for women, but only in their roles as sisters, daughters, mothers, and wives.

Concerns regarding accuracy and representativeness underpin the choice of linking criteria to minimize sources of both error and bias in the linking process. At the most basic level, record linking methods choose time invariant traits to avoid overrepresenting individuals whose circumstances (such as address or occupation) are more stable over time or incorrectly linking individuals whose attributes have changed (Ruggles et al., 2018). In practice, historical records have many discrepancies in individual entries which must be accounted for in the linking process, ranging from misspelled names or imprecise recording of ages to the practice of women changing surnames at marriage (Hwang & Squires, 2024).

The functional distinctions between different automated linking methods are not only in their ability to identify possible links between records from two censuses, but also in their ability to discern between multiple competing links based on a limited number of linking variables. A genealogist or manual record linker often considers additional pieces of information such as address or occupation in choosing the right match for John Smith and Jane Doe, but on a systematic basis these decisions contribute to a biased linked sample where people who change jobs or move house (or simply record these details differently) are more likely to be lost from the historical record. When linking an entire population, therefore, researchers must rely primarily on variables that are expected to be constant over time and are consistently recorded in the source data – in historical censuses, these characteristics are usually name, sex, age, and birthplace.

A researcher’s tolerance for false negatives and false positives (often referred to as Type I and Type II errors) will vary according to the purpose of her data. Studies of socioeconomic or

geographic mobility over time often have a lower tolerance for false links which might skew estimates of mobility upward, but a higher tolerance for compositional bias in the sample which might be addressed by reweighting the population (Bailey, Cole, & Massey, 2020; Ruggles et al., 2018). Research using linked data for event history analysis may be more concerned with false negatives or missed links causing censored intervals in the reconstructed life course. A linking method which performs well for the population as a whole might have serious flaws for marginal populations or subgroups whose demographic and administrative patterns differ (Ó Gráda et al., 2024).

These concerns have contributed to the new wave of record linking projects which make use of additional individual and contextual variables to improve both the accuracy and coverage of linked samples. The strength of household-based linking methods is related to their ability to distinguish between multiple potential matches for one individual (Antonie et al., 2020). When performed iteratively, this process can have knock-on effects by removing high-confidence matches based on household contexts from the pool of potential matches for other individuals, thus increasing linking success across the population – except for women who have married in the intervening decade.

Many linking studies start by selecting census records for men, later including women grouped with their households. Since my methodology includes a separate step to link women to records of their marriage, in the first stage I aim to link women who did not marry between censuses. I group the full county census files by sex and by marital status for women. This prevents single women in one census from being compared to married women in the next census, and vice versa. Separately, married women in the first census are compared with women in the next census who are either married, widowed, or whose spouses are absent on census night. This approach prevents me from linking widows forward at this stage but reduces the risk of false or missed matches for married women who are widowed between censuses.

Each record linking approach begins by generating pairs of records from two census files. Even restricting the population to one county, the potential combinations of records between the 385,947 people in Derbyshire 1881 and the 432,128 in 1891 would be astronomical without limiting the universe of potential pairs. In addition to separating files by gender and by marital status for women, I do this by ‘blocking’ or grouping the population by birthplace so that pairs are only generated which exactly agree on county of birth. Pairs are assessed based on the linking variables to create some measure of similarity – whether a string comparison for names such as a Jaro Winkler similarity score or the absolute difference between imputed birth years. Links are then accepted if they exceed a chosen threshold of similarity.

Comparing automated linking methods

Probabilistic vs deterministic methods Deterministic linking methods follow a relatively simple set of rules for comparing pairs to a threshold of similarity but often falter when multiple potential matches for one individual fall above the chosen threshold. Increasing the linking threshold (e.g. by requiring exact match on birth year or same spelling of name) does not solve this problem for individuals with common names and risks discarding true, unique matches which fall below the higher threshold. As a basis for comparison, I first link the Derbyshire data using an adapted version of the ABE deterministic method, with thresholds of 0.9 Jaro-Winkler string similarity scores for names and age differences of one year or less (Abramitzky et al., 2021). These thresholds are also consistent with the most common reporting errors found by Hwang and Squires (2024) when comparing census data with genealogical profiles.

After blocking on birthplace and comparing pairs of records from each census, I find that there is a significant attrition of potential links due to the need to discard duplicates. For example, combining 192,898 men in Derbyshire in 1881 with 215,172 men in 1891 results in 156,729,233 pairs of records with the same birthplace and initials. Out of these pairs, 167,358 pairs have names and ages similar enough to exceed the matching threshold, but most of these matches are not unique. Removing cases where a man in 1881 has been paired with multiple possible matches in 1891 (and vice versa) leaves 56,242 pairs, representing a match rate of 29.16% for the 1881 male cohort.

Probabilistic linking addresses this problem of multiple matching records, which are especially concentrated among people with common names, by estimating the probability that a pair of records is a match based on the characteristics of the total population (Abramitzky et al., 2020; Fellegi & Sunter, 1969). This allows the similarity threshold for a link to be based on the probability that two random individuals could share the same characteristics by chance. For example, there were 255 men named John Smith who were born in Derbyshire and still living in the county in 1881, but only one man named Walter Shakespeare. Probabilistic linking algorithms weight the similarity of linking variables by their prevalence in the population, so an exact match on name and age for John Smith might be given a lower probability of matching than a match for Walter ‘Shakspere’ with a year’s difference from the expected age.

Probabilistic methods mimic the decisions made by human record linkers when presented with a slate of possible matches to contextualize the best candidates, but obtain the same results every time given consistent inputs and thresholds (Bailey et al., 2023). Studies which have compared the results of automated linking methods to a ‘ground truth’ sample of manually linked or genealogical data find that probabilistic algorithms have lower false positive error rates, with

incorrect matches representing between 5% and 15% of men linked compared with up to 30% of deterministic links (Abramitzky et al., 2021; Bailey, Cole, Henderson, et al., 2020; Helgertz et al., 2022; Massey, 2017). Unlike manual linking or trained machine learning algorithms this process is fully automated and replicable.

The Reclin2 package for probabilistic record linking in R allows the user to specify the threshold of probability to select a pair of records as a link and to enforce one-to-one linking in the final linked sample (Van Der Laan, 2022). In the case of multiple potential links falling above a 50% probability of matching (because these probabilities are estimated independently), the Reclin2 package selects the highest probability link. For use with historical data here, I adapt the package with a custom comparison function to allow for age discrepancies between possible matches. After running several variants of this method, I find that the most efficient version of this method differs by gender: in addition to the variables used in deterministic linking above, match rates for men are better when middle initials are included among the linking variables, and for women when allowing for a wider range in implied year of birth.

Applying probabilistic linking between the 1881 and 1891 Derbyshire censuses and selecting unique pairs with a greater than 50% matching probability obtains 123,265 links, or 31.94% of the starting population in 1881. This threshold is less conservative than some purely probabilistic linking methods because these links are further assessed and validated in the following household linking stages. Probabilistic linking increases match rates for all groups, but the improvement is slightly smaller for men than for women (Table 3.1). This is consistent with the expected effects of probabilistic linking on the chances of matches for common names because the census data reveal a smaller name pool for men. Nearly 70% of the male population of Derbyshire in 1881 shared just ten first names; in contrast, women’s names are less concentrated with only 56% of women covered by the top ten first names. This illustrates why the inclusion of middle initials for men helps to clarify some of these links.

Match rates for women who were married in the first census are the highest in the probabilistic links, while match rates for single women lag far behind. This is in part because I allow married women to be linked to women in the second census who are coded as ‘married, spouse absent’ or are widowed, while single women can only be linked to women who are still single in the second census at this stage. The practice of the head of a household filling out a census schedule on behalf of their dependants means that women’s census records are expected to be subject to errors in enumeration as fathers, husbands, and employers may have misstated ages or misspelt names. Thus, even though women have a higher prevalence of middle names and middle initials in their census records, including this variable in the linking process does not improve match rates but, on

| | 44.25% | 33.15% | 43.10% | 39.33% |
| 1881 census population | 192,898 | 113,631 | 63,452 | 385,947 |

Source: I‐CeM; Derbyshire Registration County. Linking methods as described in the text.

the contrary, may decrease matching efficiency by ‘tightening’ the criteria too far and privileging poor quality matches with middle names over higher quality matches without (Goeken et al., 2011).

Some common surnames, including Smith, Taylor, and Walker, are better represented in the probabilistic links. Other surnames which appear to be less common or unique are less likely to be linked because they overlap with another common name, suggesting that they are misspellings or mistranscriptions of the same name (e.g. Sharp and Sharpe, Davies and Davis, or Higgenbotham, Higgenbottom, and Higginbottam, etc.). The same is true for first names because not all spelling differences and nicknames can be standardised and there are also significant overlaps between distinct names like Helen and Ellen or Abraham and Abram. These patterns suggest that the probabilistic method may result in fewer false positives by better assessing potential matches based on a comprehensive similarity score across these attributes rather than on discrete thresholds for each variable.

Linking based on households Several recent articles have expanded the selection of linking variables by including household characteristics or even neighbourhood contexts in scoring potential matches (Akgün et al., 2020; Fu et al., 2014; Helgertz et al., 2022; Wisselgren et al., 2014). This is an important movement towards diversifying the composition of linked datasets by including more women in the linking process but does not solve the problem of linking women across marriage. In addition, this approach advantages links for individuals who remained in the same household across censuses, while research that is interested in mobility, marriage, and family formation must include links to new households. Time varying characteristics like household composition are best used as part of an iterative linking process, in which high-confidence links for individuals who remain in the same household are

removed from the pool of potential matches for individuals who move, thus improving linking success across the population (Antonie et al., 2020).

For this project, I use probabilistic linking as a basis for further household linking. This approach combines the higher linking rates of household-based methods with the higher confidence links of probabilistic methods for those who changed households between censuses. Evaluating household characteristics provides a useful check on the accuracy of probabilistic links (Ó Gráda et al., 2024) and additional household links are expected to be highly accurate (Helgertz et al., 2022). Household contexts can be used to both rule in links that are otherwise uncertain (whether because of discrepancies in name and age or because of competing possible links) as well as rule out links which are logically improbable (including children with different parents and wives with different husbands).

In this second stage of record linking, households which have a member linked from the first probabilistic stage are used to limit the universe of potential matches for other unlinked household members. Blocking on household, individuals are compared using deterministic thresholds on name, age, and some additional conditions based on relationship to other household members. To prevent erroneous matches – for example between sisters and wives of the same first name – I require that linked household members must have the same mother, father, or spouse present or have the same relationship to the head of household (including grandchild, servant, boarder, etc) if a spouse or parent is not present in both censuses. In addition, I compare linked spouses to each other to avoid false links for married women – for example, if the link for Herbert Hodgkinson shows that he married for the first time between censuses, his wife should not be matched to a married woman in the previous census. Otherwise, similarity thresholds can be relaxed since a link between records with greater discrepancy in ages or lower name similarity is more likely if they are listed as a member of the same household in two census years.

Although there are further ways to enrich individual census records with household variables, including characteristics of siblings, children, or extended kin, this straightforward approach has the advantage of exploiting the existing household descriptors in the I-CeM dataset, including unique household IDs and indicators for spouses and parents within households. This process is primarily intended to consider matches for unlinked individuals, but I include all individuals who are part of a linked household, thus confirming first-stage links which also pass the household linking criteria. When links from the two stages are combined, if a potential household link disagrees with a probabilistic link for the same individual, I accept the household match as more likely. First-stage links which fail to pass household-linking thresholds (i.e. have been linked to individuals with different parents or spouses) are discarded.

The household linking stage also provides a rough estimate of the extent of false positive errors in the probabilistic linking process, although the true extent of error (both false links and missed links) is unknown. In total 10.3% of the probabilistic first stage links are discarded in the household stage – and the proportion discarded varies by subgroup from 8.2% of married women to

13.4% of single women. This error rate is probably a lower-bound estimate but is within the range found by comparison of other probabilistic linking methods to genealogical or hand-linked samples.

It is also important to note that ‘ground truth’ comparisons are themselves subject to error – human linkers often disagree with each other and where they disagree with algorithmic results there may not be a clear ‘winner’ (Abramitzky et al., 2021). Individuals for whom links can be confirmed using genealogical records are likely not representative of the total population.

Comparing examples of each type of link helps to illustrate this process. The probabilistic linking stage finds matches for each member of the Johnson family in four different households in the 1891 census, which would be incompatible with their continued family relationships (Table 3.2).

Focusing within the household of the two family members who are linked together finds better matches for all other members, in several cases with greater discrepancies in reported ages, illustrating why competing matches outside the household were initially scored higher. Making each ‘correct’ match also allows for further matches; for example, the new link to Alice M Johnson in

Swift Lane in 1891 means that the entry for Alice Johnson at Station Road is free to be linked to a

household at the same address in 1881.

The household linking process improves total match rates significantly, particularly for men and married women. For single women the improvement is more muted as there is an upper limit to the matches that can be made without additional information from marriage records (Table 3.1). These results support the inclusion of women in individual as well as household-based linking – the expected limit to direct census-to-census links for single women and low error rates in links for married women suggest that the algorithm is performing well for these groups compared with the results of studies which linked men only. This stage also doubles the number of complete households, in which all members in one census are linked to the next (8,508 distinct households, excluding single-person households). On average, 67.5% of individuals within each household in 1881 are linked to 1891. This is an underestimate of linking success among the linkable population since some household members will have died between censuses. Figure 3.1 compares the additional household links with those from the probabilistic and deterministic methods, showing that most of the new matches are unique to the household stage, not simply confirming links which could have been obtained deterministically.

To obtain the initial linked census dataset, I repeat the probabilistic and household linking stages with the remaining unlinked populations, under the assumption that the removal of competing matches during the first linking run may help to confirm additional links. For the second probabilistic run, the probabilities of matches are based on the characteristics of the entire population, as otherwise common names might be even further overrepresented in the unlinked group. Again, improvement in the match rates for this stage of linking is mostly among men and

Source: I-CeM; Derbyshire Registration County. Linking methods as described in the text.

Figure 3.1 
Venn diagram of links made by deterministic, probabilistic, and household linking methods, 1881 to 1891 

married women. In total, 151,798 links are obtained after this phase of census-to-census linking, representing 39.3% of the starting population in 1881 or 48.3% of the 1891 population over 10 years old. This represents a thirteen percentage point improvement over the baseline deterministic match rates.

Representativeness of the linked sample 
Although I developed this methodology using the 1881-91 census data, application to subsequent 
censuses for Derbyshire confirms its efficacy. Moving forward in time, linking success for all groups 
improve for a total match rate of 44% 1891-1901 and 45.4% 1901-11. These results suggest that the 
linking results are not an artifact of the characteristics of the 1881 and 1891 censuses and that the 
probabilistic and household linking steps behave consistently with improved data quality over time, 
despite differences in enumeration and transcription between censuses. 

The fact that this sample is geographically defined rather than randomly selected has implications for patterns of error which cannot be systematically evaluated without a source of ground truth. Other studies which link from random samples to full count censuses may reach different conclusions about the likelihood of false positive errors, given the larger pool of potential matches in the terminal census and the need to distinguish accurately between them (Bailey, Cole, Henderson, et al., 2020; Helgertz et al., 2022). Here, however, the restriction of linking within one county is likely to have a different effect by slightly reducing the potential pool of matches in the terminal census due to outmigration. There might thus be fewer false matches to be made, with migration affecting match rates more than accuracy.

The effects of migration as well as mortality between censuses can be estimated by looking at the terminal census for the ‘potentially linkable population’ who are native-born and over the age of ten (Long & Ferrie, 2013; Ruggles et al., 2018). For the whole of England, the native-born population born before 1881 was 16% smaller in the 1891 census. When focusing here on a county rather than a country, the linkable population can be affected by short-distance and return migration as well as by emigration. It is therefore useful to compare the Derbyshire-born population within and outside the county in each census. The equivalent decrease between 1881 and 1891 for the population born in Derbyshire and living anywhere in England was 7%, suggesting that this group was less likely to emigrate or experienced lower mortality, or both. However, in 1891 a greater proportion of the surviving Derbyshire-born cohort were living outside the county (increasing from 36% in 1881 to 50% in 1891).

Comparing the native-born population remaining in the county at each census, the maximum match rate for the linkable population is around 70%, assuming little to no return

migration between 1881 and 1891. This proportion is calculated from the difference between the populations born in Derbyshire before 1881 and still present at each census (247,294 vs 181,166), adjusted downward to account for under-enumeration and non-unique cases (about 6%) (for more details see Long & Ferrie, 2013; Wen et al., 2022). For single women in the county who are also ‘lost’ to marriage, the maximum direct match rate between censuses is around 40%. The initial census-to census links thus do not ‘overperform’ expectations.

Migration effects are reflected in lower match rates for parishes near the county borders where people may have easily moved outside the view of the selected data. Linking within the county prioritizes less-mobile links and might be helped by filtering the universe of potential matches or hurt by not considering likely candidates outside the county. The most common birthplace in each census is Derbyshire itself, with 64.1% of the population in 1881 reporting having been born in the county. This is followed by the neighbouring counties of Nottinghamshire,

Staffordshire, and Yorkshire, and match rates are up to twenty percentage points lower for individuals born outside the county (Table 3.3).

Other characteristics which are associated with mobility are also underrepresented in the linked sample. Unskilled workers have the lowest match rates, possibly related to migration in search of work and to lower levels of literacy contributing to inconsistent census entries. Both women and men who reported textile occupations in the census are slightly more likely to be linked than those in other broad occupational categories such as in agriculture or domestic service, possibly related to lower migration levels associated with their employment. These patterns are expected given the better linking performance for native born, middle- or upper-class populations found by

other studies (Helgertz et al., 2022; Massey, 2017). These biases, particularly regarding migration, will affect my analysis using these data.

In addition to migration, linking success is also affected by mortality and marriage. Figure 3.2 shows the age distribution of the female and male populations in Derbyshire in 1891 and the proportion of each age group which are linked to the 1901 census by household-based linking, by probabilistic linking, or which are unlinked. The higher proportion of each bar in green shows declining match rates in oldest age categories, illustrating the effects of mortality. Most links are made or confirmed in the household linking stage, but there is a higher proportion of men in their teens and twenties who are unlinked or only linked as individuals in the probabilistic stage, as it is these ages when the transition to a new household is most likely. Widows and widowers are much less likely to be linked as a function of both age and, for women, remarriage. Marriage also explains the large deficit in links in Figure 3.2 for women as they entered their twenties. Just 22% of women aged 15 to 25 at the 1891 census are linked directly to 1901, compared with 47% of men. For married men and women match rates are nearly identical, reinforced by spouses linked together within households (Table 3.4). Direct census-to-census linking is successful in linking women up to or after marriage, but the transition between states requires additional information.

Linking couples to marriages

Complete and detailed marriage records for England and Wales are not currently available on a large scale. Parish marriage records are more accessible but only include marriages in the Church of

England and are decreasingly useful after the introduction of Civil Registration in 1837 and in areas with many Nonconformist congregations, such as Methodists and Unitarians, including Derbyshire.

This makes the process of linking women to their marriages much more complicated but not

impossible.

Published indexes to nineteenth-century marriages have been scanned and transcribed by volunteers so that references for each marriage in the administrative county of Derbyshire can be selected for the period covered by the census data (Free UK Genealogy CIO, 2024). However, the content of the indexes is limited: date of marriage is given as quarter of the year, ages are not recorded, individuals are listed alphabetically instead of with spouses, and women are listed only under their own (maiden) names. Each marriage entry is indexed to a page in the original registry book, where there are usually two couples (four individuals) per page. There are also duplicate entries caused by transcription errors. Thus, linking brides to grooms requires knowledge of the correct pairing of couples, which can be determined by reference to a later census entry based on

the first and last names of the husband and the first name of his wife. Linking backwards from the census to the index in this way is successful because it is based on the appearance of two individuals together in multiple records; linking individuals separately from the index to the earlier census will be less certain because, unlike in the original registers to which the indexes refer, the names of the parents of the bride and groom are not recorded.

The index transcriptions do not include a gender field, which must be inferred from first name. It is possible to link to the index on name similarity alone, however not distinguishing between brides and grooms significantly increases the pool of potential matches and affects the choice of linking thresholds. For example, a name similarity threshold high enough to exclude false matches between Charles and Charlotte, with a Jaro-Winkler string similarity of 0.84, would exclude likely matches with differences in spelling or nicknames (e.g. Fanny and Fannie, J-W similarity 0.82). To address this problem, names in the marriage index are assigned a gender using the R package ‘gender’, which uses historical data on name frequency (Blevins & Mullen, 2015). This method successfully assigns a gender to 97% of names in the marriage index; the uncategorized entries (i.e. uncommon names, unusual spellings, or names which could belong to either gender) are included in both bride and groom pools.

Successful linking to the marriage index depends on defining the ‘linkable population’ of couples who married in the relevant decade. The most straightforward approach, given these data limitations, is to focus on marriages between bachelors and spinsters. This category represents the majority of marriages in any given year; for example, 83% of all marriages registered in 1881 in England were between a bachelor and a spinster, while in 12% of marriages one of the partners was either widow or widower and 5% of marriages were between widowers and widows (General Register Office, 1883). Without information about the civil status of partners from the original marriage registers, re-marriages are difficult to identify based on the census data, in particular because in the previous stages I excluded potential links for men which would imply that they had changed spouses in the intercensal decade. This is a conservative choice to exclude possible false positive links. By focusing on first marriages, I also ensure that children observed in later censuses or reported in 1911 belong to my couple of interest and not to other partners.

First marriages can be most easily identified in the census-to-census links based on a change in the marital status of the husband. Compared with the approximately 28,000 marriages registered in Derbyshire between 1881 and 1890 (General Register Office, 1895), just over 11,000 men are linked from ‘single’ to ‘married’ in these censuses. Another 998 single men are classified as ‘married, spouse absent’ in 1891 and 296 appear to have been married and widowed in the same decade,

according to the linked census entries. Without information about their absent spouses, it is difficult to confidently link these last two groups to the correct marriage records.

I expand the sample of potential grooms to include unlinked married men in the second census aged 35 and under, representing the cohort likely to have married for the first time within the previous ten years. This expanded cohort includes men who married anywhere between the ages of 15 and 35. This age range was chosen to centre on an average age at marriage around 25 years old. The wider net captures another 10,000 men, making up much of the deficit between linked and registered marriages. Two factors require caution when linking this expanded sample: there is no way to be sure that these men married in the same decade (except for a smaller number of younger married men) or that they married within the county. This is also a difficult life stage for record linking due to changing authorship of the original records; often a bride has shifted from a census entry filled out by her father to writing her own name in the marriage register to appearing in a post-marriage census entry filled out by her husband.

Linking to the indexes of marriage occurs in two phases. First, single women who are unlinked after the census-to-census stages are taken as potential brides and merged with possible entries in the index by name and district of marriage, under the assumption that women are most likely to marry near their homes. Index entries are then grouped with potential partners on the same page of the marriage register, and each of the possible pairings is compared to a newlywed couple in the next census (whether identified by census linking or by age cohort). These groupings of single woman, bride, groom, and couple are compared based on the husband’s first name and surname, the bride and wife’s first names, and the women’s ages in each census (Figure 3.3).

I require potential links to have first names with Jaro-Winkler similarities of at least 0.8, surnames with J-W similarities of at least 0.9, and for the estimated birthyears of a single woman in the first census and wife in the second census to be within three years. Competing potential links are selected based on name and age similarity and middle name agreement. If there is no unique best link based on these criteria, then all potential links for the individual are discarded. I discard potential links to marriages which imply ages at marriage under 15 years old. Experiments in manual linking suggested that discrepancies in brides’ and wives’ birthplaces contributed to low match rates so I exclude this variable in initial marriage index links in favour of focusing on matches by district of marriage. For example, if a wife had migrated as a child before meeting her future husband, he might assume she had been born in the county. After removing the first round of marriage links, the same process is repeated for unlinked newlyweds without the restriction on district of marriage. Instead, single women and wives are additionally compared on county of birth.

Of the couples who married between 1881 and 1891, 7,529 brides are linked across their marriages in the first stage and another 1,986 are linked in the second. This total includes a few links to marriages registered in neighbouring districts which are outside the registration county borders but within the administrative borders (e.g. Mansfield, Rotherham, Worksop). These links imply short distance migration either before or after marriage. The final successful links for women who married for the first time between 1881 and 1891 represent around 33% of the total marriages registered in Derbyshire in the same decade. In total, 43,313 couples are identified by census-to-census linking who married in the period between 1881 and 1911. With the expanded sample by age cohort, 81,039 potential brides and grooms are included in the index linking process for these three decades and 36,334 wives are successfully linked to their pre-marriage census entries, or 44.8% of the total sample. This match rate is probably an underestimate of the true linking efficiency for couples covered by the index of marriages given that the marriages in the index include some in which one or both partners had been previously married and widowed and, in addition, an unknown number of the potential grooms in the sample were married outside the county.

Quality of marriage index links Linking women via indexes of marriage significantly reduces the gender linking gap by improving total match rates for single women to over 40% (Table 3.4). This improvement is mostly concentrated among women in their twenties, increasing match rates by fifteen to twenty percentage points (Figure 3.4). The age profile of the total linked sample after linking via the

Figure 3.3 Example links to the Index of Marriages

Source: FreeBMD. 
Note: Names pseudonymized. 

marriage index is very similar for men and women, although match rates for women remain slightly lower overall. Marriage links are influenced by migration in two ways: first, a skewed sex ratio among the Derbyshire population may have caused local grooms to seek brides elsewhere, as 22% of grooms linked in the census were born outside the county compared with 39% of their wives. In addition, the expanded selection of potential grooms includes men who moved into the county between censuses, with up to 70% reporting birthplaces outside Derbyshire. Only those who married after migration can be found in the index and their wives found in the previous census.

By selecting men based on changed marital status or age cohort, the linked sample is designed to capture first marriages for single, never-married women. The average age gap between spouses is identical to the average for all couples in the census (husbands 1.5 years older than wives). In addition, that the dates of linked marriages closely follow trends in the dates of all marriage index entries for the period suggests the time gap between marriage and census does not influence the chances of making a link. The average estimated ages at marriage for linked couples marrying in Derbyshire between 1881 and 1891 are just under 23 for women and 24 for men (Table 3.5). These averages are lower than singulate mean ages at marriage (SMAMs) calculated based on the proportions single by age in the census. These population estimates for women and men in Derbyshire in 1891 are 25.5 and 26.7, respectively (Reid et al., 2018). Given the migration patterns
