discussed earlier, it is not surprising that these sources disagree. SMAMs are vulnerable to movements of single and married people which affect the age structure of the census population. In the linked sample, non-migrants are also likely to marry earlier than migrants.

The accuracy of marriage index links can be evaluated for 26,433 women who are linked to both the index of marriages and to the 1911 Census when they were asked to report the duration of their current marriage. This sample only includes women who did not move outside the county and were still married at the time of the census. Age at marriage in the linked sample is calculated from age at the nearest census and year of linked index entry, while marriage dates in the 1911 Census are based on retrospectively reported marriage durations. These ages are both approximate (due to the imprecision of marriage dates and birth dates) and subject to misreporting or mistranscription.

Average age at marriage for each of these three decades as implied by ages and marital durations reported in the 1911 Census are not as high as the SMAMs calculated from population distributions, but the index links still represent a slight underestimate (Table 3.5). Some of the women recorded in 1911 were in second marriages, which are not included in the linking process for single, never-married women. Comparing these estimates at the individual level, the vast majority of women reported marriage durations which agree with the estimated ages at marriage obtained by record linking: 96% of the estimates from both sources agree within three years and 98% within five

years (Figure 3.5).

The distribution of estimated ages at marriage from the 1911 Census confirms the decision to set the lower threshold for linked marriages at 15 years old (there are some implied ages at marriage for index entries which appear to fall below this age threshold because of slippage between age at marriage as calculated from the nearest census to the index and from age as reported in the 1911 Census). In a few instances, women who reported marriages from before 1881 are linked to index entries after 1881; these cases must be either false links, mistranscriptions, or misstatements, but they are also a useful reminder of the imprecision of these measures which depend on calculating age or date of marriage from age and duration reported in the census. There are also some census entries which imply impossibly young, even negative, ages at marriage (not shown here). The combination of links to the marriage index and subsequent census linking up to 1911 thus seems to be successful in reconstructing individual life courses, but the resulting linked sample does not reflect the full range of marriage experiences in the population.

Figure 3.5 Differences in reported and linked age at marriage, 1911 census

Source: As for Figure 3.2, married women observed in the 1911 census and linked to the Indexes of Marriage within the previous three decades. Note: Reference lines indicating minimum age linking threshold and age agreement +/- five years.

Full linked dataset 
Having completed separate linking processes on all census years 1881 to 1911, I can combine linked 
panels and follow individuals over more than one decade. To make the dataset more efficient, I 
organise observations by individual rather than by census panel, retaining the first observed values 
of age, sex, birthplace, and year of marriage and all observations of marital status, occupation, 
subdistrict, and personal and household identifiers. I also create two related datasets: one for 
mothers and children, linking women to lists of all co-resident children observed in any linked census 
entry by age and sex; and another for spouses including all observations for both husband and wife, 
their year of marriage, and responses to the 1911 fertility survey. 

Looking at individual timelines rather than individual observations in this way reveals some linking errors, mostly due to false negatives rather than false positives. For example, the linking process should not result in a woman linked to two distinct husbands, but this could appear to happen if the husband wasn’t linked between the same censuses because of name or age dissimilarity (particularly due to nicknames and middle names). Where an individual is observed with a spouse in each of two censuses, but the spouse entries were not linked to each other in the initial linking process, I accept a new link between the two spouse entries with larger name and age discrepancies as long as it does not result in competing links with other potential spouses.

The same could happen for children if a mother is linked between subsequent censuses but one of her children is not, which would create the impression that the mother had two separate children of the same age. I force a new link between children in the same household at two censuses if they have the same gender and age, after accounting for twins of the same age who are observed together in the household at one census. Where a child is linked to a different household than the mother in a subsequent census, I replace that link with one inside the household if the entries match on sex and age. Initially, the longitudinal dataset contains 1,251,741 individuals (although some ‘individuals’ will in fact represent unlinked observations for the same individual). After reconciling spouse and child relationships, the dataset covers 1,227,874 census-person timelines.

Table 3.6 
Number of observations by first and last linked census record, 1881‐1911 

| Census | 1881 1891 | 1901 | 1911 | No. of observations |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1881 218,074 | 67,958 | 41,491 | 58,424 | 4 |
| 1891 | 153,814 | 47,712 | 63,607 | 3 |
| 1901 | | 154,247 | 121,284 | 2 |
| 1911 | | | 301,260 | 1 |

Source: Linked I‐CeM data; Derbyshire Registration County.

In total there are 99,915 individuals who are observed in at least the first three linked censuses, or 38% of the population aged twenty and over in 1901 (Table 3.6). Match rates improve over the final three decades, and there are 122,890 individuals with links from 1891 to 1901 and 1911, or 39.7% of the linkable population in 1911. Combining these groups of links requires that individuals are present in each consecutive census, although linking between non-consecutive censuses might obtain added matches for individuals who left and re-entered the county. However, a greater time span also carries the risk of more false links, particularly since linking to the marriage indexes over this time span carries more uncertainty. For the entire period, 58,424 people are identified in every census from 1881 to 1911, or 27% of the 1911 population over thirty. This group is skewed towards links for men, representing 56% of the whole-period links, but includes 12,776 women who married in one of these three decades of observation.

Without the inclusion of indexes of marriage, following so many women over such an extended period would be impossible. Links via marriage indexes improve match rates over multiple decades by up to five percentage points. The combination of all census and index links reveals that most of the women linked across marriages are traced over several decades – just 24% are only observed in the censuses immediately before and after marriage. Out of 76,553 women who are linked across more than one decade, 27,748 of these life courses include the date and location of marriage. This pattern is driven by the strength of combining individual and household linking, since women are likely to be linked in conjunction with their parents before marriage and with their husbands after marriage.

I draw from each of these data tables – individual linked timelines, husband-wife pairs, and mother-child groups – to assemble samples for further data enrichment and analysis in the following chapters. I will mostly be focusing on married women whom I observe in the 1911 census and the subset who are linked to one or more previous censuses, including the census before their marriage. I describe each selected sample – coverage, composition, and potential bias – in the introduction to each chapter. But first, in the next chapter, I explain the method used to reconstruct complete birth histories based on the 1911 census and the linked household data.

CHAPTER 4: RECONSTRUCTING BIRTH HISTORIES USING LINKED HOUSEHOLD DATA AND THE 1911 CENSUS FERTILITY SURVEY

Introduction2

In the first chapter, I discussed theories about stopping and spacing of births (i.e. parity-dependent and parity-independent forms of family limitation) during the fertility transition. Changes in the timing of births could be as important for fertility decline as changes in the total number of births over the course of a woman’s childbearing years. However, evaluating these theories requires detailed birth histories for individual women, which include maternal ages at birth and birth intervals, and these types of data are lacking for most historical populations. Unlike research in parts of Europe and Scandinavia which can make use of comprehensive civil registration sources or continuous population registers for family reconstitution, the main source for this research in England and Wales is the 1911 census fertility survey.

The 1911 marriage and fertility survey provides valuable information on total marital fertility regarding children ever born and children surviving per married woman but lacks details on individual births. Estimates of recent net fertility by mother’s age use observations of children in the same household in the census, but these measures are also affected by unobserved changes in age at marriage and infant mortality (Jaadla et al., 2020). Indirect estimation techniques such as the Own Children Method address this bias by combining counts of coresident children with estimates of infant mortality to calculate age-specific fertility rates, but the results do not allow for analysis of individual-level fertility trajectories (Reid et al., 2020). Short of obtaining individual birth and death certificates, fertility is observed primarily in the aggregate.

Creating large-scale datasets on childbearing histories, even with modern technologies, is resource intensive. Luther and Cho (1988) developed a method to address these data limitations by reconstructing individual birth histories based on retrospective surveys (such as the 1974 Korea National Fertility Survey) and household census data. With the release and digitization of historical census data, this method has been adapted to sources like the 1900 and 1910 US censuses (Hacker, 2020). The ages of coresident children in the census are known and, combined with survey responses on the number of children ever born and surviving, these data reveal the number of children who died or left home whose ages are not known but can be imputed. The results are expected to be most reliable for women with fewer unobserved children – mostly younger or more

2 A version of this chapter was submitted to Population Studies on 30 May 2025 and received a revise decision and reviewer comments on 14 October 2025. It has also been deposited as a working paper: Diduch, E. (2025). Reconstructing birth histories using linked household data and the 1911 census fertility survey [Working Paper]. Cambridge Working Papers in Economic and Social History. https://doi.org/10.17863/CAM.118480

recently married women with children under fifteen years old (too young to leave home) and who have reported lower infant and child mortality.

The estimated probability distribution for each imputed year of birth is derived from standardized fertility schedules, which can have important effects on the reconstructed birth intervals. Particularly during the period of transition, when both levels and trends of fertility are expected to differ between subgroups and cohorts, finer-grained analysis may be biased by generalized fertility inputs. An expansion of the Luther and Cho method addresses this concern by iterating the imputation process and re-fitting standard fertility curves with Poisson regressions which incorporate contextual demographic data from the census (Hacker, 2020; Timæus et al., 2019).

My linked census dataset allows me to take this method a step further, as records linked to the 1911 census can supplement the retrospective marriage and fertility data with information about household structure, occupation, and migration from previous censuses. Record linking also increases the number of children whom I observe with their mothers at any census and extends the chronological span of observed marriages and births. For women who are linked across multiple censuses, these observations go a long way towards completing birth histories, especially for surviving children, by capturing the eldest children at one census who have left the household before the youngest are observed a decade later. However, even with perfect record linking, many of the total births reported in the fertility survey remain unobserved due to the births and deaths of infants and children in the ten years between censuses.

Thus, I adapt Luther and Cho’s method to use partial birth histories obtained from linked census data for Derbyshire. I compare three versions of the method: first following Luther and Cho to reconstruct complete birth histories using only the 1911 census sample (V1), then incorporating linked census data from between 1881 and 1911 to reduce the number of births which need to be imputed (V2), and finally using Poisson regression to create bespoke fertility schedules and re impute the missing birthyears (V3). Each iteration of the method is expected to improve the precision of the reconstructed birth histories. After describing the data preparation and each method version, I illustrate the results using duration-specific marital fertility rates for different birth cohorts and occupational groups.

Data Out of the 90,000 married women in Derbyshire who answered the fertility questionnaire, 77,000 had at least one birth to their current marriage before 1911, and around 50,000 have been linked to at least one previous census. Figure 4.1 illustrates how the linked data alter the scope of

childbearing observations. The top, blue line tracks the average reported number of children ever born (CEB) by the mother’s birthyear (the vertical dashed line separates women who had completed their childbearing years from those who could go on to have more births after 1911). The bottom, red line tracks the average number of children per woman that are residing in the same household in 1911. At every point this estimate is lower than the reported CEB because of infant mortality, but the shortfall dramatically increases for mothers in their forties in 1911, as more of their surviving children had left home. Among the oldest cohorts – who are important for describing trends throughout the fertility transition – less than a fifth of their children can be observed in the same households in 1911.

In contrast, the middle green line tracks the number of children per woman that are observed with their mothers either in 1911 or in links to the same household in a previous census. As stated in the previous chapter, I have reconciled the linked data to avoid double-counting children who are observed at different censuses but not linked to each other (i.e. determining whether the census records belong to the same individual and were not linked due to error or whether they are distinct children with similar ages). Although not every woman is linked in every census, the number of children matched to their mothers via census linking significantly reduces the

Figure 4.1 
Children per woman present in the 1911 census and observed in linked census data compared with 
reported children ever born 

Source: Linked I-CeM data; Derbyshire Registration County.

gap between observed and reported births for older women. For women who married in 1861, on average half of their reported births can be observed in the linked data. Similar coverage in the cross-sectional 1911 data is not reached until marriages from the 1880s.

The remaining gap between observed and reported births is due to a combination of unlinked records, child mortality, and children leaving home before 1881. Among women who are linked in every census between 1881 and 1911 (20.7% of mothers in the 1911 survey), 30% of their CEB remain missing, either due to mortality or leaving home. Following the imputation methods described below, 15% of these missing births are estimated to have occurred before 1881, and 85% after – since these children are unlikely to have escaped observation in the next census by leaving home before the age of ten, these estimates demonstrate that even with perfect record linking, infant mortality is a significant cause of censored observations in the partial birth histories. These totals and the subsequent discussion are based on women who married after 1861, which is the earliest date for which duration-specific fertility rates were calculated. This restriction excludes fewer than 500 older women who were still married and reported their fertility in 1911 and does not substantially affect my analysis in later chapters, since these women cannot be linked to a census before marriage and are unlikely to have contributed to the fertility decline.

Both the 1911 cross-section (red line) and the linked household data (green line) are thus biased samples of total fertility (blue line). The remaining unobserved births are not randomly censored, and neither are the timelines of women who are not linked between censuses. Analysis of birth histories for just the 30,000 women whose reported CEB matches the number of coresident offspring in 1911, or the additional 8,000 women whose total CEB are observed through record linking, would seriously underestimate both total fertility and infant mortality. Reported deaths of children before 1911 are only 34 per thousand births in the latter selection, compared with 294 per thousand for women whose birth histories remained incomplete after linking across censuses. Given the high proportion of these deaths which occurred in early infancy, these missing births are unlikely to be observed in any census. These biases motivate the reconstruction of birth histories for the remaining population.

Methods

Version 1: Replicating Luther and Cho’s method Luther and Cho presented their reconstruction method as an extension of the Own Children Method for estimating total fertility. ‘Own children’ are those who are matched to their mothers by being enumerated in the same household. Comparing this number to the reported number of children ever born (CEB) and children died (CD) reveals the number of ‘non-own’ children who had left home

(CLH). Partial birth histories for own children provide the framework for imputing the birthyears of these CD and CLH. In contrast to the survey data used by Luther and Cho and the US census data used by Hacker (2020), the CEB reported in the 1911 census for England and Wales were supposed to refer only to the fertility of current marriages, for which duration is also reported. Duration of marriage reduces uncertainty in the reconstruction process by allowing more precise estimates of the window of time a woman was at risk of an unobserved birth. Here I refer to each woman’s birth history in terms of year of marriage and child’s birthyear rather than as mother’s implied age at birth relative to age at survey date in the terminology of Luther and Cho, also used by Hacker.

Approximate birth dates for CD and CLH are imputed by estimating a probability distribution for a possible set of years based on a woman’s marital duration and the cumulative risk that a child born in year y has either died or left home before 1911. This adjustment for survival accounts for the lower likelihood that a child born closer to 1911 has already left the household, and vice versa. For each possible year of birth, I obtain cumulative mortality by 1911 from cohort life tables (Human Mortality Database, 2017). The probability that a child born in year y has left the household by 1911 is obtained from both the probability that a child born in y has survived to 1911 and the proportion of y-born children in 1911 who are non-own, i.e. not matched to their mothers.

For each woman, CD and CLH are iteratively positioned in the context of own births. The standard duration-specific fertility rate for the first year of marriage is adjusted downward to account for the time to conception and first birth, and for marriages that took place mid-way through the year. 3 The probability of a birth occurring in each year-centred interval of the fertility distribution is adjusted for whether the unobserved child is alive or dead at the time of the census. Luther and Cho obtained this probability from an integrable fertility curve, but it can also be estimated by subtracting the values of the cumulative distribution function that open and close the interval. The estimated probability of birth is reduced to zero at each known birthyear and by 75% for years on either side. This notched interval enforces spacing of the imputed births to account for the duration of pregnancy and postpartum insusceptibility.

To avoid bias, each imputed birth is selected randomly from a woman’s remaining number of CD and CLH. After each iteration, the imputed birth is notched from the fertility curve, and the process is repeated for the next child. Figure 4.2 illustrates standard and notched fertility rates for a woman who reported a marital duration of 21 years and five children ever born, of which three survived and were observed in the census and two had died. The standard fertility rate is notched

3 Thanks to Ian Timæus for sharing duration‐specific fertility rates based on reverse‐survival; for methods see the Tools for Demographic Estimation online resource (Timæus & Moultrie, 2012).

for intervals around the ages of the three own children with implied birthyears in 1891, 1896, and 1899. The red bars show the estimated yearly birth probabilities conditional on a child having died before 1911. The first missing child has been randomly assigned a birthyear of 1893, and the second has been randomly assigned a birthyear of 1907.

In a few cases the loop is broken when there are too many reported births to fit in the standard fertility distribution; this could be due to misreporting of previous marriages or errors in transcription. Mothers without successfully reconstructed birth histories are excluded from the analysis in later chapters. In addition, I have not made any assumptions in the reconstruction process about the prevalence of multiple births. There are 3,692 ‘observed’ twins in the partial birth histories – that is, children of the same mother, with the same age at the same census. Assuming these are all true twins and not children with short birth intervals, these observations would represent an incidence of 1.2% of all births, compared with an expected rate of twins as 2% of births, leaving approximately 3,600 unobserved twin births. If all unobserved twins are missing in pairs, this would mean that 3,600 of the imputed births are wrongly identified as singletons; if all unobserved twin pairs have one observed member (for example, if all the missing twins are due to the death of one and survival of the other) then around 1,800 of the imputed births are falsely given a unique

Figure 4.2 
Example duration-specific fertility schedule for a woman married in 1890 with three own children born 
1891, 1896, and 1899 and two children dead 

Source: Linked I-CeM data. Fertility schedules from Timæus et al. (2019).

birthyear when they should have been paired with an observed birth. In both cases, the effect might bias results towards shorter estimated birth intervals for the mothers of twins.

Version 2: Adapting the method using linked data 
Before the Luther and Cho method can be replicated with the linked data, observed children and 
children ever born must be reconciled with children dead and children having left home. In the 
linked data, children may be observed at a previous census but be non-own – that is, not in the 
mother’s household – in 1911. Children present at a previous census may also have died before 
1911, adding to the categories of observation: compared with her reported CEB, a woman’s partial 
birth history may include a combination of own children; observed non-own children; observed dead 
children; unobserved non-own children; and unobserved dead children. 

I can deduce the survival of children from earlier censuses if they are linked to other households in 1911. A smaller number of children can be declared dead by process of elimination based on the status of their siblings and their mother’s reported CEB and CD. This leaves a substantial number of children observed at previous censuses who either died before 1911 or survived but were not linked to the final census. I categorize each child based on several assumptions: if a child is present with the mother or linked outside the household in 1911, it is included in the number of children surviving; if the number of unobserved children is equal to or less than the number of reported dead children, all unobserved children are declared dead; if there are additional reported child deaths and one or more children observed at an earlier census have not been linked to any other census, the earliest born, unlinked child is assumed to have died; finally, any additional unobserved surviving children are accounted for.

These assumptions are based on the expected effects of record linking on patterns of observed and unobserved births. Linking women over time as their children left the parental household is expected to decrease the proportion of unobserved births belonging to surviving children, with mortality being the main reason for residual unobserved births. Similarly for children who are observed with their mothers only at one previous census, I expect mortality to be one of the main reasons for unlinked census records, along with migration and (for women) marriage; but these latter forces primarily affect older children as they leave the household.

The primary goal is to arrive at the expected total number of children surviving and dead; in this application, the death or survival of an observed child does not directly affect the imputed birthyear of an unobserved sibling. For analysis that is interested in the implications of infant and child mortality for parity progression, these assumptions would need to be refined, for example considering the age at which children are first observed to determine the likelihood that they died

before the next census. However, it is important to remember that reported child deaths could have occurred at any age (including into adulthood); high rates of infant and early childhood mortality make it more likely but not certain that these deaths occurred at young ages.

For example: I observe a woman with three own children in the 1911 census who is linked in previous censuses with another eight children in the household. The woman’s 1911 census entry reported ten children surviving and three dead out of thirteen children ever born. The eleven children observed over multiple censuses leave two births to be imputed, and at least one of the observed children must have died before 1911. Two of the children observed at earlier censuses are not linked to any later census entry (born 1872 and 1881). Thus, I allocate the two unobserved children and one child born 1872 to the reported number of CD.

After making these assumptions, I filter the data to exclude birth histories in which the inferred number of dead and surviving children does not match the reported totals in 1911; this condition applies to women who are observed with more than the expected number of children because of either reporting errors, linking errors, or children or stepchildren from another marriage. In 7,574 cases, women are observed with more children than reported ever born; 3,565 of these observations are due to the number of children present in the household in 1911. It is likely that these observations represent children from the previous marriages of one or both spouses, and indeed 2,374 of these potential stepmothers are enumerated with children whose ages imply that they were born before their mother’s reported date of marriage.

Version 3: Refining fertility inputs using Poisson regression 
The standard duration-specific fertility rates used in the imputation process do not vary by age at 
marriage or marriage cohort. The reconstructed birth histories will thus tend to smooth differences 
in the pace of childbearing between cohorts, especially for groups with a higher proportion of 
imputed births due to duration of marriage, higher child mortality, or lower record linking rates. 
These biases will affect analysis of fertility trajectories for different social classes, occupational 
groups, or regions. Following Hacker (2020) and Timæus et al. (2019), I refit the standardized fertility 
curves for each subgroup using a Poisson regression model. These bespoke fertility estimates are 
then used to re-impute the missing observations. 

Hacker performed four iterations of the Poisson regression to predict age-specific fertility rates by a range of individual-level variables. Hacker’s regression results by geographic and occupational subgroups showed the strongest convergence after the first iteration. In these data, record linking already substantially reduces uncertainty in the partial birth histories, so I chose to estimate the Poisson model once and re-impute the histories using the predicted duration-specific

fertility rates. Since not all women with reported birth histories in 1911 have links to other censuses, the variables included in the Poisson regression are restricted to the information available at the final census, which might not capture the characteristics of a couple during their peak childbearing years.

The Poisson regression predicts the number of births per woman per year adjusted for a woman’s age at marriage, marital duration, district classification (urban, rural, textile, mining), and husband’s occupational group (agricultural labour, heavy industry, professional classes, shopkeepers and retailers, textile workers, transport workers, and others). The total number of observed and imputed births by group and year are offset by the population at risk (women of a given marital duration) to obtain predicted fertility rates. Interactions between categorical and chronological variables allow predicted fertility curves to vary in both level and shape. When the imputation process is repeated, a few women cannot be matched to a re-fitted fertility curve due to missing data (for example, null values for husband’s occupation), so the final sample is a combination of 161 V2 and 33,772 V3 reconstructed birth histories in addition to the 38,158 complete birth histories already observed.

Results 
The advantage of combining Luther and Cho’s reconstruction method with linked census data is that 
there is a smaller proportion of births to be imputed, especially for marriages of longer duration. The 
1911 Derbyshire census extract includes 200,545 own children in the same households as their 
mothers; record linking retrieves another 50,399 own children from earlier censuses whose mothers 
are linked to 1911. The number of remaining births to impute is thus decreased by two fifths, from 
120,746 to 70,347. For women married for 25-30 years, on average half of their CEB would need to 
be imputed based solely on own children in 1911, compared to on average less than a quarter of CEB 
missing from the linked data. 

Duration-specific marital fertility rates (DSMFRs) derived from the linked, reconstructed birth histories include more estimates based on observed rather than imputed birthyears. The DSMFRs differ in shape from the rates obtained from the birth histories reconstructed using only own children in 1911 (Figure 4.3). The linked data result in the greatest differences among the middle cohort in Figure 4.3, born in the 1850s and marrying in the 1870s and 1880s. These women are likely to be observed soon after marriage with young children at home in the 1881 census, so that links to subsequent censuses up to 1911 cover the entirety of their childbearing careers.

The V2 reconstructed DSMFRs for the 1850s cohort are higher in the early years of marriage than those obtained from only own children in 1911. The V1 reconstructed birth histories estimate

that women in this cohort had 33.6% of their total births within five years of marriage; with the linked data (V2), the reconstructed birth histories suggest that 37% of births occurred within the first five years after marriage. On either side of this cohort, the differences between the V1 and V2 results are less noticeable. There is a larger proportion of births imputed for mothers born in the 1840s, due to infant mortality and children leaving home before 1881; conversely, among women born in the 1860s, more of their children were still at home in 1911 (56.6% of CEB vs 37% of CEB co resident in 1911 for women born in the 1850s), so the added benefit of the linked data is more limited.

Results by husbands’ occupational group using the Poisson-predicted fertility schedules differ from the results of the other two method iterations using standardised fertility schedules (Figure 4.4). The effect is greater for groups that have a higher proportion of imputed births – for example, wives whose husbands worked in heavy industries and mining reported over 215 deaths per thousand births, compared with around 150 per thousand for women whose husbands worked in white-collar and professional occupations. The adjusted fertility schedules predict birth rates falling more slowly with marital duration for industrial couples than for others, while the V3 DSMFRs

Figure 4.3 
Duration-specific marital fertility rates by birth cohort, birth histories reconstructed using own 
children in 1911 and linked census data 1881-1911 

Source: Linked I-CeM data; Derbyshire Registration County. Duration-specific marital fertility rates calculated based on reconstructed birth histories as described in text.

for women whose husbands worked in professional occupations are not substantially different than the results from Versions 1 and 2 using standard fertility schedules. Seventy percent of CEB in the professional group are observed in the linked data, compared with 64% of total births to the wives of miners. The V3 birth histories increase the estimated average age at last birth for industrial couples (from 40 to 41.2) while only slightly altering the estimate for professional couples (38.3 to 38.7). These duration-specific patterns of childbearing are important for understanding how wives aged 45-65 in 1911 with professional husbands had reduced their total fertility to an average of 3.5 CEB, compared with 5.9 CEB to the average woman with a husband in industry.

Limitations The reconstructed birth intervals must be treated with caution, both because the intervals between imputed births are inherently uncertain and because the imputation process alters the inferred birth order of observed children. In addition to the possible effects of ignoring twins in the imputation process mentioned above, the data do not account for stillbirths or miscarriages. The results may thus imply that births were more evenly spaced across a woman’s reproductive career than was the case, since the time a woman actually spent at risk of pregnancy is unknown. Ages and marriage

Figure 4.4 
Duration-specific marital fertility rates for wives born between 1846 and 1866 and married to husbands in 
selected occupational groups 
Source: As for Figure 4.3. 
Note: ‘industry’ = miners and metalworkers; ‘professions’ = all white-collar occupations. 

durations in the census are reported in completed years, which makes implied birthyears and observed birth intervals fuzzy. Depending on the distance between precise birthdays and the date of the census, the true interval between events could vary by almost a year on either side of the estimate. This level of measurement results in a greater burden of proof to demonstrate significant differences in average birth intervals in terms of years rather than months.

Reported duration of marriage provides information about the time a woman was at risk for unobserved births but also prevents births from being plausibly imputed before marriage. In the partial birth histories, 5,054 children are observed with ages greater than their mother’s reported marital duration. These births are retained in the birth histories but are not notched from the marital-duration specific fertility curve in the imputation process. I made no direct adjustments for the rate of premarital conception among unobserved births in the imputation process; after estimating the Poisson regression, higher predicted fertility rates in the first year of marriage may reflect these patterns. The survey data are also subject to errors in reporting and recall which preclude confident estimates of rates of illegitimacy and bridal pregnancy, including women rounding up their marital duration to agree with the age of their oldest child. In addition, a large amount of total fertility remains unobserved because the sample excludes births to women who were widowed or single.

Recent studies using similarly reconstructed birth history data for the United States restrict their analysis to birth events occurring in the fifteen years before a census (see for example Alter & Hacker, 2024; Gauvreau et al., 2025). Using the linked data, I can more confidently use the reconstructed birth histories for marriages from earlier in the nineteenth century. On the other hand, my analysis of women’s occupations before marriage can only be based on observations for couples linked to the index of marriages after 1881. The linked data cover three decades of marriages and births but include a smaller range of ‘completed’ fertility – i.e., women observed from before marriage to the age of 50, whose birth histories are complete. In the following analysis I deal with these limitations in several ways: by comparing childbearing trajectories by age for cohorts from different stages of the fertility transition; by using parity-specific models which can account for censored intervals due to age at observation; and by exploring marriage patterns for a larger sample of women in the linked dataset.

CHAPTER 5: WORK, MARRIAGE, AND CHILDBEARING TRAJECTORIES IN DERBYSHIRE, 1881-1911

Introduction4

As shown in Chapter 1, male occupations have featured prominently in studies of historical fertility decline. Since the Registrar General, analyses have classified couples by husbands’ occupation and social class to capture some element of their motivation (or lack thereof) to limit births within marriage. In different contexts, these classifications can be taken to represent the economic determinants of fertility related to wage levels, labour market fluctuations, and the prevalence of child labour (Friedlander, 1973; Hacker et al., 2021; Ittmann, 1992); or these groupings can be intended to reflect shared social norms, cultures of childbearing, or aspirations to certain standards of living (Banks, 1954; Bras et al., 2013; Pooley, 2013). Occupation has thus featured on both sides of arguments for either an adjustment or an innovation-diffusion process of fertility decline (Bras, 2014; Carlsson, 1966; Casterline, 2001; Friedlander et al., 1999). Studies of occupation ask whether differing levels of marital fertility by employment reflect how these groups responded to changing incentives regarding family size or whether they arose from shared ideas about family size and birth control which had as much to do with social networks as with labour markets.

Studies of fertility decline in other countries in Western Europe have benefitted from growing access to longitudinal microdata and birth histories reconstructed from population registers. Bras and Schumacher (2019), using sequence analysis and occupations from marriage certificates linked to birth certificates in the Netherlands, found that age homogamy between spouses explained some of the propensity to adopt parity-specific birth control, while much of the variation in fertility trajectories was driven by social class. Dribe et al. (2017) assembled comparable male occupational data from five countries in Europe and North America, finding a consistent upper class vanguard when these populations began their fertility transitions. However, couples further down the social ladder did not follow consistent rankings in fertility levels or trends. These results reveal the limitations of broad class categorization as a theoretical framework for fertility decline, when the social and occupational composition of such categories are neither geographically nor temporally stable. Women’s occupations also do not fall neatly into the same class hierarchies. Other European studies using more granular occupational groupings have highlighted the ways in which fertility transitions were often dependent on highly local contexts (Bengtsson & Dribe, 2014; Gortfelder et al., 2021).

4 A version of this chapter was published as Diduch, E. (2025). Work, marriage, and childbearing trajectories in Derbyshire, 1881–1911. The History of the Family, 1–29. https://doi.org/10.1080/1081602X.2025.2564391

Postponing or spacing births has been hypothesized as an important component of the low fertility of the wives of textile workers in England mentioned above. In areas where married women’s labour force participation was high, like in the textile districts of Lancashire and the West Riding of Yorkshire, married female textile workers might have been motivated to delay first or second births in order to continue working (Atkinson, 2012; Garrett, 1990). Lower fertility among active female textile workers could also be an effect of selection, as women with fewer surviving children were more available for work (Garrett & Reid, 1994). Alternatively, textile factories and their dominance as employers in local communities could facilitate the diffusion of attitudes and knowledge concerning birth control, contributing to lower fertility among former textile operatives who left work (Gittins, 1982; McDonald & Moyle, 2018).

Domestic service was the most common occupation for single women in the nineteenth century and therefore must also be considered as a possible conduit for fertility decline. Echoing Banks (1981), Szreter highlighted the low fertility of male domestic servants to argue that residential servants absorbed contraceptive knowledge and attitudes from their employers. On the other hand, Gittins’ (1982) interviews with former domestic servants found that they were often isolated socially and notably ignorant in matters of sex and birth control. Matthys (2013), using linked population data from Flanders, found that female domestic servants had fewer births than other working-class women, but also pointed out the difficulty in distinguishing between the effects of service and the effects of urban migration on the women in her sample.

Many occupations, particularly in both the textile and mining industries, were also significantly geographically clustered, participating in distinct local cultures and social norms governing work and family life. Garrett et al. (2001) emphasized the interaction between occupation and location to argue that much of the total difference in fertility between occupational groups was due to their differential geographical distribution. Their analysis reflects a compositional approach towards fertility decline which reconsiders occupational and social groupings at the level of the individual, couple, community, and cohort (Kreager, 2011; Szreter & Garrett, 2000). A compositional framework recognizes that subgroups are not static over time and may be affected both by changing composition within groups and changing relations between groups, as overlapping identities are created through marriage, migration, or kinship. Such shifting compositions make it difficult to isolate the effects of discrete variables, as these effects are highly contingent on other mediating characteristics such as spouse’s occupation, residential contexts, and migration status.

This chapter explores the role of both male and female occupations in the trajectories of fertility decline in Derbyshire between the 1870s and 1911. Having reconstructed complete birth histories from the linked census data and the 1911 fertility survey, I can now assess the relative roles

of starting, stopping, and spacing in the fertility transition in Derbyshire in much greater detail than has previously been possible. However, I also now have a mismatch between the level of granularity in the birth history data and in the information available from the census. I know when each of a woman’s children were likely born, but I only know her occupation and place of residence every ten years.

Previous analyses using the 1911 census were retrospective, assessing patterns of past fertility by current occupational status. I begin my exploration of demographic trends in Derbyshire by flipping the script and prospectively relating occupations before marriage to subsequent trajectories of marital fertility. The birth histories provide the building blocks for sequence analysis, in which all of the components of total fertility are considered simultaneously as parts of a continuous trajectory (Abbott, 1983; Bras & Schumacher, 2019). I use a clustering algorithm to identify associations between demographic states within these sequences. This approach reveals, for example, whether longer birth intervals were associated with later marriage or with stopping at a lower parity. The resulting childbearing typologies, defined by both the number and timing of births, allow me to characterise multifaceted childbearing histories by a single variable which I model with occupational and geographical covariates using multinomial logistic regression.

I compare the results of cluster analysis and regression models for three cohorts of women born at different stages of the fertility transition. The sequence analysis approach allows me to assess longer term trends in marital fertility during the Demographic Transition, even though I only have complete information about both pre-marital occupation and completed family size for a limited cohort of women, by revealing commonalities and differences in the childbearing trajectories of all three cohorts. These cohorts reveal that the shift away from a high fertility norm involved both stopping at a lower number of births and increasing the intervals between births. I discuss the role of the occupational and social backgrounds of both husband and wife in determining a couple’s adoption of family limitation in terms of their ability to negotiate and act on changing fertility goals, conditioned on their relative ages, social backgrounds, and contributions to the family economy (Seccombe, 1992).

In the following analysis, I consider the extent to which female and male occupations – jointly or independently – were associated with different fertility trajectories. Professional and white-collar occupations are expected to be associated with early fertility decline, along with employment in the textile industry for both women and men. The new visibility of other types of female employment, including domestic service, motivates this focus on occupation as the variable of interest rather than broader social class groupings. Couples who were more similar in background or whose contributions to the family economy were more equally distributed may have been more

able to agree and act on desires for smaller families, whether these desires derived from social pressures or economic incentives.

Data and methods

Childbearing sequences 
Using the reconstructed birth histories described in Chapter 4, I combine age at marriage and age at 
every birth into holistic fertility trajectories. Each woman’s reproductive period between the ages of 
15 and 50 is described at yearly intervals according to her marital status and achieved parity. These 
sequences reveal how individual elements of total fertility, such as age at last birth, are related to all 
others, including age at first birth and inter-birth intervals. These relationships between states within 
sequences are identified using cluster analysis and are in turn used to analyse changes in total 
marital fertility over time. I take a similar approach to that of Bras and Schumacher (2019) for the 
Netherlands, while adopting a different definition of fertility sequences by age at marriage that also 
places greater emphasis on parity-specific birth control. 

Focusing on premarital occupations in relation to completed marital fertility requires careful sample selection by age. Older cohorts who might exhibit pre-transition or early transition fertility patterns were mostly married before they could be observed in the first linked census in 1881. Younger women are more likely to have been followed over several censuses before marriage, but their completed fertility and marriage rates after 1911 are unknown. Identifying patterns of parity specific family limitation in marriages of longer duration requires observing women past the age of 50 who are unlikely to have had any further pregnancies.

To observe change over time while maximising the amount of information that can be gathered about individuals, I choose three birth cohorts for comparison: the first, born between 1851 and 1856, selects women who were mostly married before 1881 and who came of age at the onset of the fertility transition; the second cohort born 1861 to 1866 includes women who can be observed before marriage aged between 15 and 20 in the 1881 census and were aged 45 to 50 in 1911, having completed their childbearing; the final cohort of women born between 1871 and 1876 are observed before marriage in one or two censuses and were still at risk of future births aged 35 to 40 in 1911.

These samples include only fertile marriages, where women reported at least one birth before 1911. The chances of remaining childless are considered separately in Chapter 6, but I exclude childless marriages from this sequence analysis for both theoretical and practical reasons. Drawing on both the 1911 census reports and from the 1946 Family Census, Anderson (1998) found that the proportion of couples who remained childless increased slightly between marriage cohorts

in the late nineteenth century, but was small in comparison with the shift towards one- and two child families in the same period and with the increase in childlessness after the First World War. Szreter and Schürer (2019) argued that sexually-transmitted diseases increasingly caused sterility among some of the male professional classes during this period, leading to delayed conceptions and higher rates of childlessness. However, these changes in themselves do not account for a substantial amount of fertility decline. In analysis of a sample of couples in 1911 by marital duration, Garrett et al. (2001) found that age at marriage was more important than social class in explaining different rates of childlessness.

In addition, there are data issues which particularly affect childless couples in the 1911 census. Figure 5.1 shows the distribution of children ever born in 1911 by husbands’ occupations and birth cohorts. Between 10% and 17% of couples in each group were apparently childless. Since these cohorts are selected by age rather than marital duration, some childless couples in 1911 were only recently married. The 1911 fertility survey was directed to current unions, excluding children born to widows or widowers. A remarried woman could therefore have been correct in recording no children born to her current marriage despite being observed with coresident offspring. Older ages

Figure 5.1 Parity distributions by birth cohort and husband’s occupational category

Source: Linked I-CeM data; Derbyshire Registration County, women in selected birth cohorts as 
described in text, who married between the ages of 15 and 45. 
Note: ‘aglab’ = farmers, agricultural labourers, and general labourers; ‘industry’ = miners and metal 
workers; ‘professions’ = all white-collar occupations. 

at marriage for apparently childless women may be skewed upwards by second marriages of shorter duration. In addition, children who were recorded with ages greater than their mother’s reported marital duration may represent illegitimate births or stepchildren; equally, women may have rounded up the duration of their current marriage to agree with the age of their eldest child.

These idiosyncrasies make it difficult to reconcile potential errors in the linked data and in the census returns in the case of childless couples. If the number of children linked to a mother at a previous census or the age of a woman linked to the index of marriages does not agree with the information that she later reported in 1911, the discrepancy may be due to errors either in linking or in enumeration. In the record linking process I focused on first marriages, so timelines for men and women who remarried are censored. Reconstructing birth histories for women who had at least one child ensures a baseline level of consistency in reported, observed, and imputed births by marital duration. Excluding childless women will inflate estimates of total marital fertility but will avoid falsely characterizing some groups as more prone to late marriage or childlessness who were in fact more prone to remarriage. In Chapter 6, I address these issues in my selection of a separate sample of childless couples.

For each of the three selected cohorts, the reconstructed birth histories are used to define childbearing sequences using the TraMineR package in R (Gabadinho et al., 2011). Each sequence is made up of states defined by having experienced events like marriage and childbirth, and the duration of each state is measured in years to the next event or to the end of observation. Bras and Schumacher (2019) focused on three temporal aspects of reproduction: a ‘starting’ phase beginning at age fifteen and ending with the first birth (rather than marriage), a reproductive phase encompassing all subsequent births, and a ‘stopping’ phase following the last birth up to age 45. Here, I further define starting patterns by a separate state for the duration between marriage and first birth; place greater emphasis on birth spacing and durations of states during the reproductive phase; and break down stopping patterns by the parities at which women stopped.

Childbearing sequences are thus made up of nine states: all women experience at least the first three states (single, married with no children, married with one child); separate states designate the duration at each parity from two to five (the median number of births in the pre-transition cohort); births past the fifth birth are grouped together into one state; and for women with more than five births, a final state is defined for the duration between the last birth and the end of the sequence, when the mother reaches age 50 (Figure 5.2). Thus, the final state in each sequence depends on the total number of births and the mother’s age at last birth; this approach emphasizes emerging patterns of parity-specific fertility limitation.

Conditional on being present in the 1911 census, the sample size increases between each cohort, reflecting both linking success and marriage survival. In total 4,709 women are included in the 1851-6 cohort; 8,486 in the 1861-6 cohort; and 13,193 in the 1871-6 cohort. Altogether these women had a median age at marriage of 23, and half of those with completed fertility (i.e. from the first and second cohorts) reported between three and eight children ever born. Women in the same age cohorts in the 1911 census who reported no children ever born had a median age at marriage of 26. Average total parity of fertile women decreased from 6.4 births per woman in the oldest cohort to 5.5 births per woman in the second cohort. The youngest cohort, still in the reproductive phase, had achieved 4.1 births per woman by 1911. Average age at last birth similarly decreased from 41.1 in the oldest cohort to 37.6 in the second. The underlying fertility distributions of each cohort illustrate the forces driving these averages downward: the modal number of children born to the oldest cohort was six, representing about 10% of women; in the next cohort, the modal parity, representing almost 13% of women, was only two (Figure 5.1).

Cluster analysis Given this compositional shift towards lower parities, the next step of analysis identifies the components of childbearing sequences which are associated with different fertility regimes. Did total fertility vary independently of age at marriage or average birth intervals, or were these variables correlated within childbearing sequences? Cluster analysis reveals the emergence of a low fertility

Figure 5.2 Ten random childbearing sequences from the 1851-6 birth cohort

Source: Linked I-CeM data; Derbyshire Registration County, reconstructed birth histories for married women observed in the 1911 census with at least 1 CEB.

pattern characterized by a unique progression between childbearing states, including the transitions to marriage, interbirth intervals, and final open birth intervals. Rather than focusing separately on each event, sequence analysis and clustering emphasizes how these events relate to each other within the life course.

To avoid censoring bias, I apply the clustering algorithm on the childbearing sequences of the first two cohorts only, for women who had completed their childbearing before 1911. The states and durations of sequences are compared using optimal matching distances, which represent the minimum amount of insertion, deletion, or substitution needed to transform one sequence into another (Gabadinho et al., 2011). Hierarchical clustering using Ward’s method organizes sequences into groups based on this similarity measure in order to minimize within-cluster variance, and agglomerates similar clusters into groups of increasing size until the final cluster includes all individuals (Maechler et al., 2025). This clustering approach does not require a pre-determined number of clusters, instead creating a nesting of groups which can be divided into any number of clusters post hoc.

Hierarchical clustering of childbearing sequences results in a tree of clusters with several main branches. The first branch divides a cluster of High Fertility sequences, with younger ages at marriage and at least six births per woman, from another cluster of sequences which stop at lower

parities. The second branch, with lower fertility overall, can be split into several more clusters which are distinguished both by the number of children born and by average age at marriage (Figure 5.3). I chose a three-cluster solution as most informative, separating a Low Fertility cluster, with one or two births per woman, from a Moderate Fertility cluster with three, four, or five births per woman.

In addition to experiencing fewer births, women in the former group also married on average two to three years later. The Moderate Fertility cluster could be divided into further categories by parity, but for analytical purposes these three main clusters define the most common childbearing

trajectories.

Another key advantage of this sequence analysis approach is the ability to wind back the clock on fertility patterns by age among the older cohorts and use these observations of the completed fertility to predict fertility typologies for the youngest cohort. Women in this cohort, born between 1871 and 1876, had not yet completed their childbearing trajectories, with an average of

4.1 births per woman by the ages of 35-40 in 1911. The sequences of the older cohorts reveal that, despite the variation described below, childbearing patterns were already well-established by age

35, with very few outliers who ‘caught up’ to higher parities in the following ten years. Although the average ages at marriage for all cohorts are almost identical, the total variance in the third cohort is lower, due both to a reduction in the number of very young marriages and to the omission of older

marriages after age 35-40 (Table 5.1).

Independent variables

I model the features associated with these fertility clusters, including occupational, environmental, and spousal characteristics, with regression analysis. To model an outcome variable with more than two categories, I use a multinomial logistic regression, with the High Fertility cluster as the reference

category. Although total fertility is graduated between clusters, the multinomial regression treats these categories as independent, equal alternatives rather than ordered steps along a deterministic path; an ordinal regression would require that the effect of each variable be proportional on the likelihood of following a Moderate or a Low Fertility trajectory, precluding the possibility that some groups display a greater propensity for one type of trajectory but not the other. For each of the categorical explanatory variables, the reference category is the group with the highest average fertility in each cohort.

Regressions are run separately for the two cohorts (1861-6 and 1871-6) for whom pre marital occupations are observed. I discuss observations of married women’s occupations, but their numbers are too small to be included in the model. Precisely when a woman stopped working outside the home in relation to her marriage and childbearing is not known, but her occupation before marriage is expected to influence both her social networks (and choice of spouse) and the likelihood that she could continue working or return to work as necessary after marriage.

The second and third age cohorts are first observed aged between 15 and 20 in either 1881 or 1891. The pre-marital occupational distributions of these cohorts closely match the recorded occupations for all single women of similar ages in those censuses, although this agreement may hide various biases (Table B.1). A lower proportion of women in the linked sample were employed in domestic service before marriage, probably due to lower matching rates for women from their employer’s household to their spouse’s household as well as to rates of migration. A proportion of single women in the census would also have remained single, and thus not entered the sample of fertile married women. In addition, women without an observed occupation before marriage are a heterogeneous category, because they may represent women from middle-class households who did not have to work, women from mining households with greater domestic burdens, or women whose occupations were missed or misreported at the time of the census (these compositional questions are explored further in Chapter 7).

The husbands of these women may be linked to a different occupational description in each of several censuses. A half to three-quarters of husbands that are linked in more than one census had the same occupational category in both. For ease of comparison, husbands linked over more than two censuses are given a single occupational category defined by the most frequent occupational description, or by the first observation. In addition to the main male occupations in mining, heavy industry, and agriculture, smaller but still significant numbers were employed in the textile industry, in various occupations related to transport, in shopkeeping and retail, and in the professions. These groups are highlighted both to avoid a large residual ‘other’ category and to explore variations in family forms related to this work. For example, shopkeepers and other small

business proprietors might have their family involved in the business or live on the premises, perhaps providing motivation for a more moderate family size. Similarly, female dressmakers might be more able to combine home-based work and childcare.

A version of the model which controls for male and female occupations separately is presented in Table B.2. Exploratory analysis of cluster membership reveals significant compositional differences within occupational groups driven by assortative mating. Some of these occupational groups are too small, or too rarely combined, to be modelled as separate interaction terms. Instead, I create a couple-level occupational categorization based on the most common patterns of inter marriage. I define the variable in this way to test whether couples from similar circumstances were more likely to follow the expected fertility trajectories than couples from different, even clashing, backgrounds.

The couple-level variable distinguishes between persistently high fertility, ‘traditional’ groups on one hand, including domestic servants, agricultural workers, and men in heavy industries, and the rapidly declining fertility of textile workers on the other. Other occupational categories which follow common trends towards low fertility, including dressmakers, transport workers, and shopkeepers, are numerically too small to be further broken down into subtypes, so they are grouped together with the professions to provide a larger comparison category representing the vanguard of fertility decline. Rare occupational combinations (such as the 3% of domestic servants married to male textile workers) are also grouped with other categories.

Both husbands’ and wives’ ages at marriage are based on the ages reported in the census and the duration of marriage reported in the fertility survey. Women’s ages at marriage are included in the regression model, and their husbands’ ages are captured in the measurement of the spousal age gap. As shown below, the childbearing clusters overlap significantly in the range of ages at marriage, so only part of cluster membership and completed fertility is determined by the timing of entry into childbearing. Bras and Schumacher (2019) highlighted the spousal age gap as a proxy for egalitarian gender relations within marriage, which could affect a couple’s ability to cooperate on family planning decisions. Age homogamy was the norm for first marriages in these Derbyshire cohorts (65% of spouses had an age gap of less than three years) and higher fertility is only weakly associated with more unequal ages. There is a difference of less than a year between the average age gaps of High Fertility spouses and those of Low Fertility spouses.

In addition to occupation, the regressions control for the type of district a woman lived in before marriage. The registration subdistricts at each census are categorized according to their occupational structures and population densities into an environmental typology of urban, rural,

textile, and mining districts. Since the linked subsamples include records from at least two censuses, this variable is further defined by whether a woman is observed in the same type of environment in both censuses or experienced a changing environment by either moving to an urban district or staying in a district that was urbanizing. These movements could have happened before or after marriage, depending on age at first observation.

I define this variable in terms of changing environment rather than strictly focusing on migration both because of the changing classification of districts between censuses and because there are too few observations to separately analyse urban migrants and urbanizing districts. About 5% of women in each cohort experienced this shift to an urban environment through either migration or urban growth. The share of women from urban districts increased from 34% to 37% between the first and third cohorts, and an underrepresentation of rural districts in the first two cohorts compared to the 1881 census population masks the total degree of urbanization during this period (Table B.1).

Results

Clusters Although the clusters of childbearing sequences are partly defined by convergence on the number of children ever born, they also reveal associations with ages at marriage and birth intervals. On the whole, later marriage and fertility control within marriage were complementary behaviours. The High Fertility cluster, encompassing most sequences in the oldest cohort, implies the absence of deliberate birth control, with childbearing beginning soon after marriage and continuing at a steady pace as long as a woman remained fertile. The Low Fertility cluster illustrates parity-specific stopping, as women who had one or two births successfully prevented further pregnancies. The average length of a woman’s reproductive period in the High Fertility cluster was 19 years, compared with women in the Low Fertility group who completed their families in just over three years (Table 5.2). However, birth intervals also suggest that at least some of the women in the Low Fertility cluster were practicing birth control from the very beginning of their marriages.

The average duration from marriage to first birth among women in the Low Fertility cluster was more than three years, compared to an average interval of just over one year to first birth for women in the High Fertility cluster. Without detailed birth and marriage dates, it is impossible to know how many short first birth intervals resulted from pre-nuptial conceptions. Whether some of these women in the High Fertility group were more sexually active or less able or motivated to avoid pregnancy before marriage, pregnancy also could have hastened early marriages. Second birth intervals, for women who had at least two children, are also longer in the Low Fertility cluster: the

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| High Fertility | 22.7 | 44.1 | 268.5 | 6.5 | 22.1 | 40.9 | 234.3 | 6.8 | 21.9 | 213.9 | 6.0 |
