Source: As for Table 5.1.

median interval to second birth in this cluster was four years, compared with the 75% of women in the High Fertility cluster whose second birth occurred within three years.

The Moderate Fertility cluster, containing around a third of women in the older cohorts, is situated between these extremes in terms of age at marriage (24) and total number of births (4). In addition to lower ages at last birth than in the High Fertility cluster (Table 5.2), women in the

Moderate Fertility category also had longer inter-birth intervals, evident both in the intervals between low parities and particularly in lengthening final birth intervals, which could reflect contraceptive failure. The childbearing sequences in this cluster thus had 54% fewer births than those in the High Fertility cluster, over an average childbearing period that was only 42% shorter.

Almost 80% of women in the High Fertility group had their first birth within a year of marriage, compared with 65% in the Moderate Fertility and 50% in the Low Fertility clusters.

Age at marriage varies considerably within each cluster and there are differences in variability between clusters. Younger ages at marriage are not universal in the High Fertility cluster, which includes a few later marrying women who ‘caught up’ to others with a high number of births while, on the other hand, the Low Fertility cluster includes women who married and completed their childbearing relatively young. The High Fertility cluster has a wider range of total births, but the least variability in age at marriage. This distribution reflects the biological constraints of fertility – within a set fertile window, a high number of births can generally only be achieved by starting early – but also illustrates how the shift towards lower fertility was accompanied by increasing diversity in the onset of childbearing. Women who had only one or two births (in the Low Fertility cluster) had the largest interquartile ranges of age at marriage and age at first birth (Figure 5.4).

These patterns conflict with conventional conceptions of stopping behaviour as concentrating childbearing early in marriage, altering only age at last birth and not previous birth intervals (Knodel, 1987). While some variation in time to conception could be due to differences in fecundity, breastfeeding, or infant survival, these patterns could also be consistent with the contraceptive confidence hypothesis and a combination of spacing and stopping intended to reduce a woman’s duration at risk of pregnancy after her last birth (Ní Bhrolcháin, 1988). Increasing contraceptive confidence could explain slightly lower ages at marriage within the Low Fertility cluster over time, as women with greater knowledge of birth control were able to achieve a smaller family size even with longer exposure to risk.

The cluster analysis reveals the bundles of childbearing behaviours which were associated with the onset of the fertility transition in Derbyshire. Smaller family sizes were achieved both by stopping and spacing births, which were also associated with the starting effects of higher ages at marriage. While the Low Fertility cluster provides the clearest illustration of parity-dependent birth control, the Moderate Fertility category provides an alternate path towards fertility decline, whether this group represents an intermediate (or ineffective) application of parity-dependent birth control, a distinct, parity-independent ‘spacing’ regime, or a variation on ‘natural’ high fertility characterized by different breastfeeding practices or lower individual fecundity.

Figure 5.4 Boxplots of age at first birth by children ever born

Source: As for Figure 5.2.

Cohorts Compared with four births per woman by 1911 in the youngest cohort, women in the oldest cohort had an average of five births by the same ages in 1891, and the second cohort had averaged 4.7 births by 1901 (Table 5.1). After categorizing the 1871-6 sequences into the cluster typology based on age at marriage and achieved parity in 1911, the average births by cluster for each cohort are almost identical; the differences in total average parity are driven by a strong shift away from the High Fertility cluster in each subsequent cohort. The share of women with high fertility halved between the first and third cohorts, and although Moderate Fertility became the modal cluster, the Low Fertility trajectory saw a larger relative increase. If the third cohort were to follow the same subsequent fertility sequences by cluster as the second, the weighted average of their total births by age 50 would be around 4.5, a clear shift in fertility in only ten years from the average of 5.5 births to the 1861-6 cohort and a 30% decline in marital fertility compared to the 1851-6 cohort.

The stability of characteristics within clusters over time is striking. In revealing that the decline in total marital fertility between cohorts was driven by a compositional shift away from the High Fertility regime, the results demonstrate that this transition involved the simultaneous adoption of later marriage, greater birth spacing, and more intense stopping at low parities. It is also possible that total fertility of the younger cohort was reduced further, in terms of both additional children born after 1911 and the fertility of late-marrying women, which are not observed. Concomitant changes in the rates of marriage or childlessness as well as non-marital fertility, some of which will be explored in later chapters, may have also affected total fertility decline during this period.

Occupations Having established relationships among childbearing patterns within the life course, these trajectories can now be related to individual, couple, and community-level variables. As discussed above, fertility levels are expected to be strongly related to socioeconomic status and occupational group. Fertility cluster distributions by male occupational category generally follow the expected trends. The professions (including white collar and middle-class occupations) are the only category to end the period with the majority of couples in the Low Fertility cluster (Figure 5.5). Smaller but distinct occupational groups, such as female dressmakers and male workers in transport and shopkeeping, display similar trends from High to Low Fertility. These patterns agree with Garrett et al.’s estimates of changes in total marital fertility for male occupational groups between 1891 and 1911. However, my ability to include younger cohorts in the clustering and to examine birth spacing simultaneously with final or achieved parity suggests a slightly earlier onset of fertility decline than expected, particularly in the shift towards Moderate Fertility among miners and metalworkers.

Szreter suggested that the fertility transition did not take hold among some working-class groups until after the First World War (Szreter, 1996, Chapter 9) and Garrett et al. found an increase in total marital fertility among some groups between 1891 and 1901 before the pace of fertility decline accelerated between 1901 and 1911 (Garrett et al., 2001, Chapter 5)

In addition, dressmakers notably contained a consistently high proportion of women in the Moderate Fertility cluster, suggesting that this combination of birth spacing and moderately limited fertility had special appeal, perhaps related to conditions of home-based needlework. Fewer women with no recorded occupation were classified in the High Fertility cluster than some occupational groups; this pattern might suggest that a large proportion of these ‘unemployed’ women came from more affluent class backgrounds. Contrary to Szreter’s hypothesis for male servants and Matthys’ findings for Flanders, female domestic servants in Derbyshire were consistently the occupational category with the highest proportion of women in the High Fertility cluster, and their shift away

Figure 5.5 Fertility cluster distribution by occupational category and birth cohort

Source: As for Figure 5.2. 
Note: ‘aglab’ = farmers, agricultural labourers, and general labourers; ‘industry’ = miners and metal 
workers; ‘professions’ = all white-collar occupations. 

from this cluster between cohorts resulted in relatively larger increases in the proportion in the Moderate Fertility group. This category includes all kinds of service, excluding governesses and farm servants, and the predominance of rural as opposed to urban domestic service in Derbyshire is an important difference from the setting Matthys’ study.

Despite the expected role of the textile industry in the vanguard of fertility decline, both male and female textile workers in the early birth cohorts displayed relatively low proportions in the Low Fertility category. By the youngest cohort, however, textile occupations showed some of the strongest shifts towards Low Fertility, larger than the increase in the share in the Moderate Fertility cluster. Although female occupations before marriage are only observed for the second and third cohorts, male occupations for the first cohort show that trends were largely continuous, with some groups showing an accelerating shift towards low fertility in the second decade.

The different relative distributions and trends towards the Moderate and Low Fertility clusters by occupational groups suggest that multiple mechanisms were at work in this process. There are few observed quantitative differences between cohorts to explain these changes in the rate of fertility decline, except for a moderate degree of urbanization and associated reduction in the share of men employed in agriculture. This trend has implications for domestic servants, with a ten percentage-point decline in the share of rural domestic servants as part of a decline in the share employed in service overall. Women who worked in textiles, despite showing the strongest fertility changes between cohorts, were very similarly represented and geographically distributed in both.

Another source of compositional differences within occupations is the rate of intermarriage between occupational groups (Figure 5.6). Partner choice is both geographically and socially determined, as evidenced by the strong degree of assortative mating among textile workers (and, to a lesser extent, the professions). While around 65% of women from textile districts were employed in the textile industry before marriage, 80% of husbands in these districts who worked in textiles had wives from the same occupation. From a woman’s perspective, the sex ratio of employment meant that the number of eligible bachelor textile workers was relatively smaller, but still 35% of female textile workers in textile districts were married to men in the same sector, compared with a total male occupational share in those districts of 27%. In other districts, the degree of assortative mating was attenuated by the greater diversity of occupational groups.

Implicit in these fertility differences between occupations is the likelihood of women continuing in certain types of work after marriage. Some occupations, such as textile work, were associated with almost continuous employment for several years after marriage, providing direct economic incentive to delay or space early births (Garrett, 1990). Other types of work were more

easily combined with childbearing, including home-based needlework or dressmaking. Women could also contribute to family income by taking in lodgers (Anderson, 1999). In contrast, domestic service was a life-cycle stage that generally ended at marriage. The Derbyshire textile workers married slightly younger on average than domestic servants, but this is not a universal tendency when ages at marriage are broken down by spouse’s occupation (I explore these patterns further in Chapter 7).

Although the data lack detailed information about the duration of employment, a rough idea of married women’s labour force participation can be obtained from occupations observed in linked census records after marriage. In total, 13% of married women in Derbyshire between the ages of 15 and 45 had a recorded occupation in the 1881 census. For the 1861-6 birth cohort, just over 10% of women in the sample can be observed with an enumerated occupation after marriage in any of the linked censuses; the lower estimated rate of labour force participation, despite incorporating a wider perspective on married women’s work, might be related to the exclusion of childless wives and older married women from the sample. Labour force participation in 1881 was 11% among married women with at least one child living at home. However, the enumeration of women’s work

Figure 5.6 
Own occupation by spouse’s occupation, birth cohort 1861-1866 

Source: Linked I-CeM data; Derbyshire Registration County. 
Note: ‘aglab’ = farmers, agricultural labourers, and general labourers; ‘industry’ = miners and metal 
workers; ‘professions’ = all white-collar occupations. 

also changed over time, and after 1881 women were less likely to be recorded as occupied in the census enumerators’ books if they were assisting in their husband’s trade (You, 2020). By 1911, only 6.6% of married women of reproductive age recorded an occupation in the census.

Some occupations were clearly associated with a larger demand for married women’s work. A quarter of female textile workers in the 1861-6 cohort were recorded with occupations after marriage (20% remaining in the textile industry and 5% in other occupations). Among homogamous textile couples, this rate increases to 45% of textile workers’ wives. Married women from professional backgrounds can be observed with similar levels of labour force participation, and about a fifth of dressmakers are observed working after marriage. Women who had been domestic servants were the least likely to be recorded in any kind of work after marriage, with employment rates only just above those of women with no observed pre-marital occupation.

Married women’s work could have played a direct role in determining fertility levels; for example, textile workers in the second cohort who are observed working in the industry at any time after marriage had an average of 4.6 births compared to their peers who appear to have left employment and reported an average of 5.7 births. However, these factors have little power to explain change over time, as married women’s labour force participation rates were declining overall. There is also little information in the census data about the timing and duration of such employment in relation to the timing of marriages and births to establish a causal rather than selective effect for lower fertility among working women. Very few women are observed shifting sectors (for example, entering factory employment as an adult with no previous observation in textile work) and some occupations, like dressmaking or white-collar professions, involved apprenticeships or extended education. Work trajectories, and thence marriage and childbearing trajectories, were thus set into motion early in the life course.

Regressions Controlling for environment and age at marriage, the main variable of interest is the combination of bride and groom occupational categories. Compared to couples consisting of a former domestic servant married to a man in agriculture or heavy industry, several occupational combinations are estimated to have significantly higher odds of following Moderate or Low Fertility patterns rather than being in the High Fertility cluster. In both cohorts, couples from among the other occupational groups had almost three times higher odds than the reference category of being in the Low Fertility cluster. Although all occupational categories and types of places participated in the shift away from High Fertility, many differences persisted or strengthened in the second cohort.

If one spouse’s occupational group tended towards low fertility and the other tended towards high, did one dominate? Or were individual tendencies in fact defined by this context of assortative mating and geographies of work? Occupational interactions can be considered from multiple perspectives. Focusing on differences within a bride’s occupational category, women in the 1861-6 cohort who had worked in the textile industry before marriage were significantly more likely than the reference couples to have moderate or low fertility only if they married men in the textile industry or other occupations (Table 5.3).

This finding shows that the effects of women’s labour force participation were not straightforward, because women with husbands in the agriculture-industry category are observed working in the textile industry after marriage more often than those married to men with other occupations, but they were not more likely to follow a lower fertility trajectory. In the negotiation between male and female backgrounds, it was often – but not always – the male occupation which dominated. The textile and mining/agriculture occupational alliance accounts for around 45% of female textile workers in the 1861-6 cohort, explaining the unexpectedly high initial proportion of female textile workers in the High Fertility cluster. However, the impact of female occupations can be seen in differences within male occupational groups: male textile workers who married women from outside the textile industry did not have significantly higher odds of moderate or low fertility in the older cohort, and women from other occupational categories were more likely to follow a moderate fertility path even when married to miners and farmers.

An alternative regression model controlling for male and female occupations separately is presented in Appendix B (Table B.2). The compositional effects of assortative mating on fertility within occupational categories clearly explain why the alternate model does not find a significant difference in the odds of Low Fertility between domestic servants or miners and textile workers in the 1861-6 cohort. Without considering these marital dynamics, one might come to a false conclusion that the low fertility of textile workers only emerged between the 1861-6 and 1871-6 cohorts, and search for explanations in changing wage levels or patterns of married women’s work. The couple-level model demonstrates that explanations for textile operatives’ fertility should be considered both in terms of the assortative mating among textile workers and in the context of the onset of fertility decline among miners and agricultural workers.

In the 1871-6 birth cohort, the rapid shift of textile workers away from the High Fertility cluster is reflected in significantly higher odds of experiencing a Moderate or Low Fertility trajectory for all women who are observed working in the textile industry, regardless of spouse’s occupation. For the 42% of textile workers in this cohort who were married to men in the agriculture-industry group, a significant difference is only found for the odds of Moderate Fertility. Men in the textile

industry who married women in other occupational categories now had twice the odds as their ‘traditionalist’ peers to follow a Low Fertility trajectory but were not significantly more likely to be in the Moderate Fertility category. These changes between cohorts illustrate a catch-up effect as previously high fertility groups shifted into the Moderate Fertility cluster.

Controlling for occupation, couples who lived in textile districts had 50-60% higher odds than their peers in rural districts to be classified in the Low Fertility cluster. Couples in urban areas had even higher odds of being in either the Moderate or Low Fertility groups. In the 1861-6 cohort, women who experienced a change in environment from rural or mining to urban – including migrants and women in urbanizing districts – had significantly higher odds of being in the Moderate or Low fertility cluster than women who stayed in rural environments. This migration or contextual effect is blunted in the results for the 1871-6 cohort, either because the pace of urbanization was slowing or because of the onset of fertility decline in the rural hinterlands. Convergence on lower fertility is reflected in the smaller, though still significant, odds ratios for Moderate and Low Fertility for couples who stayed in urban areas in the younger cohort.

Discussion 
Together, the fertility sequences, cluster analysis, and regression models reveal how marriage and 
childbearing patterns accumulated throughout the life course and how these behaviours were 
associated with individual, household, and community contexts. The new visibility of women’s 
occupations demonstrates the importance of understanding how interactions between groups, such 
as the rates of intermarriage between textile workers and miners, have implications for variations 
within groups, including the illusion of high textile fertility. The identification of domestic service, 
particularly in more rural settings, as a correlate of high fertility has implications for aggregate 
trends of declining fertility which were concurrent with urbanization and a decline in the share of 
women’s employment in domestic service. The 1901 and 1911 censuses reveal that these shifts 
continued in subsequent cohorts. 

The reconstructed birth histories and sequence analysis reveal new temporal dimensions to fertility decline. Rather than only seeing alterations to childbearing in the later years of marriage, the fertility transition in Derbyshire also involved increasing ages at marriage and lengthening birth intervals among younger mothers. These patterns agree with findings using similar birth data for other countries (Dribe et al., 2017) and with inferences from ASMFRs for other locations within England and Wales (Garrett et al., 2001). This chapter has focused on entire fertility trajectories more than individual intervals, and these insights provide context for further investigation of the relationships between women’s work and birth spacing using both the linked data and other primary sources like employment records.

These results are consistent with several explanations for the ultimate causes of fertility decline. Employment interactions between spouses and within the life course, combined with other economic factors like the rate of child labour in different industries, could have affected fertility incentives within the family economy. High male wages in mining and the family labour needed to run a farm help explain the large families of miners and agricultural workers. However, the ability of children to earn wages in textile factories from the age of ten does not seem to have had a similar impact on their parent’s childbearing calculations.

These different labour market factors do not explain, however, why domestic servants, miners, textile workers, and urbanites all displayed marked shifts away from a High Fertility mode during the same period. The data do not reveal substantial compositional shifts within these groups – a changing geographic distribution or rate of inter-marriage – that can be used to identify the catalyst of fertility decline. Married women’s work, although important for describing the low fertility of textile-textile couples, is not sufficient for explaining why textile-industry couples, with higher rates of married women’s labour force participation, were not as likely to have low fertility as

textile-other couples. This is a drawback of the couple-level model, as some differences between occupations are blurred by the need to combine small groups; occupational differences in stopping and spacing are drawn out in more detail in the next chapter.

Although the results do not provide evidence for vertical diffusion of birth controlling behaviours, such as from employers to servants, they are consistent with the expected effects of lateral diffusion of attitudes towards reproduction and family size via factory employment and urban society. The interacted effects of male and female occupations suggest that power dynamics and bargaining processes within the household – regarding the burdens of childrearing, contributions towards the family wage, or shared participation in social networks – influenced couples’ abilities to act on shared or competing fertility preferences (Agarwal, 1997; Fisher, 2000; Testa et al., 2014).

Particularly for the low fertility typology, which the sequences reveal included a significant proportion of a married woman’s life during which pregnancy must have been actively prevented, the fertility transition implies a significant degree of cooperation between spouses, which could be encouraged or impeded by their relative positions within the family defined by both social norms and labour markets. This interpretation of couple-level interactions draws on the arguments of Folbre (1983), Greenhalgh (1995) and others calling for more explicit theoretical consideration of gender and the ways in which unequal distribution of power within marriages affected fertility limitation (Pooley, 2013; Seccombe, 1990). In the following chapters, I zoom in from the life course to the event to examine factors associated with starting, stopping, and spacing individually and to explore the conditions of work for men, women, and children in a cotton spinning mill.

CHAPTER 6: WHETHER AND WHEN: SPLIT-POPULATION CURE MODELS OF WOMEN’S WORK AND PARITY PROGRESSION

Introduction The ‘stopping vs spacing’ question is a recurrent focus for both theoretical and methodological debates in the field of historical demography. We associate parity-dependent fertility limitation, or the tendency of couples to stop childbearing when they have reached a desired number of births, with the emergence of new attitudes towards reproduction during the Demographic Transition in nineteenth-century Europe. Another way in which family size can be reduced is through lengthening the intervals between each pregnancy to reduce the number occurring within the period between marriage and menopause. This kind of parity-independent birth spacing might have been easier to achieve than parity-dependent stopping before the advent of modern contraception, but it is also generally harder to observe and measure in historical data. Longer intervals between births may be unintentional, the result either of miscarriage or stillbirth or of unsuccessful attempts to stop childbearing. Common demographic measures like age-specific fertility rates (ASFRs) reveal declining fertility among women in older age groups, and this pattern has been interpreted as evidence of parity-dependent family limitation, but ASFRs offer no direct information about changes to birth intervals. Assessment of the role of birth spacing in the fertility transition has been limited by the scarcity of suitable population microdata, and in particular of individual fertility histories.

Research on birth spacing and stopping in historical populations has run along three lines: first asking whether variations in birth intervals before the onset of the fertility transition were the result of deliberate spacing behaviours, proving that fertility was ‘within the calculus of conscious choice’ (Bengtsson & Dribe, 2006; Cilliers et al., 2024; Coale, 1973; Dribe & Scalone, 2010); secondly, whether changes to birth spacing played an important role in reducing completed family size during the fertility transition (Anderton & Bean, 1985; Knodel, 1987); and thirdly, what patterns of birth spacing as well as stopping can reveal about couples’ fertility intentions and their determinants (Bengtsson & Dribe, 2014; Reher et al., 2017). The first two questions about the long-run history and importance of deliberate birth spacing have been evaluated using birth histories reconstructed from population registers or other sources and produced mixed results.

Although there are multiple sources of evidence for the postponement of births during economic crises such as harvest failures or trade depressions, the role of conscious choice in temporarily depressing birth rates must be balanced against the unconscious or unintended effects of stress, migration, or illness on coital frequency and the chances of conception. Deliberate postponement of births in response to changing external pressures is a distinct behaviour from other

forms of birth spacing which are tied to the age of a woman’s youngest child and the desired interval before another birth (Timæus & Moultrie, 2008). Nevertheless, Timæus and Moultrie have argued that lengthy or indefinite postponement of births has played an important role in declining fertility in some African populations, pushing against a view that smaller families are always achieved as the result of a new rationality in family planning. Several studies have found evidence for increased birth spacing during the Demographic Transition, but they have generally concluded that longer birth intervals played a relatively small role in total fertility decline compared with the number of couples stopping childbearing at smaller family sizes (Bengtsson & Dribe, 2014; Reher & Sanz‐Gimeno, 2007).

The other question, about what patterns of birth spacing and stopping can reveal about reproductive strategies and intentions, has received growing attention with the advent of new data and methods in historical demography (Alter, 2019). Event history analysis using longitudinal population microdata reveals how couples managed their fertility through sequential decision making in response to internal household composition as well as external economic and social pressures. The increasing use of models which simultaneously analyse birth intervals and parity progression overcomes some of the limitations of other event history techniques. These models can show, for example, how couples responded to the effects of a child death or twin birth on their family size (Alter & Hacker, 2024; Gortfelder & Puur, 2020). Adjustments to spacing but not stopping might reflect concern for maternal health or temporary demands on family resources; differences in the likelihood of stopping at a given parity indicate sustained changes in desired family size and composition.

The following chapters use split-population models, also known as cure models, to analyse the likelihood and timing of several transitions across the life course. In this chapter, I analyse parity specific differences in birth spacing and stopping between both male and female occupational groups in late nineteenth-century Derbyshire. I also use the split-population model to analyse the timing of first births after marriage and the likelihood of remaining childless, elements which have not been included in most previous papers using the cure model in historical demography. I find that women’s work impacted fertility through several pathways, including through age at marriage and longer spacing of births early in marriage, reflecting the likelihood of continuing in work in certain industries, and through occupational and spousal differences in the likelihood of stopping childbearing to achieve a smaller family size.

In the next chapters, I model the transition from work to marriage, first for women across the county and then for a sample from the district of Belper, which contained a large cotton spinning mill. I augment the linked census data with archival sources from the Strutt mills, which reveal the impact of the textile industry in Belper beyond what can be seen in the census. The factory wage and

workforce registers illustrate patterns of work in the textile industry by age, sex, and marital status. I particularly focus on the role of child labour in the textile industry in contributing to the distinct demographic regimes of textile districts.

Background 
Figure 6.1 shows the average numbers of children ever born and average numbers of children 
surviving in 1911 for selected occupational groups in Derbyshire. Different levels of achieved and net 
fertility appear within the first few years of marriage, suggesting that couples had their first and 
second births at different rates. As noted in Chapter 1, previous research on the fertility transition in 
England and Wales has only obliquely addressed the role of spacing, either by calculating indirect 
fertility measures from cross-sectional observations or by comparing the number of children ever 
born as reported in the 1911 census (Ní Bhrolcháin, 1987; Szreter, 1996). Reconstructing ASFRs from 
observations of recent net fertility in the nineteenth century censuses, Garrett et al. (2001) inferred 
simultaneous changes to birth spacing along with stopping which resulted in flatter fertility curves. 
Jaadla et al. (2020) interpreted consistently lower recent net fertility among long-distance migrants 
across the nineteenth century as the result of postponed marriage and childbearing rather than 
early adoption of family limitation. Crafts (1989) argued that an ‘excess of childlessness’ and lower 
fertility for marriages of short duration in areas with higher women’s labour force participation in 
1911 reflected substantial spacing of first and second births. Garrett’s study of the worsted 
manufacturing town of Keighley followed women’s work and fertility over multiple censuses but was 
not able to distinguish fully between the contributions of spacing, stopping, and infant mortality to 
the low fertility of textile families (Garrett, 1990). 

My linked data offer a new opportunity to investigate differences in birth intervals as well as parity progression ratios during the fertility transition, and split-population cure models offer a new tool to do so. Cure models are a relatively new method in historical demography. As the name implies, cure models have roots in biomedical research and are designed to estimate the effects of a treatment on the proportion of the population that is ‘cured’ (do not experience the event of interest) as well as the time to event for ‘uncured’ subjects. Since this approach involves dividing the population by cured status and estimating separate equations for those who do and do not experience the event of interest, the models are also known as split-population models or mover stayer models in the social sciences where the concept of a ‘cured’ fraction is less relevant. Although split-population cure models were adopted early by some branches of the social sciences because of their capacity for analysing both the likelihood of an event and its timing, they have only recently been implemented in historical demographic research (Alter et al., 2007; Yamaguchi & Ferguson, 1995).

The split-population model has several advantages over other methods in event history analysis. The Cox proportional hazards model has been used widely to estimate changes in the risk of experiencing an event such as marriage, birth, or death. However, the Cox regression estimates the transition rate or hazard of experiencing an event rather than directly modelling the time to event, so it identifies variables which are associated with an earlier or later transition but does not model absolute differences in timing. Cox regressions also assume that all subjects eventually experience the event of interest, so that intervals which are censored or left open because the event does not occur during the period of observation are included in the calculation of time spent at risk and may skew the estimated transition rate downwards. If one group experiences an event like childbirth at a faster rate than another but ultimately contains a higher proportion of women who never experience the event, then the hazard functions of these two groups would not be proportional over time, violating an assumption of the Cox model.

The split-population model allows variables to have differing effects on stopping and spacing by first estimating the proportion of women who do not have another birth before analysing birth intervals among the ‘at risk’ population. In a mixture cure model, censored intervals are allowed to

Figure 6.1 
Children ever born and children surviving by husband's occupation in 1911 

Source: Linked I-CeM data; Derbyshire Registration County, married women observed in the 1911 census with husbands who married between the ages of 20 and 34, 3-year moving average. Note: ‘industry’ = miners and metal workers; ‘professions’ = all white-collar occupations.

be a mixture of ‘cured’ and ‘uncured’ cases; this framework recognizes that events may occur after the end of observation, particularly for intervals which were censored at shorter durations. The model deals with censored intervals by estimating whether they are likely to be among the ‘at risk’ population at a given time. With sufficient follow-up time, long open intervals may be confidently classified as ‘cured’, while shorter intervals for younger women which are predicted to be ‘uncured’ at that point in time are allowed to contribute to time at risk for the second timing sub-model. This approach addresses the biases produced by analysing only closed intervals when censoring disproportionately affects observation of longer intervals.

The ability to deal with censored intervals makes the split-population model especially advantageous for use with data based on retrospective surveys or reconstructed from linked records, in which timelines are truncated by unlinked records or by the survey date. However, estimates may still be biased if the length of censored intervals differs substantially between groups. For example, individuals who are younger at the survey date may be predicted to be more likely to experience the event of interest, whether marriage or childbirth, as an artifact of the fact that the events observed for the group necessarily occurred at younger ages, while the censored open intervals are also shorter than those of older cohorts. The desire to maintain a large sample size has to be balanced by careful selection and controls for age and time at risk, as described below.

Alter and colleagues (2007) first proposed using the cure model for research on the fertility transition because of its ability to detect changes in birth stopping and spacing more precisely than the prevailing event history methods. Using detailed birth histories obtained from population registers and reconstitutions, studies using split-population models have analysed the propensity to have another birth in response to a child death or to achieve a desired sex composition of offspring (Gortfelder & Puur, 2020) as well as the likelihood of stopping or spacing births following the arrival of twins (Alter & Hacker, 2024). These studies find that couples continuously adjusted their birth control strategy in response to household circumstances, and that groups who were already limiting their fertility were more responsive to exogenous shocks than those who were not. Cilliers and colleagues modelled birth postponement separately from spacing and stopping as a change in the variability of birth intervals in the context of wealth shocks to white settler families during the abolition of slavery in the Cape Colony (Cilliers et al., 2024; Cilliers & Mariotti, 2021). These papers focusing on responses to defined events and circumstances leverage the model’s ability to include time-varying covariates in either or both the likelihood and timing sub-models.

Split-population models have also been used to study more general, time-invariant characteristics associated with birth intervals and parity progression over time. Gauvreau et al. (2025) used cure models to show the demographic convergence of second-generation French-

Canadian immigrants with the birth intervals and family sizes of American women of native-born parents. Redivo et al. (2024) examined social-class differences in spacing and stopping over the long run, including pre- and post-transition cohorts, finding that spacing played an important, though subsidiary and somewhat lagged, role in fertility decline in Sweden. The results of parity-specific models often find that differences in stopping and spacing are not statistically significant in the transitions beyond third or fourth births; past this point, couples practicing parity-dependent birth control drop out of the sample and the remaining women are selected for higher completed fertility.

Thus far, none of the literature in historical demography using split-population models has examined the transition to first birth. The timing of first births is affected by different factors (notably the timing of marriage and likelihood of pre-nuptial pregnancy) than those affecting higher order births, such as the duration of breastfeeding and post-partum insusceptibility. It is common to model first births separately for this reason. Redivo et al. (2024) replicated analysis from Bengtsson and Dribe (2014) which emphasized increasing intervals from marriage to first birth during the fertility transition in Sweden, using Cox regressions rather than the cure model. More than 95% of couples marrying around the median age in nineteenth-century Derbyshire had at least one birth; the hazard model thus does not suffer as much from the confusion of long intervals with censored observations as it does for subsequent births. However, distinguishing between factors affecting first birth timing and the likelihood of remaining childless are important for illustrating the role of birth spacing independently from parity-dependent fertility control. Longer durations to first birth are unlikely to be due to unwanted conceptions arising from failed attempts to stop, as may be the case at higher parities.

On the other hand, varying intervals from marriage to first birth between different groups of women will also reflect differences in the number of prenuptial conceptions, which are difficult or impossible to identify in the linked census data. Groups which are associated with a slower progression to first birth may also therefore be associated with lower rates of bridal pregnancy. Both factors reflect different attitudes towards and degrees of control over sexual activity, whether before or after marriage. Women who were pregnant at marriage, and who may also have been selected into younger marriage by higher fecundity, were found to have higher subsequent fertility within marriage in parish reconstitution studies (Wrigley et al., 1997). As fecundity decreases with age, older-marrying women are expected to be more likely to remain childless or to stop childbearing after fewer births. Even among younger women, childlessness could result from indefinite postponement of a desired first birth, from infection-acquired infertility, or from a deliberate rejection of normative values of family life. These pathways have very different

implications for our understanding of the causes of fertility decline. Without qualitative evidence, conclusions about the mechanisms of change will remain speculative.

Some women might have been more motivated than others to remain childless or delay the birth of their first child. Textile workers were more likely than other women to continue working after marriage and might do so indefinitely as long as they remained childless. Around 25% of female textile workers over age 20 in 1911 in Derbyshire were married, compared with 14% of dressmakers and 17% of servants. Among married women employed in textiles, over 60% had one or fewer surviving children in 1911: 33% had no children born to their current marriage, another 4% had no surviving children, and 26% had only one surviving child. Combining work and childcare was easier for dressmakers, reflected in the fact that half of married dressmakers in 1911 reported more than one child ever born. These patterns agree with Garrett’s findings for Keighley, which showed that married textile workers generally left work after the birth of their second child (Garrett, 1990). In the linked sample for Derbyshire, 20% of female textile workers are observed still working in the industry after marriage. This figure underestimates the continuity of married women’s work in textiles since these observations are limited to decadal intervals.

I continue this exploration of women’s labour force participation by industry and parity below. Observations of women’s occupations before marriage in the linked data allow me to assess whether women who worked in the textile industry were more likely than others to stop or space births early in marriage, rather than lower fertility among working women being primarily a selection effect. In the previous chapter, sequence analysis revealed that female textile workers, and other female occupational groups, were increasingly likely to follow childbearing trajectories characterised by a mixture of stopping and spacing. Split-population models estimate the likelihood of stopping and spacing at each parity separately, offering greater insight into the mechanisms linking occupation and fertility via economic, social, or cultural pathways.

Data and variables Having considered the reconstructed birth histories as holistic fertility trajectories in the last chapter, this chapter focuses on each birth individually. I restrict the sample to women who are linked to pre marital census records as single women via indexes of marriage registers. Couples who married before 1881 or remarried are thus excluded. Occupational groups for both women (using pre-marital census observations) and their husbands are defined in the same way as before.

The split-population model is implemented using an R package called spduration and specifications can be found in Beger et al. (2017). Unlike traditional cure models which model the likelihood that an event will not occur, this implementation of the split-population model predicts

the likelihood of event occurrence: that is, whether an individual is more or less likely to have a(nother) birth, even if the birth is not observed within a censored interval. The time-to-event sub model is in accelerated failure time format; the dependent variable is the logged time to event (birth interval). In the regression results, coefficients are exponentiated, so the likelihood sub-model shows the odds ratios of having another birth associated with each level of a variable (values greater than one indicate higher parity progression ratios and less family limitation) and the timing sub-model shows the percent difference in time to next birth (values greater than one indicate longer birth intervals and greater family limitation).

I define parity-specific split-population models for each birth, starting with the transition from marriage to first birth and ending with the transition from seventh to eighth birth. Women’s fertility timelines are broken into spells of years which start with a birth (or with marriage, for the transition to the first birth) and last until there is another birth or until the spell is censored. I define a spell as censored when it has been fifteen years since a woman’s last birth, when a woman reaches age 50, or at the end of observation in 1911. This definition excludes a very small number of births which occurred after an interval of more than fifteen years, a duration which might reflect unreported infant and child deaths, miscarriages, or stillbirths. Table 6.1 summarizes the number of women and the person years spent at risk for every transition from marriage (parity 0) to the eighth birth (parity 7), broken down by whether the spell ends in an event or is censored. The proportion of intervals ending with a birth which has been imputed – either because the child had died or left the household before observation – is fairly consistent across parities. A greater proportion of intervals are censored at increasing parities, both by maternal age and by the end of observation.

Birth intervals are estimated based on the reported and imputed ages of children in years; marital durations are also reported in completed years in the 1911 census. Intervals of zero years are thus possible and are not uncommon in the case of first births occurring within a year of marriage. For higher order births, intervals of zero years present a problem in the case of twins. About 1,800 of the reconstructed birth histories include two or more children with the same birth year, all of which are estimated from observed ages in the census since the imputation process did not reconstruct multiple births. It is obviously not informative to analyse the intervals between births which resulted from the same pregnancy; however, these observations could also result from short intervals between separate pregnancies or from age reporting errors in the census. I do not adjust the estimated parities and birth orders for these mothers by treating all children with the same birthyears as twins (as done by Alter & Hacker, 2024), but I do exclude the intervals between possible twin births from the models for that parity. This exclusion may inflate the estimated average birth intervals but affects less than 4,000 births in total.

After the second birth, the models include the length of a woman’s first interbirth interval to control for potential differences in fecundity. Despite the importance of infant mortality, both in shortening birth intervals by interrupting breastfeeding and in its effect on net fertility, it cannot be included as a control variable in the models. The mortality status of children was reported in 1911, but not all deaths can be assumed to have occurred before a woman’s next conception. In addition, birth dates for deceased children which have been imputed have a large degree of randomness in their assigned birth order. Likewise, a replacement effect of the loss of one child on increasing the likelihood of having another birth cannot be directly evaluated with these data.

The selected samples have slightly different compositions in each parity model, both because of the exclusion of women who stopped childbearing from the models for higher-order births and because of the need to select age cohorts which have a sufficient number of person years at risk for each transition. The models control for age at marriage and for age at last birth for parities over one, in five-year age groups. Five-year birth cohorts are included to control for both changes in childbearing patterns over time and for exposure to risk and age at censoring. In the models for higher-order births, there are fewer young women who have achieved a given parity, contributing to more censored intervals. Thus, I exclude cohorts under thirty years old in 1911 from models of the

transition to fourth and subsequent births. Variable distributions and summary statistics for each parity-specific model are presented in Table C.1.

The data have also been filtered to remove observations which may be affected by errors in enumeration, record linking, or imputation. This filtering ensures that the implied age at marriage for both bride and groom is at least fifteen and that the age difference between spouses is no more than 15 years. As noted in previous chapters, the fertility survey was directed to children born to current marriages, so it (at least in theory) excludes illegitimate fertility and previous fertility to widowed spouses who remarried. The number of reported births is thus an underestimate of total births to women in this period. This exclusion can affect the estimation of all birth orders but is particularly visible among women who reported no children ever born in 1911 despite being observed with co-resident children in the household, who may be own children from a previous marriage or illegitimate offspring.

In the birth imputation process, women who were observed with more children (co-resident or linked) than reported ever born have already been excluded. For the first birth model, I exclude women who completed the fertility survey in 1911 by reporting no children born but are observed with co-resident offspring in that or any linked census entry. Women with children whose reported ages imply a maternal age at birth younger than fifteen or a birth more than five years before their marriage are also excluded. Births before marriage are not included in the first birth model (since they would result in negative intervals) but women whose timelines include a pre-marital birth are included in higher order birth models.

Results

First births Figure 6.2 illustrates Kaplan-Meier survival curves of the time between marriage and first birth by women’s ages at marriage. The slope of the curve shows the speed of transition – approximately 50% of women who married between the ages of 20 and 24 had their first birth within a year of marriage. The curves level out after four years, reaching a steady plateau which represents the proportion of women who did not give birth and remained childless after ten years of marriage. In the second panel, this subset of childless women is removed, and the curve focuses on the rate of transition for women who did have at least one birth. The slopes of these curves are steeper and reveal that while women who married at older ages, between 30 and 34, were more likely to remain childless, those that did have at least one child gave birth slightly more quickly after marriage than women who married younger, as the survival curve dips below that of other age groups. These figures visually represent the simultaneous processes analysed by the split-population model.

Births which occurred within the first year of marriage include an unknown number of pre nuptial conceptions, given that neither exact marriage nor birth dates are available. Family reconstitutions based on parish records found variable but generally high rates of bridal pregnancy, representing around of quarter of first births across the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, as estimated from the number of births occurring within eight months of marriage (Wrigley et al., 1997). Combined with illegitimate births (most of which were first births), these studies estimated that up to half of all first births in England were conceived outside of marriage in the early nineteenth century (Reay, 1994). Pregnancy was likely to hasten couples to marriage, so this factor may disproportionately affect estimates of the timing of first births among younger married women, although ‘shotgun weddings’ occurred among brides of all ages.

Complete model estimates for the transition to first and subsequent births are presented in Table C.2. Coefficients greater than one in the likelihood sub-model indicate that a group is more likely have a first birth (and thus are less likely to remain childless); coefficients greater than one in the timing sub-model indicate that a group has a longer interval between marriage and first birth. Controls for the age of the mother at the opening of the interval show consistent effects on likelihood and timing across parities. Older women are less likely to have a(nother) birth and those

Figure 6.2 Kaplan-Meier survival curves from marriage to first birth by age at marriage

Source: Linked I-CeM data; Derbyshire Registration County, reconstructed birth histories for married women observed in the 1911 census.

that do have longer birth intervals, although these differences are not always significant. The relationship between age at marriage and the timing of first births may be pulled in opposite directions by both pre- and post-marital behaviours: on the one hand, older brides may have been slower to conceive a desired child or have already avoided a pre-nuptial conception through effective birth control. On the other hand, older-marrying couples may have been less likely to deliberately delay first births, either because they had substituted late marriage for fertility control within marriage or because they desired a large family and were making up for lost time.

Coefficients for the main variables of interest – husband’s occupation and wife’s occupation before marriage – from the model of first births are plotted in Figure 6.3. Controlling for age at marriage, only two female occupational groups were significantly less likely than domestic servants to have at least one birth. Firstly, women with no observed pre-marital occupation follow diverging paths in the two sub-models: they were more likely than women who had been domestic servants to remain childless, but those who did have at least one child gave birth more quickly after marriage. Secondly, women who were working in the textile industry before marriage had about 35% lower odds of having had at least one birth by 1911. Converted to a relative risk, this means that textile workers were 2% more likely than domestic servants to remain childless. Textile workers, as well as women who worked as dressmakers or in white-collar occupations before marriage, also had significantly longer intervals between marriage and their first birth. Since these data measure the prospective effects of women’s work on later fertility rather than the retrospective associations between achieved parity and work at the time of the census, these models provide greater evidence for a causal relationship between textile work and fertility limitation rather than a process of selection removing fertile women from the workforce after marriage.

The model also finds significant differences in the timing of first births by husbands’ occupational groups. Women whose husbands were in white-collar and professional occupations stand out with birth intervals 13% longer than those with husbands in mining and heavy industry. The middle and upper classes were likely placing more restraint on pre-nuptial conception as well as on marital sex. However, women married to professional husbands were also the only group estimated to be significantly more likely than women married to miners to have at least one birth when controlling for age at marriage. These estimates are based on small numbers of couples, as men in white-collar professions made up approximately 6% of the workforce. Rates of childlessness within marriage also do not account for the larger proportion of women from middle-class backgrounds who remained single, as shown in the next chapter. However, these results suggest that the longer intervals to first birth among professional couples are not the artifact of lengthy postponement of births, which would also contribute to higher rates of childlessness. If these groups

were the most likely to be planning their fertility, they seem to have had clear desires for one or two children, as shown below (see also Van Bavel et al., 2018). These findings are robust to restricting the sample to first births occurring within the fifteen years before the census (Table C.4). In the restricted sample, women who were more recently married are less likely to have misstated their marital duration (for example by rounding it to the age of the eldest child, which would bias birth intervals downwards) and fewer births have had to be imputed.

The model estimates that, controlling for other factors, women from urban districts took 2% longer to have their first birth after marriage than women from mining districts but otherwise finds no significant differences in childlessness or the timing of first births between types of districts (Table C.2). These results suggest that individual factors determined fertility patterns early in marriage more than contextual factors, through behaviours either encouraging pre-nuptial pregnancy or preventing first births. Communities likely exerted control over courtship and the entry to marriage which would affect the rate of pre-nuptial conceptions and the spread of birth control,

Figure 6.3 
Split-population model coefficients for first births by husband’s occupation and wife’s occupation 
before marriage

but differences at the subdistrict level do not explain variation in first births independently from the effects of age at marriage and individual occupation. Several of these patterns are altered or reversed in the results of the split-population models of higher parities.

Parity progression 
Full results from each parity-specific model are in Table C.2 and Table C.3. For ease of comparison 
and presentation, I have used the model coefficients to calculate predicted parity progression ratios 
(PPRs) and median birth intervals which are summarised in Table 6.2 (the equations for these figures 
are also provided in Appendix C). Since both of these measures are affected by a woman’s age at the 
beginning of the interval, which is controlled for in the models, I follow Alter and Hacker (2024) and 
calculate the predicted values for each age category and take a weighted average, using the age 
distribution of the reference category of domestic servants as constant weights. Since these 
estimates hold the values of all other variables constant rather than averaging the effects over 
observed variable distributions, the results are not intended as approximations of observed PPRs or 
birth intervals. For example, the predicted parity progression ratios for the transition to first birth 
are higher than would be expected from family reconstitutions for the eighteenth century, in which 
about 4% of women marrying in their early twenties remained childless (Wrigley et al., 1997). 
Nevertheless, the predicted values provide a more straightforward illustration of the differences in 
childbearing patterns between groups. 

For women who had at least one birth, progression to further births could have been influenced by both voluntary and involuntary (or unconscious) factors, including birth trauma, breastfeeding duration or interruption due to infant death, desires to delay pregnancy, or desires to stop childbearing. Although the model finds significant differences in the relative risks of first birth by age and occupation, absolute differences in childlessness are limited, as almost 95% of all married women in the sample had at least one birth before 1911. More substantial differences emerge in the progression to second and higher births, with greater implications for the completed fertility of these groups. The predicted parity progression ratios and median birth intervals for a reference individual (a domestic servant married to a miner, based on model estimates for the intercepts) are plotted in Figure 6.4. Even among the groups least likely to be intensely limiting their fertility, predicted birth intervals increase, and parity progression ratios decrease, with parity (and by extension with age).

Controlling for age at marriage and age at previous birth, the models find persistent, significant differences in the timing and likelihood of higher order births between both male and female occupational groups compared to the reference groups of miners and domestic servants (Table 6.2). Among couples with at least one birth, groups that are found to have had longer

durations to a second birth are generally also predicted to have had a higher likelihood of stopping after one birth. The exception is women with husbands who were textile workers, who had significantly longer durations to second birth than the wives of miners but were not more likely to stop at one birth. Women who worked in the textile industry before marriage, regardless of their husbands’ occupations, had longer intervals to second birth than former domestic servants and had about 20% lower odds of having a second birth; women in the professional classes were even less likely to have more than one child.

The occupations of husbands had consistent effects on birth intervals up to the fourth birth, while women’s occupations before marriage had fewer independent effects on birth spacing after the second birth. Female textile workers stand out particularly for having longer birth intervals across multiple parities, even though they are not predicted to have significantly lower parity progression ratios at higher order births. These results further reinforce the conclusion that the lower marital fertility among textile workers observed at the aggregate level was not simply a case of reverse causality. In addition, if poor maternal care or limited breastfeeding among working mothers contributed to higher infant mortality among actively employed textile workers, as was
