suggested by contemporary medical officers of health, this would be expected to result in shorter birth intervals in the absence of deliberate birth spacing (Garrett & Reid, 1994).

Women who worked in the textile industry before marriage did report more children dead relative to total children born than some other occupational groups in 1911, but this pattern was contingent on husband’s occupation and location of residence in addition to a woman’s working status. Women who had been domestic servants before marrying miners also reported high infant and child mortality, which could be due to their husband’s occupation or to their residence in mining areas. Longer birth intervals among the professional classes could reflect the fact that these couples reported fewer child deaths before 1911 (as shown in Figure 6.1); however, the weakening of this pattern at higher order births suggests that differential infant survival was not the primary driver of different birth intervals. Nevertheless, these factors do have important consequences for completed family size: the combination of higher child mortality and a higher incidence of birth spacing and stopping among textile workers accentuated the gap between their net fertility and that of domestic

servants.

The fading differences in stopping and spacing by female occupational groups at higher parities are not surprising, since the models measure a woman’s occupation before marriage, closest in time to her first and second births. The previous chapter demonstrated, however, that the combined effects of these differences across parities contributed to significant divergence in childbearing trajectories between occupational groups across the life course. Women’s work could be affecting childbearing in two ways. First, women’s wages provided direct incentives to continue working, which would be less possible with growing childcare burdens and would provide strong motivations to delay births or stop after one or two. Women with two or more young children were less likely to still be working outside the home, and incentives to space or stop additional births would be reoriented towards the impact of husband’s employment on the family economy. Alternatively, women’s occupations could impact fertility by connecting them to social networks via the workplace which circulated attitudes about birth control and family size, continuing to affect childbearing behaviours even when a woman was no longer working. These contextual effects are harder to isolate because they are also correlated with spouse and district characteristics.

Very few married women working in the textile industry in 1911 reported more than two previous births. Women with older children might be more able to return to work, but at that point could also substitute the children’s labour for their own. The exception is widowed women, who were more likely to be recorded with an occupation in the census regardless of how many children they had. Whether married women observed in the census planned to work until their next pregnancy or planned to delay their next pregnancy while they were working can be demonstrated more clearly by looking at the timing of the next birth after the census. Focusing on the events in the reconstructed birth histories which occurred in the years immediately surrounding a census – when I have direct observations of married women’ employment status – I can estimate labour force participation by parity, time since last birth, and time to next birth.

The sample of reconstructed birth histories includes observations for 12,271 women who were married at the time of the 1891 census. Including all ages and parities, 3.75% of these women were enumerated with occupations in the textile industry, and 4.2% were recorded with other occupations. This sample agrees with a total observed labour force participation rate for married women in Derbyshire of 7.3% in 1891. In addition, the linked census data show that 6% of ‘unoccupied’ wives in the census had previously worked in the textile industry. Half of the women working in the 1891 census were enumerated with zero or one coresident child in the household. The reconstructed birth histories confirm this pattern and further show that working wives had fewer previous births as well as fewer surviving children; married women’s work was associated with lower total as well as net marital fertility.

For married women who were not working and had one child ever born in 1891, it had been on average 3.1 years since their first birth. Married women who were actively working in the textile industry had their first births on average 3.6 years previously. Among women who were not recorded as occupied in 1891 but were working in textiles in a previous observation, their first births had occurred on average 2.4 years before. This pattern reflects the fact that these women were more recently married (at slightly older ages) and suggests that they had recently left textile employment, perhaps during their first pregnancy. These durations since last birth are not adjusted for age and may be inflated by open intervals among women who had stopped childbearing after their first birth. However, parity progression ratios did not substantially differ by occupational status in 1891: after the first birth, 76% of married women working in the textile industry would go on to have a second birth after the census, compared with 77% of women who did not report an occupation in 1891.

The effects of women’s recent work histories are captured in differences in the length of their closed birth intervals. Figure 6.5 shows the average length of the intervals which closed after women were observed in 1891 by birth order and occupation. Married women who were working in textiles consistently had the longest birth intervals, followed by women in other types of work.

Figure 6.5 
Duration of closed interval to next birth and number of women by parity and occupation in 1891 

Source: Linked I-CeM data; Derbyshire Registration County, reconstructed birth histories for married women observed in the 1911 census who married before 1891.

These estimates are based on observed or imputed birthdates for children ever born as reported in 1911, whether surviving or dead; the results thus show that the relationship between married women’s work and fertility was not simply an artifact of infant mortality allowing those with lower net fertility to go back to work, but was also the result of slower progression to the next birth among women who were already working. In addition to a direct effect of labour force participation, an indirect effect of occupation is revealed in longer birth intervals among women who had previously worked in the textile industry (the exceptions are current and former textile workers who had a fifth birth after 1891, who may be a highly selected group). Some of the numbers are too small to break down by age, location, and husband’s occupation, but these patterns provide provisional evidence for the other proposed mechanism linking women’s work and fertility via cultural and social factors which affected childbearing even when women were no longer working.

Discussion 
The split-population cure model is a powerful new tool in demographic analysis but comes with 
limitations. Although one advantage of the model is the ability to include censored intervals in the 
estimate of time spent at risk for experiencing an event, the distinction between ‘stoppers’ and 
‘spacers’ may at times be too stark. Couples did not necessarily decide at the beginning of a 
marriage or the opening of a birth interval how long they would postpone the next conception, or 
which one would be their last. These data have not allowed me to include time-varying covariates 
such as infant survival which would affect these childbearing decisions. 

Nevertheless, these results provide further evidence for the importance of birth spacing in the low fertility associated with the textile industry. The reasons for this distinct fertility regime may partly lie in the balance between demand for married women’s work and child labour in the textile factories. By spacing births, women reduced the gap between their ultimate withdrawal from the workforce and the entry to work of their eldest child. Women’s contributions to the family wage economy also gave them bargaining power to assert a preference for fewer children, if this differed from their husbands’ fertility desires (Lundberg & Pollak, 1994; Seccombe, 1995). If female textile workers were deliberately delaying only the first and second births after marriage, these patterns might be more accurately described as ‘postponement’ than as evidence of parity-independent birth spacing (Timæus & Moultrie, 2020). On the other hand, there is growing evidence – including the longer birth intervals identified in the Moderate and Low fertility clusters in Chapter 5, the split population model predicted birth intervals by parity, and the timing of births following observations of married women’s work shown above – that former textile workers and other occupational groups also had different birth intervals at parities two and above or when no longer working.

It remains possible that the conditions of work in textile factories contributed to higher risks of miscarriage or stillbirth among working women which led to longer intervals between live births. In a systematic review of recent studies, Bonde et al. (2012) found that the working conditions associated with the greatest increase in the risk of miscarriage were nightshifts, while more moderate risks were associated with working long hours, heavy lifting, and long periods of standing. In Derbyshire in this period, women were prevented from working night shifts by the Factory Acts and limited to 10-hour days. On the other hand, these more recent findings do not address women’s historical exposure to pollutants in the workplace (particularly in dyeing operations) or their general level of nutrition (Greenlees, 2016; Harrison, 1996).

The relationship between women’s labour force participation and family size was not simple or mechanical. Stopping and spacing differed between female occupational groups, even when accounting for compositional differences in the rates of married women’s work as shown above. The split-population models also find that women with no observed occupation before marriage were more likely than domestic servants to stop at lower parities. The composition of this ‘unoccupied’ group is explored further in the next chapter. This finding does not seem to be a proxy for the fact that women who did not work were disproportionately the daughters of middle-class families. Miners’ daughters were also not likely to be observed working outside the home before marriage, both because of the lack of employment opportunities in the mining districts and because of the domestic burdens of miners’ households (Friedlander, 1973).

Since domestic service was a life cycle stage which ended in marriage, and former domestic servants are rarely recorded with occupations in subsequent censuses in the linked data, the immediate circumstances of women who did not work before marriage and former domestic servants during their peak childbearing years might appear to be very similar; yet, women with no observed pre-marital occupation were less likely than former domestic servants to have more than two births. These patterns require further investigation into compositional differences within occupational groups: were some women with no recorded occupation in the census in fact contributing to a family business? Were some women able to remain longer in formal education before marriage because they had older siblings out at work? In the following chapter, I also investigate possible biases in the age at which I first observe women in different occupations in the linked census data.

The split-population models reveal that stopping and spacing were complementary but not inseparable behaviours. ‘Stoppers’ were particularly associated with one or two births, but ‘spacers’ who had longer birth intervals than the reference group of domestic servants can be found at every parity. These findings agree with the Low and Moderate Fertility clusters of childbearing sequences

identified in the previous chapter. Although the temporal range of these data are limited, the results align with several studies of long-term trends in the prevalence of childless and one-child families in Europe during the fertility transition (Anderson, 1998; Brée et al., 2017). On the one hand, highly restricted fertility suggests a stark discontinuity between pre- and post-transitional ideas about family size, as opposed to a gradual reduction in desired fertility or gradual improvement in abilities achieve it. On the other hand, the different ways in which this change played out in different social and occupational groups, including through a combination of both stopping and spacing, suggest that there were multiple pathways for the adoption of these new attitudes and behaviours. Since these potential mediating factors include both spouses’ occupations and community-level characteristics, the next chapter elaborates on the relationship between people, occupations, and spaces by extending the split-population model to analyse the timing and likelihood of marriage.

CHAPTER 7: WOMEN’S WORK AND MARRIAGE: CONTEXTS AND CONSTRAINTS

Introduction 
Nuptiality – including ages at marriage and proportions marrying – had significant impacts on total 
fertility and population growth in England and Wales before the Demographic Transition. The 
Northwestern European Marriage Pattern, as formulated by Hajnal and demonstrated for England 
by Wrigley and Schofield, provided a Malthusian preventative check on population growth by 
limiting access to marriage (and thus reproduction) through protracted residential service and strong 
social norms which required that couples be able to establish an independent household on 
marriage (Hajnal, 1965; Wrigley & Schofield, 1981). The disruption of these patterns during the 
Industrial Revolution, when new jobs and the growth of cities allowed earlier and more universal 
marriage, contributed to substantial population growth. 

The Demographic Transition was characterised by deliberate alterations to fertility within marriage, but this does not mean that levels of nuptiality were constant. After a century of decline, age at marriage in England and Wales was creeping upwards in the late nineteenth century (Schürer et al., 2018). In the 1881 census, 20% of thirty-year old women in Derbyshire had not yet married; in 1911, this proportion had risen to 26%. In the previous chapter, I compared the roles of stopping and spacing as drivers of total marital fertility decline, but in this section, I consider whether the factors affecting the transition to marriage can also provide insight into mechanisms behind the changing relationships and behaviours among spouses within marriage. Applying the split-population model to the transition to marriage also considers the extent to which the parity progression models developed above are subject to selection bias governing the age and occupational distributions of the sample of married women. In addition, the associations between a woman’s observed occupation before marriage and those of her father and future husband reveal how the operation of occupation as a driver of demographic change was qualitatively affected by interactions between and within occupational groups.

Background The mining and textile industries were associated with unique fertility patterns within marriage and were also characterized by distinctive factors affecting the entry to marriage. The dominance of local industries which employed large numbers of men or women – but not both – contributed to sex selective migration, unbalanced sex ratios, and significant geographic variation in nuptiality. The textile districts in Derbyshire generally had more working-age (between 14 and 64 years) women than men while the mining districts had substantial male surpluses, and other agricultural and urban districts had more balanced sex ratios. The subdistrict of Belper, home to the Strutt cotton spinning

mills, had 89.5 working-age men for every 100 women in 1881. The nearby mining district of Ashover had 117.2 men per hundred women. The county seat of Derby had a sex ratio of 97.8, and in the agricultural district of Bakewell it was 95.4. Comparing location of residence in the 1881 census with reported places of birth, the data suggest that most of the gender imbalance in textile and mining districts was due to outmigration of men from the former to the latter: 43% of men in the mining district of Ashover were born more than ten kilometres away (Reid et al., 2018). In contrast, women were not migrating to Belper in large numbers, and only 21% of women there were more than ten kilometres from where they were born. Men and women in Derby were about equally likely to have migrated into the city.

William Ogle, Superintendent of Statistics in the General Register Office, presented a paper to the Royal Statistical Society on the impact of female employment on marriage in the 1890s, arguing that the rate of marriage for men was positively associated with the availability of employment for women, while women’s ages at marriage were negatively correlated to the relative wages of men (Ogle, 1890). Ogle suggested that women who were gainfully employed in industrial occupations were attractive spouses to men, pointing to high proportions of men married in areas with high female employment; however, this calculation did not account for variations in the sex ratio. It could be that women’s wages allowed couples to marry, but it could also be simply that the surplus of women made it easier for men in those districts to find wives.

A skewed sex ratio could also explain Ogle’s finding that women’s ages at marriage in industrial districts were not as low as he expected, compared to those in mining districts where few women were employed. Subsequent analyses of marriage patterns throughout the nineteenth century have argued that women’s employment provided motivation to delay or forgo marriage in order to remain independent (Hacker, 2008; Hill, 1989). On the other hand, where women’s industrial employment continued after marriage, there might be no need for delay, and younger ages at marriage for women in the straw-plait trades, for example, would confirm Ogle’s hypothesis (Goose, 2008; Woods & Hinde, 1985). The timing of marriage was not just determined between two individuals but also between their respective families, and the potentially competing interests of siblings and parents (Alter, 1988). These competing and contingent influences on marriage timing and occurrence are well suited to analysis using the split-population model.

Local sex ratios and wages affect the ‘supply’ side of marriage, in terms of the pool of potential spouses. Different occupational structures might also affect demand for marriage, if wage levels either allowed couples to set up households or allowed individuals to remain independent. Demand for marriage could also be conditional on preferences for a particular type of partner – whether of the right age, occupation, or social class. With a skewed sex ratio, the gender in greater

demand might have more power of choice and ability to ‘marry up’ to a higher social class (Abramitzky et al., 2011). Families and communities exercised a considerable amount of control over who married whom and when, whether parents preferred to keep their children’s earnings at home or had strong preferences for whom they should marry (Oris et al., 2014). Women who became pregnant as a result of pre-marital sexual activity might be younger at marriage; indeed, Alter (1988) argued that pregnancy could be a bargaining strategy for couples seeking parental permission to marry. Patterns of marriage within and between occupations were important in shaping the different marital fertility patterns displayed by socio-occupational groups, as shown in Chapter 5. Thus, the transition to marriage is important not only for its association with the beginning of childbearing, but also for its relation to the social and cultural dynamics affecting the choice of spouse and relations between spouses.

Using the linked census data, I can analyse these patterns based on individual-level observations of occupation and age at marriage instead of relying on aggregate analysis of nuptiality based on estimates of labour force participation and age at marriage by district. If an unbalanced sex ratio in textile districts affected nuptiality, it is important to know whether it was the textile workers themselves who either changed the timing of marriage or did not marry at all. Variations in population composition between districts can bias indirect estimations of singulate mean age at marriage which are based on the distribution of population by marital status and age, especially if women worked in one locale and married in another. For example, large numbers of domestic servants in a middle-class neighbourhood swell the population of single women, which increases the estimated average age at marriage based on census data, but this estimate does not prove that domestic servants themselves married later than other women in the working classes (Garrett et al., 2001). Migration remains a source of bias in the linked data since long-distance migrants are more difficult to link, and the exact timing of migration compared to the date of marriage is not known.

Marriage data 
Around 30,000 women have been linked from pre- to post-marital censuses in the three decades 
between 1881 and 1911. This sample represents a small proportion of the total marriages (including 
re-marriages) occurring in the period and still leaves a deficit in links for women in their twenties, 
especially among migrants who cannot be matched to the index of marriages for the county. 
Because of this limitation, selecting a sample of single women from one census and following their 
outcomes to the next census would be significantly biased by censored intervals which are non 
random, since censoring is caused by the event of interest as well as by migration, mortality, or 
linking error. 

Instead, I assemble the sample of women which I use to analyse the transition to marriage retrospectively, starting with all married women in the 1911 census who have been linked to a census entry from when they were unmarried in any of the previous three censuses. To model the risk of remaining single, I use the distribution by age and marital status of all women in the 1911 census to select a sample of single women in 1911 which is weighted by age group so that the combined dataset of 51,700 women is representative of total proportions married by cohort. Overall, 30% of women aged 20-24; 62% of those aged 25-29; 85% those aged 40-44; and 90% of women aged 55-59 were ever-married in 1911.

I limit the selection of single women to those who are either observed in census links before the age of 25 or observed at any age living in the parental household. For women who are not observed at a younger age, I can assume that their exposure to the risk of getting married began at age 15 and continued to the age at which they are observed still single. This requirement is designed to ensure that these women had accurately reported their marital status in the census (were not previously married) and to ensure comparable observations of occupation by life-cycle stage for the married and unmarried. The interval to marriage for the timing sub-model starts at age 15 (the age threshold for linked marriages) and ends either at marriage or in 1911. Since both open and closed intervals are thus much longer than those for the parity progression models, resulting in a larger dataset, I take a random sample of 8,500 marriage spells before running the split-population model in order to reduce processing time.

In addition to women’s pre-marital occupations, defined by observation before marriage or by occupation observed closest to age twenty for women who remained single, I retrieve the occupational categories of fathers for women enumerated in the same household at any census. A category for ‘unknown’ paternal occupation is added for women with widowed mothers and for domestic servants who are first observed in the households of their employers. Location of residence is measured at first observation, when women were either children or young adults, using the same environmental typology of urban, rural, textile, and mining districts (Table D.1).

It would be useful to measure the effect of migration on marriage outcomes for women who moved between environments before marriage, in their teens and early twenties, but the data allow for only limited observations of mobility. Direct observations of pre-marital migration (as opposed to cruder measures of lifetime mobility based on reported birthplaces) depend on linking women in at least two censuses before marriage, which would exclude older birth cohorts and skew the total sample toward women who married later. Migration is not included in the split-population model, although migrants likely differed in the timing and likelihood of marriage, as discussed below.

Results

Marriage patterns by occupation 
Firstly, the full linked census dataset allows me to explore associations between a woman’s father’s 
occupation, her own work as a child and young adult, and, if she married, the occupation of her 
husband. In this section, I define both women’s and men’s occupations as the most frequent 
occupational category observed across linked censuses and include all individuals who are observed 
past the age of fifteen. Several processes govern the sorting of individuals into occupations and from 
occupations into marriage. As might be expected, the Derbyshire census data show that sons tended 
to inherit their fathers’ occupations. Figure 7.1 illustrates a chi-square test of independence between 
father-son and father-daughter occupations by highlighting the difference between observed and 
expected frequencies of father-child occupational combinations, relative to their share of the total 
population. 

Occupational patterns also reflect the range of opportunities within local labour markets: generations of men in rural districts worked in agriculture, men in mining districts became miners, men in the textile districts worked in the factories. Some occupations which were less geographically clustered show a weaker but still notable tendency to be passed down to the next generation, as the sons of professional men went into the professions, the sons of shopkeepers inherited the business, and the sons of railway clerks and porters also worked in transportation.

Although the occupations open to women were different, daughters were still notably likely to be observed with the same broad occupational category as their fathers in the professions and white-collar occupations, textile factories, shopkeeping, and agriculture. In addition, the daughters of farmers and agricultural labourers were likely to go into domestic service (as well as farm service, although this has been classified into the agriculture category where possible). The intensity of occupational inheritance in the textile industry is striking and clearly associated with the demand for child labour in the factories. In addition, the censuses show that textile workers were particularly unlikely to have fathers who worked in mining and heavy industry, even though these industries often operated nearby to each other.

Women whose fathers were in the white-collar group and in the heavy industry group are both slightly more likely than others to not be observed with an occupation in the linked census data. This pattern agrees with the expectation that middle-class women would be less likely to work, both because their labour was not necessary to family income and because they cultivated domesticity as a marker of class status. The sons of middle-class families are also more likely to have no observed occupation, which may reflect a longer period of formal education. The reasons for

miners’ daughters’ ‘unemployment’ are likely different, as these women would have been helping with domestic burdens at home and had fewer employment options in mining districts. Conversely, the daughters of textile operatives were less likely to either go into domestic service or have no observed occupation in the census data.

These figures are not standardised for age or duration of observation, except for ensuring that I have observed boys and girls past the age when they would be likely to leave school and begin regular employment. Particularly for women, I have likely missed observing occupations for those who were younger and thus not working in one census and married before the next census. These patterns are also driven by the demand for child labour in the textile industry, meaning that occupations in textiles can be observed at younger ages. Among women who are linked in at least two censuses, were single in their first observation, and are observed past the age of fifteen, those classed as textile workers were on average 12.9 years old when they entered observation; domestic servants were 15.3 years old at first observation; and women with no observed occupation were 9.7 years old in their first census entry. Single occupational descriptors necessarily underestimate the amount and diversity of women’s work, especially since they are constructed from observations taken ten years apart. In the next chapter, I demonstrate how the estimated number of men and

women ever employed in the textile industry grows when incorporating yearly observations from factory wage books.

Several occupational groups also show a notable tendency to marry each other, as illustrated in Figure 7.2. Relative to their share of the population, textile workers were disproportionately likely to marry other textile workers. This pattern is not surprising, since half of textile workers lived in districts which were dominated by textile employment, and couples perhaps met in the factory. In addition, white-collar workers were likely to find partners from the same background, and about 40% of professional occupations were found in urban and semi-urban areas. Local industries not only determined the occupational structure but also affected how different occupational groups were integrated into social networks which facilitated courtship and marriage. Domestic servants were found in every district and married to men of nearly every occupation, but servants were slightly more concentrated in urban and rural rather than textile or mining districts, these locations implying different kinds of service.

In the face of geographic and demographic constraints on partner choice, one could wait for the right person of similar or complementary background, migrate to an area with wider economic

Figure 7.2 Chi-square residual frequencies of observed vs expected occupational combinations for husband/wife pairs

Source: Linked I-CeM data; Derbyshire Registration County, couples linked to the Index of Marriages. Note: ‘aglab’ = farmers, agricultural labourers, and general labourers; ‘industry’ = miners and metal workers; ‘professions’ = all white-collar occupations.

and marital prospects, or forgo marriage altogether. In the 1911 census, a third of women in textile districts were single at age 30, compared with only 15% of women in mining districts. Age at marriage and proportions married could vary in tandem or in opposite directions. In districts with surplus single women, competition for husbands might push the average age at first marriage downwards for one segment of the population, while leaving a large proportion of women unmarried. Adult migration might delay marriage while migrants took time to integrate into new social networks, but some studies have found that migrants ultimately were more likely to marry (Oris, 2000). Among women 40-44 years old in the 1911 census, those born outside the county were slightly more likely than those born in Derbyshire to be married (86.1% vs 85.2%) and on average reported slightly older ages at marriage (24.1 vs 23.4 years old).

Cure models of nuptiality In a model of the effect of occupation on marriage timing or occurrence, I expect that this relationship will depend on location, not only because of the availability of partners but also due to the local economic and social conditions which governed access to marriage. If marriage came with the expectation of setting up a new household or farm, then couples might have to wait longer for house or land to become available in rural districts. In textile districts where factory owners had invested in workers’ housing, textile couples might have been advantaged in setting up new households. Women working in the textile industry may also have had better chances of securing a husband despite the skewed sex ratios in their districts because they commanded good wages and because they were connected to potential partners via the social networks of the factory. To highlight the distinctive marriage markets operating in each district and the differential impact of occupation by type of district, I include an interaction term between female occupations and district types in the split-population model for the timing and occurrence of marriage.

The importance of these interactions is illustrated by the results of the first model in Table 7.1, which does not include the interaction term. Overall, the average ages at marriage observed in the sample were 23.6 for women in domestic service and 23.5 for textile workers. Holding location of residence and father’s occupation constant, the model predicts that female textile workers had almost 40% higher odds of marrying than domestic servants and would be 4% younger at marriage. In contrast, the estimated effect of district of residence before marriage presents a paradox, in that women of all occupations are predicted to marry later and have lower odds of ever marrying in textile districts than in rural areas. Are the better marriage chances of textile workers driven by their advantages compared to other occupational groups within textile districts or by their marriage patterns in other districts? In the interaction model, the effect of occupation is allowed to vary by place, and vice versa.

In the second (interaction) model of Table 7.1, the main effect of occupation shows that, within rural districts, servants and textile workers did not have significantly different marriage patterns, while women in white-collar occupations married significantly later and were more likely to remain single than domestic servants. The main effect of district of residence for domestic servants shows that servants were less likely to marry in textile districts (although the coefficient is not significant) and married earlier in mining districts, compared with rural areas. Women with no observed pre-marital occupation are predicted to marry 20% younger than domestic servants. As discussed above, these results could be biased by the age at which women are first observed in the linked census data; women may have no observed occupation because they married early and could not be observed in multiple censuses before marriage

Several of the coefficients on the interaction between occupation and location are significant, confirming that stratifying the regression in this way is meaningful. To make comparison between groups easier, Table 7.2 shows the predicted median ages at marriage and predicted probabilities of ever marrying (in parentheses) by occupation and place. These estimates illustrate the general pattern of lower nuptiality in the textile districts – in terms of both higher ages at marriage and lower proportions marrying – which is likely driven by the skewed sex ratios in these districts. However, the interaction model shows that there were exceptions to this rule among both dressmakers and textile workers. Dressmakers married late in general and were particularly old at marriage in textile districts, but they were also more likely than other occupational groups to ever marry in these districts; this unique relationship between occupation and location is confirmed by the significant coefficient for this interaction term.

Textile workers also are not estimated to experience the same marriage penalties in textile districts as other occupational groups, although they are still predicted to have relatively greater chances of marriage in urban and mining districts. The models do not suggest that textile workers were distinctive in delaying marriage in any district, compared with the estimated ages at marriage for dressmakers and women in other occupations. On the contrary, women’s ability to continue working in textiles after marriage and to delay childbearing, as shown in the parity progression models in the previous chapter, meant that these women might not have had strong motivations to avoid marriage in order to continue working. About 24% of textile workers came from rural districts which were home to smaller-scale textile factories which employed less than a quarter of the local workforce. Despite the proximity of male agricultural labourers as potential spouses in rural districts, textile workers in these areas are not predicted to have lower ages or higher chances of marriage than in textile districts.

The lower estimated nuptiality within rural districts may suggest that traditional restrictions on access to marriage were still operating as a form of population control (Oris et al., 2014).

Predicted ages for each occupational group are not substantially higher in urban districts, where most groups are also predicted to have higher proportions married. An interesting exception to this pattern is women with white-collar occupations, who are predicted to have the lowest chances of marrying in urban districts. These environments might have provided the best chances of meeting a partner, but they clearly also offered greater opportunities for professional women to remain

independent.

Finally, the model also controls for the effect of father’s occupation on the timing and occurrence of a daughter’s marriage. These results generally agree with the findings for women’s own occupations, particularly regarding nuptiality among women from middle- and upper-class households. The higher likelihood of women without a known father’s occupation to marry compared with the daughters of miners might deserve further investigation, although the effect must be cautiously interpreted, since it is in some respects an artifact of record linking. Women who are linked in two censuses before marriage are more likely to be observed in the same household as their parents but also cannot have married before their twenties at the earliest. In contrast, women without a known father’s occupation include those in service who are not linked to a childhood census as well as women whose fathers were not present in the household at the census either

because they had died or the family had broken up. These circumstances might remove familial barriers to marriage or push women into marriage as an economic necessity (Alter, 1988; Oris et al., 2014).

Discussion 
These insights into the intersection of labour markets and marriage markets complement the parity 
progression models in several ways. Firstly, they reveal how restricted fertility within marriage began 
with restricted access to or appetite for marriage among several occupational groups, including 
dressmakers and women in white-collar occupations. In contrast, differences in nuptiality between 
textile workers and domestic servants seem to have been more rooted in different geographic 
distributions and access to potential spouses. It is notable that textile employment did not 
encourage women to substantially delay marriage; on the contrary, textile workers in Derbyshire 
seem to have been advantaged on the marriage market in some districts. 

There was not space here to consider these questions from the opposite perspective, in terms of men’s ages at marriage and proportions marrying. Men in Derbyshire married slightly older than women on average; in 1911, 36% of men in their twenties were married, compared with 46% of women, but almost 90% of people of both sexes in their forties were married or widowed. Women’s ages at marriage are more directly relevant to levels of marital fertility, but further exploration of different marriage patterns for male occupational groups could help to elaborate the social and economic contexts of courtship and family formation. It would also be particularly interesting to relate these findings to the prevalence of bridal pregnancy and illegitimate births in different districts and occupational groups, but this question would require data which cannot be reconstructed from the census or vital registration indexes.

The younger ages at marriage and high proportions marrying among women with no observed premarital occupation (although partly an artifact of age at observation in the census) helps to explain the short intervals to first birth identified by the previous section but also highlights the contrast between this matrimonial intensity and the later likelihood of stopping at one or two births among this group. Analysing the likelihood and timing of marriage simultaneously avoids confusing the two factors or focusing on one at the expense of another. As with birth spacing, marriage postponed could become marriage foregone. Both the parity progression models and the marriage model capture only a small part of the planned, unplanned, and accidental processes of family formation. The final chapter in this section introduces another data source for insight into further dimensions of work, marriage and family in the communities surrounding the textile factories.

CHAPTER 8: FACTORY FAMILIES: WORK, MARRIAGE, AND CHILDBEARING IN A MILL TOWN

Introduction 
The foregoing analysis of the county census data has confirmed that the textile industry in 
Derbyshire was associated with distinctive demographic patterns. Analysis on this scale has 
necessarily made several assumptions about how to define occupational groups, aiming to include as 
many observations of women’s work as possible. The ‘textile’ category includes all types of textile 
work in factories and workshops, even some observations where women are described in the census 
simply as ‘mill hand.’ Although these women were in many ways united by shared experiences of 
factory work which differed substantially from the working lives of women who were domestic 
servants in private homes or dressmakers in small shops, the county textile industry produced a 
wide variety of fibres and products which were associated with different tasks, wages, and 
workforces. Where the county bordered Lancashire to the north, the textile industry in Hayfield and 
Glossop was likely very different in character to the historic mills in the Derwent River Valley or the 
hosiery industry in the southeast of the county. 

Measurement of women’s work before marriage in the previous sections has also generally been based on one observed occupational description in the census before a woman is linked to the index of marriages. Many women’s occupations will thus have been missed or miscategorized depending on the ages they entered and left observation. The interesting findings for the marriage and fertility patterns of women with no observed occupation, as discussed above, may be partly explained by these data limitations. I have interpreted both male and female occupational groups as capturing cultural and social identities as well as economic circumstances to explain how women’s work before marriage could continue to affect marital fertility even when women were no longer recorded as working at the time of the census. Nevertheless, more precise information about when women started and stopped working in relation to the timing of marriage and childbirth would clearly be useful to demonstrate the short- and long-term effects of occupation. In order to explore possible mechanisms for the impact of textile work on marriage and fertility, I now turn to a case study of one cotton spinning factory in Belper, using surviving wage books and child workforce registers to enrich the linked census data.

The cotton spinning mills in Belper were established in the late eighteenth century by Jedediah Strutt (Fitton & Wadsworth, 1958). Though dwarfed in size by the growing textile centres of Lancashire and the Yorkshire West Riding, the Strutt mills remained in operation well into the twentieth century, helped by a merger with other small firms in 1897 to form the English Sewing

Cotton Company. The surviving archive of W. G. & J. Strutt and Co. includes several series of records for the period covered by the linked census data (W. G. and J. Strutt and Co., 1751). These collections include child workforce registers, weekly wages books, daily time books, workmen’s books, factory school logs, and even photographs of mill employees. Given the rich detail and density of some of these sources, it was not possible to collect and transcribe all complete series. However, I sample several types of sources which allow the identification of individual workers and link the transcribed records to the county census data.

Factory employment data Firstly, I sampled the daily time books for each of the five mills which made up the Strutt factory complex in Belper for the same months around the time of the 1881 census, which was taken on 3 rd

April of that year. The time books for each mill cover overlapping but not identical periods, and 1881 is the only census year covered by all. The books contain lists of workers by room and by occupation, starting with the overlooker responsible for the group. Each page covered two weeks, recording daily hours worked and weekly totals for time and wages owed. The occupations listed illustrate every stage of the production process, from preparing and carding raw cotton to spinning, twisting, and winding thread to finishing and packing products for the warehouse. The cross-sectional sample of the workforce in late February and early March of 1881 includes 688 individuals.

The daily time books were just part of a suite of ledgers dealing with the management of the factory workforce and running of the mill. Separate registers kept track of deductions and ‘stoppages’ to be taken out of workers’ wages to cover breakages, payments into the sick club, tea provided to workers in the factory, other provisions bought by textile worker’s families from the mill – including milk, coal, meat, and corn – and rent for factory-owned cottages. The individual stoppages account books survive for only a handful of non-consecutive years. However, both the daily time books and the other workforce accounts were assembled into the weekly wage books for mill hands. The only continuous series of weekly wage books surviving is from the 1890s.

The weekly wage books summarize the total wages earned by each worker as well as deductions for money lent or owed for tea, late fines, other goods bought, and rent; however, the total stoppages and wages to be paid were summed for each family group. Mill hands were listed alphabetically by surname; after the first entry for the ‘head’ of the family who would presumably receive the combined wage packet, subsequent family members were ordered by age. The final page for each week included entries for new workers who would be incorporated into their alphabetically correct position in the next week’s list, as well as total wages and stoppages checked against the other ledger books. The surviving wage books cover the years 1893 to 1900. The workforce

fluctuated week to week, but on average the entries cover around 650 individuals paid on 430 family ‘tickets.’ Since a full transcription would run to more than 250,000 lines, I have sampled a week from the end of each quarter of the year – March, June, September, and December – covering a total of 20,986 entries over eight years.

The Strutt company also operated a smaller bleaching and dyeing mill in nearby Milford, for which fewer records survive. To estimate the size and structure of the workforce there, I consulted the bleachers’ and dyers’ state of wages book for 1891, which listed individuals and their wage rates, with changes to wage rates noted for dates up to 1895. The state of wages book included a column of ‘remarks’ which noted that some workers had left the mill. In a few cases the clerk provided intriguing details such as ‘died Feb. 1895,’ and noted that five women were ‘now married’ with a new surname. The Milford mill employed about 175 hands in 1891.

Finally, the only series which covers the whole period of the linked census data is the register of children and young persons employed in the mills under the Factory and Workshop Acts of 1878, 1901, and 1907, which I have transcribed in its entirety. Under the factory acts, a child could start work half-time from the age of ten after passing an examination by a doctor to verify their age and fitness for work (Hewitt, 1958). A ‘young person’ could work up to ten-hour days from the age of 14, or from 13 years old if they had passed a minimum educational standard. The minimum working age was raised to 12 in 1901. The factory kept certificates for children over 8 and young persons over 13 from 1844 to 1879 which recorded only the date of employment and the names of parents. I have transcribed these registers starting in 1872, to focus on children who can be linked to the 1881 census.

From 1879 onwards, these registers were kept in a standard format which recorded a child’s name, residence, parents’ names, date of birth, date of employment, date of certificate (with surgeon’s signature), and date of any educational attainment. There is a gap in the series where the registers are missing between 1883 and 1886. Together, the registers of children and young persons include 4,360 entries. Many children had two certificates – one when they began work as a half timer and another if they continued working full time as a young person. Accounting for this, the registers cover 3,188 individual children.

Each of these sources cover slightly different groups from among the Strutt mills workforce and together they provide insight into the textile industry in Belper for the decades covered by the linked census data from between 1881 and 1911. In addition, they contain information that is useful for linking named individuals to the census such as age and parents’ names (for the registers of children and young persons) and the names of other family members (for the weekly wage books).

In cases where only an individual’s name is recorded, including the time books and the dyers’ state of wages book, the records have been sampled around the time of the census, so that I can be reasonably confident that these workers may be found living in the area of Belper or Milford and are likely to report a matching occupation in the census.

I link individuals from the transcribed factory records to the census dataset largely by hand, first searching census records within the Belper registration district and using a few filtering rules to identify candidate links based on name and age similarity. After an initial round of links, I used household information from the census to make further links or to correct links for individuals belonging to the same family. Finally, I searched records for the rest of the county for individuals who could not be linked within Belper, although if they had common names and no other identifying information then the chances of making a link were limited. Links to the census also revealed links between different types of factory record, particularly for children who appeared in both the register of young persons and in the wage books. After arranging entries by individual, altogether the factory records cover 4,170 workers, of whom 3,527 can be linked to at least one census entry.

Workers, wages, and families 
Links to the census data reveal many facets of the factory workforce which, unlike gender, cannot be 
observed directly from the wage records, including age, marital status, and relationship to other 
factory workers. In addition, the factory records reveal the extent to which the enumeration of 
occupations in the census is an accurate or complete representation of work experiences. The 
factory records also provide insight into changes to the workforce across three decades and, to some 
extent, into the duration of employment. Unfortunately, sources like the registers of children and 
the Milford state of wages book noted when an individual had left employment but did not provide 
an exact date. The weekly wages books can confirm who was working in the mills for only a limited 
number of years between the 1891 and 1901 censuses. Nevertheless, several interesting findings 
emerge from exploratory analysis of the factory records. 

The earliest evidence concerning the structure of the entire factory workforce comes from the daily time books for 1881. The largest class of workers in the mills consisted of adult women over the age of sixteen; in total, women and girls made up over half of the factory workforce. The occupations recorded in the time books reveal that several women worked as overseers, placed in charge of a combined 111 workers in rooms in the North and South Mills. Table 8.1 presents a week’s wages converted to pence for ease of comparison. The time books reveal a gender wage gap which varied with age. Children working as half-timers made the same standard wages; teenaged girls were, on average, taking home more pay for the week than their brothers; but adult men were

the highest paid workers in the mill. Excluding the high wages paid to overlookers and labourers (such as porters and nightwatchmen), adult male mill hands still outearned adult women by three

shillings per week.

Whether this gender wage gap was the result of direct discrimination or occupational segregation can be determined by consulting the occupations which were listed with each group of names in the time books (Table E.1). There were several occupations which were exclusively performed by one gender. Within the carding rooms, several of the highest-paid occupations in the mill were undertaken by teenaged and adult men who prepared the raw cotton bales and maintained the carding machines. In the same rooms, a number of adult women worked for more moderate piece rates ‘roving’ – twisting and winding the carded cotton onto bobbins ready for spinning. Rather than being associated with only one or two occupations, children assisted in nearly every process. Within the spinning rooms, girls were ‘doffers’ removing full spindles from the machines and boys assisted in cleaning the frames. All of the spinners in the mill were teenaged and young adult women who were paid by time rather than by piece rates. In the absence of other evidence about the factory machinery, this pattern suggests that the spinners in the Strutt mills were working on continuous spinning machines rather than self-acting mules, which were associated with skilled male labour in Lancashire (Lazonick, 1979). In the finishing processes, all the workers on the winding and reeling machines (creating hanks of yarn ready for bleaching or dyeing) were

female.

Most of the disparity between female and male wages seems to have been driven by the different wage rates associated with these gender-specific occupations. For example, teenaged female spinners earned around 6 shillings for a week of work while their brothers made up to 4 shillings attending the machines in the card rooms. The most common rent value deducted from family weekly wage tickets (as described below) was 2 shillings and 6 pence per week. The wages of adult women (apart from overlookers) were not much higher than the amounts earned by younger women, since they were mostly performing the same tasks. In contrast, the highest paid occupations were exclusive to adult men and some teenaged assistants, suggesting that higher pay reflected a premium attached to acquired skill and experience.

In a few cases where tasks were performed by substantial numbers of both sexes, there was no clear distinction between male and female wages when accounting for age. The select few female overlookers had slightly lower wages than their male counterparts, but still outearned most of the other men in the mills. Wages in the Strutt factory were lower than those reported for factory operatives in Lancashire in the same period, although most workers in Belper were paid according to time worked rather than earning piece rates as was common among mule spinners in Lancashire (Board of Trade, 1894). These lower wages paid for time worked seem to have been the norm for the Derbyshire textile industry, outside of Hayfield and Glossop.

The wage advantage of girls over boys may also have reflected greater experience from longer employment. The registers of children and young persons show that about equal numbers of boys and girls started work in the factory as children in the 1870s (Figure 8.1). Fewer boys than girls were registered as young persons, suggesting that boys were both less likely to continue working and less likely to start working in the mills in their teens. The slightly lower average ages of male young persons compared with female young persons in Table 8.1 implies that boys left the factory in their early teens, while girls were likely to stay into their twenties. The smaller number of men who stayed in the mills long-term were highly selected and concentrated in supervisory roles. Skipping over the gap in the series in the 1880s, fewer boys appear in the child workforce registers in the 1890s, but the total number of children and young persons entering the factory seems have remained relatively stable, as the decrease in boys was compensated by an increase in the number of girls registered for work as children and young persons.

Another discontinuity in the child labour series appears in 1901, when the Factory Act raised the minimum working age to twelve years old. This legislation seems to have seriously affected the appeal of factory work for boys, who hardly appear at all as children and in limited numbers as young persons in the registers after 1901. The number of girls registered as young persons again increased. The impression created from the cross-sectional age distributions and numbers of

children and young persons entering the factory is that, in the earlier period, it was common for both boys and girls in Belper to spend at least some time working in the mills. At the time of the 1881 census, the children recorded in the daily time books represented 16% of the population of children aged ten to twelve in the district. For boys, this work was a temporary stage preceding work in another sector. Girls were more likely to work in the cotton mills for a number of years.

This theory can also be tested by examining the occupations recorded for mill workers in the censuses. Firstly, the links between the 1881 census and daily time books address the question of how accurately occupations were reported by heads of household and census enumerators. Since the census was taken in April and the time books were sampled in March, a small number of people appear to have left the mill during the month between observations. In the census, 86% of adult women and 79% of adult men who are linked from the time books were enumerated correctly as working in the textile industry. There thus does not seem to have been a bias against recording women’s factory work in the census. However, children working half-time in the mills represented a grey area between school and work, and this ambiguity is reflected in the census: rather than being enumerated as ‘half-timers’ or otherwise recorded with occupations, 65% of children and 12% of

Figure 8.1 
The number of boys and girls registered to work half-time as children or full-time as young persons in the 
Strutt mills by year, 1872-1911 
Source: DRO D6948/H. 
Note: Register of children for 1883-1886 missing. 

young persons who were working in the mill as of March were enumerated as ‘scholars’ in April. Without links to these factory records or to another census (for those who remained in the factory for at least ten years), this work is invisible in the census.

In addition to the under-enumeration of half-time work in the mills, individuals who worked for shorter periods of time are also more likely to have been missed because their employment did not coincide with the decennial census. I can estimate the duration of employment using the weekly wage books for children who started work between 1890 and 1895. The median number of years worked before a child was no longer recorded in the wage books (or before the end of the series in 1900) was four for boys and six for girls. The limitations of the census as a source for measuring part time and short-term work are not particular to this industry, but the factory records reveal a gendered pattern which particularly biases estimation of child labour and the factory workforce. Since boys’ factory employment was more limited to a single age group, linking between censuses can only mitigate these biases for a small number of boys who were aged between 10 and 13 – and were accurately recorded as half-timers – at the time of a census. Among men and women in the linked data who are observed in Belper in at least one census, 22% of women were enumerated as working in the textile industry at some point, compared with only 8% of men. Boys left the mills for other work as children or teenagers, and 48% of young men from Belper were enumerated as coal miners or iron workers in a later census.

The extent of local employment in the Strutt mills is emphasized by the arrangement of the weekly wage books by family. The largest family groups appearing in the wage books included six individuals employed at the same time, and up to nine members of the same household have been linked to the factory records across several years. Around 130 families were paying rent for factory housing out of their combined weekly wages at any one time. In about half of these cases, rent was paid by individuals who had no other family members contributing to the wage ticket. The family ‘heads’ for whom both rent and total wages were recorded include 201 women across all years and 145 workers under the age of 18 who were paying rent on behalf of their families. These women and children were not necessarily the heads of their households according to the census, but they illustrate the importance of women’s and children’s labour to their families via both wages and access to factory housing.

The linked factory records also reveal more details about married women’s work in the mills. In 1881, 51 women in the time books, or 20% of the adult female workers, were married. Another 20 women working in the factory were widowed. Sixteen of the married women in the time books had no co-resident children in the 1881 census; eleven had one child in the same household; and 23 had more than one child at home. Not every woman can be linked to the index of marriages, and gaps in

the series of factory registers mean that the duration of women’s employment after marriage cannot be estimated consistently. However, the 1890s wage books suggest that most women did not work for more than one or two years after marriage, and many left the mills in the same year they were married. At any one time, at least 20 women recorded in the weekly wage books were married, according to the census and links to the index of marriages.

Although incomplete, the assembled evidence from the Strutt cotton mills suggests several characteristics of textile employment in this period which are relevant for analysis of the demographic consequences of women’s work. Firstly, the degree of under-enumeration of textile employment among children confirms that the industry’s impact on marriage and fertility cannot be totally captured by observations of individual occupation in the census. The dynamic relationship between families and the factory motivated the inclusion of textile environments as contextual influences on fertility in the split-population models for marriage and parity progression. The male occupational categories defined above fail to capture the sectoral shift from textiles to other jobs for boys who worked short spells in the mill and may thus mismeasure compositional differences within occupational groups. The combined datasets reveal 184 couples in which both spouses worked in the mill at one time or another, half of which would not have been identified without the factory records because one or both spouses were never enumerated with an occupation in the textile industry. The tendency for inter-marriage between textile workers discussed in Chapters 5 and 7 is thus underestimated by enumerated occupations.

Women’s wages in the factory were relatively higher than they might have expected to earn as domestic servants (Layton, 1908) but lower than those earned in the Lancashire textile districts, where active trade unions negotiated common piece rates for spinners. However, women’s earning potential did not increase with age in the same way as men’s, due both to the relative numbers of adult men and women working in the mills and to the skill premium awarded to some exclusively male textile occupations (Phillips & Taylor, 1980). This context means that the opportunity costs of childbearing for women working in textiles might decrease with age, as a wife’s earnings stagnated and the gap between her and her husband’s earnings increased. In addition to the social networks established by the employment of children, teens, and adults in the factory which linked women to future spouses, the wage records show that adult male textile workers were a special class in Belper, having been selected for occupations associated with skill, supervision, and higher wages. This status may also have played a role in textile workers’ aspirations regarding marriage and family.

Split-population models of marriage and first birth in Belper Although the Strutt mill archives provide rich insight into the working lives of men, women, and children in Belper, the incomplete series offer limited opportunities to incorporate this information into demographic models while maintaining a sufficient sample size. Out of 4,170 individuals observed, 1,304 can be assigned a reasonably exact date of starting and ending work in the factory, including just 182 women whose date of marriage is also known. In the analysis above, I suggested that the social networks created by factory employment played significant roles in the process of finding a partner and in the spread of ideas about birth control and family size, beyond the immediate economic incentives related to women’s work. The factory records provide the opportunity to test this mechanism by defining individual involvement in the textile industry by age starting work and by incorporating information about family employment in addition to father’s occupation. Since the sample size is small, I focus only on the transitions to marriage and to first birth among the Strutt employees and other women in Belper and apply split-population models of likelihood and timing as described in Chapters 6 and 7.

To define categories of work in the Strutt mills, I assume that the registers of children and young persons are complete (apart from the known gap 1883-1886) and reliable for the entire period from 1872 onwards. This assumption implies nearly perfect information about child textile labour in Belper for the cohort born between 1865 and 1895, meaning that individuals who appear in the 1881 daily time books or 1890s weekly wage books but do not appear in the registers of children can be classified as having begun work after the age of 16, even if their exact entry into the mill is not observed. This assumption is flawed, since migrants may have started work elsewhere which was not recorded in the census (as discussed above) and families may have lied about their children’s ages so that they could earn higher wages. However, it allows me to classify this cohort of women who are observed in the factory records by whether they started work as a child, young person, or adult, and whether the child workers stayed in the factory into young adulthood or left the mill as a half-timer.

The model of first births requires that women be linked forward to the 1911 census and thus has a smaller sample size. For this model, I define the variable measuring textile employment only by age starting work, since I do not have evidence from the factory records for the duration of employment after 1900. These employment variables are intended to measure the intensity of a woman’s involvement in the textile industry. The social and cultural impacts of factory employment might be particularly relevant to teens and young adults but could have operated at any age, even among children who had left the factory for other jobs. The economic motivations to stay in work by avoiding marriage or pregnancy apply only to women who remained in factory employment into

adulthood. Women who did not start working in the mill until their teens may have had more time in education but may also have been pushed into work by changing family circumstances.

As a control group, I select women in the linked census data from the same age cohorts who were resident in Belper for at least two censuses and were neither linked to the factory records nor ever enumerated with occupations in the textile sector. In addition, to capture wider district effects on the probability and timing of first births, I select a similar sample from the nearby mining district of Ashover, where 40% of working women were domestic servants and 56% of working-age men were miners. Fathers’ occupations (for marriage) and husbands’ occupations (for first births) are grouped into larger categories of textile vs ‘mining and agriculture’ vs ‘all other’ occupations.

For the marriage model, I measure family involvement in the textile industry independently from individual occupation. Across multiple linked households in the census, I select the one in which an individual is observed with the largest number of siblings who were working in the textile industry (if any). I distinguish between cases where no other siblings either appeared in the factory records or were enumerated with a textile occupation; where some siblings either reported a textile occupation or were linked to the factory records; and where more than half of all siblings worked in the textile industry. This variable measures the impact of the textile industry within households even for individuals who were not themselves factory workers and to some extent also captures the impact of family size and composition. Nevertheless, there seems to have been a relatively clear distinction between textile and non-textile families in Belper: 80% of young women who did not work in the industry themselves also did not have any other immediate family member in textiles. These women were more likely than those from textile families to have fathers who were farmers or agricultural labourers. Sample size and variable distributions for the Belper marriage and first birth models are in Table E.2.

The sample of women in the selected districts is small, but the split-population models still identify significant differences in the probability and timing of marriage and first birth between groups (Table 8.2). Controlling for family contexts, women who were working in the Strutt mills in their teens and young adult years married later than those who never worked in the factory or had left employment as children. Both categories of women who worked in the factory as children are predicted to be more likely to marry than women who never worked in the industry, but the difference is not significant. On the other hand, women of all occupations had significantly higher chances of marrying if some of their siblings worked in the textile industry. Women whose fathers were employed in occupations other than agriculture, mining, or textiles were more likely to remain single, as also found in the county-wide marriage analysis above.
