Source: Linked I-CeM data and factory employment data, DRO D6948. Sample size and variable distributions are in Table E.2. Note: ‘agr-ind’ = combined agriculture and industry occupational group. p-values < 0.05 in bold.

These patterns could reflect a combination of economic and social influences on marriage.

Firstly, the differentiation between women who left the mill as children and those who began or continued work into young adulthood could be related to economic necessity, if their wages were essential to their families. Parents might pressure daughters to delay marriage in order to keep their

labour at home (Alter, 1988). Women might also have chosen to delay marriage to retain their own financial independence; however, this ‘empowerment’ effect does not seem to have been associated with staying single permanently, apart from among the very small number of female overlookers. The family wage economy might also explain the positive impact of siblings’ employment on marriage, if the work of other family members relieved financial pressures on the household; however, a greater intensity of family employment in the mills was not associated with significantly different marriage outcomes. The lack of a clear gradient in the impact of textile work in the family may partly be an artifact of how this variable has been defined, because observing the occupational histories of siblings depends on both family size and birth order.

Another pathway for variables to affect the chances and timing of marriage is through social networks in and around the Belper mills. Women whose siblings worked in the factory were connected to other factory families regardless of their own occupation; overall, 12% of women with siblings in the mill married men who worked or had worked in the textile industry, compared with 3% of women with no family connections to the mill. Similar patterns are reflected in the likelihood of marrying a male textile worker by age and duration of own employment: women who left the factory as children were more likely than women who never worked in textiles to marry a man from the mill but almost 13% of women who entered the factory as teenagers married men in the same occupation. As the other factory records illustrated, connections established through textile employment also reached beyond the factory as boys left for other occupations in their teens.

The patchiness of the factory records means that I have not been able to directly incorporate the duration of textile employment after marriage into the split-population model of first births; however, the measurement of past textile employment in Belper is more precise than the pre marital occupational categories defined in the county-wide parity progression models (for example, 85 of the women linked to the factory records in this sample were not previously observed with a pre-marital occupation in the linked census data). The split-population model for Belper finds no significant differences in either the likelihood or timing of first births between women based on textile employment. This result is surprising, given the significant differences between domestic servants and textile workers in the progression to first birth found by the split-population models using the county-wide data. On the other hand, those models also found a significant contextual effect of residence in a textile-industry area on birth intervals at other parities.

Within a small community like Belper, community and contextual impacts on childbearing behaviours may have been strong. The limited choice of variables with this small sample may also omit important confounding factors, like family employment or the presence of other kin networks. To evaluate these community effects, I include a sample of women from the mining district of

Ashover as another control group. The split-population model finds that these women had significantly shorter intervals between marriage and first birth than the women in Belper. These results suggest either that any birth spacing among textile workers was matched by similar behaviours among other women in Belper – confirming strong community effects on fertility – or that my categorization of textile employment does not isolate the subgroup most likely to be limiting their fertility.

Discussion 
Because the Strutt factory records have revealed many more dimensions of work and family life in 
Belper, it has been difficult to narrow down the variables of interest and define the control group 
based on this limited sample. Ideally, an extended study of Belper and its surroundings would 
include a wider range of records transcribed both from the factory archives and from other sources, 
including school records, to create a fuller reconstruction of the family economy in the textile 
districts and reveal how it may have differed in other contexts. Because of data limitations, I have 
also treated this community as something of a closed system, even though migration probably 
played an important role in determining the occupational distribution of the town, district, and 
county. 

Clearly, child labour was continuing to play an important role in the local economy, even as it came under new regulation in the last decades of the nineteenth century (Todd, 2007). These patterns suggest that the measurement of women’s pre-marital occupations through linked census data in previous chapters depends as much on the average age starting work in different industries as it does on the average age at observation in the census. My findings regarding the accuracy of recorded occupations in the census do not undermine the results based on these broad occupational groupings, but they do suggest that the mechanisms linking these observations to demographic outcomes were complex.

Across the last three chapters, the characteristics associated with the adoption of family limitation have been to some extent consistent with ideas about ‘modernity’ – including residence in an urban area, middle- and upper-class social backgrounds, and high levels of female labour force participation. However, these factors operated within older structures of family and community. The Belper mill records suggest that factory work in Derbyshire in this period was less individualized than might have been expected, as families worked together and were paid a common wage. The gradual retreat of boys and young men from the factory workforce reveals how families adjusted their strategies in response to changing economic opportunities and child labour laws, while the labour of girls and young women remained a constant and vital part of the family wage economy. Although it

will take more time and effort to completely reconstruct the networks in which the Belper mills were embedded, this first step has added new dimension to the picture of fertility and change in the textile industry.

“These differences are readily explained if we attribute to human volition the fall which has occurred in the nation's fertility, but if we refuse to acknowledge this agency, it is necessary to assume the reduction of female fertility by non domestic work as a law of nature. Seeing that it is just those women whose domestic work is hardest (e.g., the wives of miners) who are most fertile, this proposition seems, to say the least, improbable.” (Stevenson, 1920)

CHAPTER 9: CONCLUSION

The contours of the life course have changed in many fundamental ways in the last hundred and fifty years. A girl born in Derbyshire in 1875, if she survived past the age of five, was expected to contribute to the household from an early age, especially if she had younger siblings. If her family lived near one of the county’s textile mills, she attended a factory-run school for a few years and started working half-time in the mill at the age of ten. If her family lived near the coalfields, she helped her mother and sisters wage a perpetual war against coal dust and cook a perpetual supply of food for her father and brothers working in the colliery. She expected to marry in her early to mid twenties, at which point she stopped working full-time outside the home due to the arrival of several children, although perhaps not as many as her mother had borne.

In high-income countries, the children born in 2025 are expected not only to survive infancy and childhood, but to spend a large portion of their youth in formal education before entering the workforce in their late teens at the earliest. Couples increasingly delay marriage (if they marry) until their thirties, and many women continue working while their own children are young. Although parents invest substantial amounts of time and money in raising their children, they also spend a large portion of their later lives as ‘empty nesters’ after their children have left home. These sketches offer an idealised, oversimplified timeline of the past and present, but such normative narratives can have a strong influence over what individuals expect from their lives and how they situate themselves in the contexts of contemporary and historical societies (Lazzari et al., 2025; Settersten & Mayer, 1997).

The path between the past and today was not linear. The rise of individualism in the modern life cycle, as seen in the behavioural patterns associated with the Second Demographic Transition, has been contrasted with the morally structured, family-oriented societies of the past; however, a long-run perspective on the history of the family reveals how the decline of mortality and fertility has contributed to greater predictability and uniformity in the transitions between childhood, education, work, marriage, and family formation (Hareven, 1977). Although divorce in nineteenth century British society was rare, higher adult mortality meant that single-parent and blended households – formed via widowhood and remarriage – were as common in the past as they are

today (Schürer et al., 2018). The majority of women worked before marriage, and many later took on part-time work that allowed flexibility for childcare, especially during periods of unemployment or illness for their husbands (Tilly & Scott, 1978). Although these threads tie us closer to the past than many may assume, the last century has also seen widespread and apparently irreversible changes in the shift from ‘unregulated’ high fertility within marriage to tightly controlled, low fertility both inside and outside of marriage.

This thesis has responded to long-standing gaps in the literature on the fertility transition in England and Wales by building a new, longitudinal dataset of both birth histories and occupational histories for women in the county of Derbyshire at the end of the nineteenth century. My analysis has addressed two main debates: first regarding the impact of women’s work, particularly in the textile industry, on marital fertility; and second regarding the role of parity-independent spacing as well as parity-dependent stopping in fertility decline. The results contribute to the field of historical demography both substantively and methodologically by demonstrating the enormous potential of these new data for studies of demographic, social, and economic change in this period.

To study women’s work both before and after marriage and measure its association with the intervals between births as well as the total number of children born, I built a novel dataset from census and civil registration data. The first chapters of the thesis describe how I developed and adapted methods to reconstruct life courses using automated record linking and to reconstruct birth histories using probabilistically imputed birth dates. In Chapter 3, I pioneered an automated method to link women as well as men over extended periods of time by supplementing direct census-to census links with links to indexes of county marriage registers for the intercensal decade. Because of the limited information contained in the marriage indexes, as opposed to full marriage certificates, couples identified as having recently married in the second of a pair of censuses are linked backwards to pairs of names indexed to the same pages of the marriage register. This method improves linking success for women in their twenties by up to fifteen percentage points. Overall match rates are between 41% and 48% of the starting census population, with substantial numbers linked over more than one decade.

The linked data retrieve a significant amount of information about married women’s childbearing histories, including the ages of children present in the same household which has been observed at multiple points in time. These observations constitute partial birth histories; however, infant mortality, children leaving home, and imperfect record linking leave many gaps. In Chapter 4, I adapted a method to impute approximate birth dates for the missing children of mothers who reported a greater number of children ever born in 1911 than the number observed with them through census linking. For the first time, the use of linked data in the reconstruction process means

that there are far fewer missing births to impute and this approach reduces uncertainty in the resulting fertility timelines, especially for older women who are linked in several censuses after marriage. The reconstructed childbearing histories provide vital evidence for evaluating age- and parity-specific patterns of marital fertility. A geographically restricted case study for the county of Derbyshire was necessary for developing and testing these new datasets, and in future these methods can be applied in wider contexts.

In the subsequent chapters, I bring new analytical approaches to these new data. Chapter 5 uses sequence analysis to simultaneously explore patterns of starting, stopping, and spacing among three birth cohorts from different stages of the fertility transition. The sequence analysis approach allows me to predict the distribution of couples from a younger cohort in a three-cluster fertility typology based on the completed family-building patterns of the first two cohorts. This analysis reveals the emergence of a Low Fertility cluster of couples who married later, took longer to have their first birth, and stopped childbearing after one or two births. This typology was particularly associated with couples from the middle and upper classes, but all occupational groups experienced shifts towards the Moderate or Low Fertility types during this period. This shift involved increases in birth intervals as well as increases in the number of couples stopping at low parities. At the same time, this analysis reveals that there were substantial compositional effects on fertility of spouse’s occupation within own occupational groups. For the first time, both male and female occupations can be observed together, and this chapter shows how their interactions could be synergistic or antagonistic in determining a couple’s likelihood of following a Low Fertility trajectory. I argue that these patterns arise from the demand for married women’s work in certain industries and reflect the consequences of socioeconomic inequality for reproductive decision-making within marriage.

Chapters 6 and 7 use split-population cure models to analyse each event in the life course individually, evaluating the factors associated with the timing and occurrence of marriage and of each birth. These analyses confirm the divergence of occupational groups in the likelihood of stopping after one or two births. Chapter 6 also demonstrates more conclusively the importance of birth spacing among female textile workers, and exploratory analysis using the census data shows that this is partly a direct result of married women’s work in textile factories. On the other hand, differences between other female occupational groups, notably domestic servants, dressmakers, and women with no observed pre-marital occupation, show that there are multiple mechanisms linking occupational histories to demographic outcomes. Most women withdrew from waged work at marriage but may have been holding their labour ‘in reserve’ to supplement family incomes as needed (Tilly & Scott, 1978). Local labour markets and cultural norms influencing intra-household bargaining determined how and when this labour was deployed and its relationship to family size.

Finally, Chapter 8 brings these observations to bear on a sample of factory records for the Strutt cotton spinning mills in Belper, examining the role of the textile industry in the family economy. These records provide greater insight into the life course dynamics of textile employment, including the short term employment of boys as half timers which was often missed in the census enumeration of occupations; the stagnation of women’s wages with age and their eventual withdrawal from work after marriage; and the continuing importance of the labour of girls and young women to their families. Quantitative analysis using observations of age starting work in the Strutt mills offered support for both an economic interpretation of the direct effect of work on delaying marriage and a diffusionist interpretation of the indirect effect of being part of the factory network on both marriage and childbearing.

There are several important limitations to the analyses offered in this thesis, which provide motivation for future research. The setting in Derbyshire has allowed me to make some comparisons between different communities, most notably between textile and coal mining districts, but the persistently rural character of the county contrasts with the rapid urbanisation of Victorian society at the national level. Urbanites were associated with low levels of fertility, and my research has not been able to contribute to explanations of the early onset of fertility decline among these groups. Urbanisation also interacted with migration, which is a challenge for linked data at this geographic scale. Although I have sometimes been able to include measures of intercensal migration in my models, individuals and couples in the linked data are selected on having stayed within the county. Since my dataset begins with the 1881 census, it does not capture the marriage cohorts of the 1870s which seem to have experienced the first movements towards family limitation. The census data also provide very limited information about educational attainment, except in a negative sense for children who were enumerated with an occupation at an early age.

Building on these foundations, future research can address these limitations by extending the dataset both chronologically and geographically, incorporating newly available census data for England and Wales up to 1921. In addition to this widening, the analysis can also be deepened through the incorporation of additional qualitative information about the experiences of individual women and men during the period of the fertility transition. Further exploration of the Strutt archives will allow more complete reconstruction of the factory workforce and their families, and other primary sources such as Medical Officer of Health reports, school attendance registers, newspapers, and health visitor records can provide insight into the wider community surrounding the mill. Narrative, administrative, and cultural sources reveal the experiences and meanings associated with different communities and types of work, expanding our understanding of the mechanisms behind variations in marital fertility.

This balance between the local and the national, and between the quantitative and the qualitative, will help to further elaborate on adaptation vs diffusion explanations for the fertility transition. A ‘blended’ version of the innovation-diffusion framework suggests that structural economic changes, which may have been the ultimate causes of fertility decline, are crucially mediated by cultural and social processes which affect the adoption of different behaviours (Cleland, 2001). The interplay of structure and culture could have contributed to the ‘multiple fertility declines’ posited by previous studies of England and Wales (Garrett et al., 2001, p. 433; Szreter, 1996, Chapter 7). Longitudinal data is necessary to reconstruct the social networks created through work, kinship, and neighbourhood which influenced the formation of perceptions of marriage and childbearing. Better data and methods allow qualitative insights to be operationalised in quantitative models, rather than taking diffusion effects to be the residual variance in observed fertility left over by structural explanatory variables. My results regarding the interaction effects of spouses’ occupational histories, the influence of their local environment, and the family networks created by the cotton mills in Belper have provided insight into some of the diffusion mechanisms which played a role in the fertility transition in Derbyshire.

In this research I have also been able to operationalise theories about gender and the Demographic Transition in new ways and to focus on individuals and couples rather than regions or classes as the ‘effective units of demographic decision making’ (Greenhalgh, 1990). The couple-level regression model developed in Chapter 5 showed that the assumption that male occupations are an effective proxy for female occupations when it comes to fertility behaviour is flawed. In this way, my research goes a step further than Szreter’s critique of the Registrar General’s social classification scheme by demonstrating the impact of compositional differences within occupational groups as well as within social classes (Szreter, 2015). Although Szreter highlighted gender roles as one of the ways in which different ‘communication communities’ contributed to the perceived relative costs of childrearing, his analysis framed gender as largely endogenous to class (Szreter, 1996, Chapter 9). The important factor according to Szreter was how the genders were positioned within changing industrial labour markets, not the ways in which conceptions of gender could be changing independently of socioeconomic forces.

The discussion of women’s occupations before and after marriage above has offered insight into one aspect of the changing relationships between spouses, parents, and children which were unfolding in this period. I have argued that the Traits-Desires-Intentions-Behaviours framework (Miller, 1994) is a useful model for interpreting the impact of both husbands’ and wives’ occupations and backgrounds on marital fertility by highlighting possible sources of agreement or tension in the formation of fertility intentions and the translation of intentions into contraceptive behaviours. In

the language of Coale’s preconditions for fertility decline (1973), this framing reinterprets ‘ability’ not only as the availability or knowledge of contraception, but also as the product of negotiation between two individuals’ readiness and willingness to control reproduction. Gender and power within marriage may be crucial processes within the blended diffusion model for the adoption of family limitation.

In highlighting the importance of gender and labour during the First Demographic Transition, I have extended theories regarding gender equity and the Second Demographic Transition further back in time (Goldscheider et al., 2015; Lappegård, 2020). Although contemporary demographers date the fertility impacts of the gender revolution to the second half of the twentieth century, the second half of the nineteenth century presents an apparent paradox between declining marital fertility and declining married women’s labour force participation. These conflicts can be reconciled by examining how the shift towards a more universal male breadwinning family model changed the affective relationships between spouses as well as the structure of the family wage economy, with implications for family-building decisions (McDonald, 2000; Seccombe, 1995). There are important quantitative and qualitative differences between the choices made by couples who had no access to modern medicine or contraception from those made by couples today, both in high-fertility countries which are still undergoing their demographic transitions and in low-fertility countries which are seeing expanding access to assisted reproductive technologies (Lazzari et al., 2023, 2025). In both contexts, the agency of individual couples interacts with larger socioeconomic structures, but it is a complicated and sometimes circular question whether agency or structure is primarily responsible for demographic change (Greenhalgh, 1990).

The causal mechanisms of fertility decline require further elaboration, but this research has contributed to the view that they are likely to be found within the household as well as within the wider community. The data and methods which I assemble in this thesis have brought us closer to this level of observation than has previously been possible in England Wales. In the future, these methods can be applied to other contexts – for a comparative perspective on fertility decline in urban settings, other textile districts, or in other countries – and expanded to incorporate additional variables, such as migration and infant mortality. In addition to testing old theories, these approaches have the potential to reveal new variables and mechanisms behind demographic change.

railway labourers navvies (contractors labourers) default 432 all other OCCODEs Other Source: Schürer (2024). Note: Although I-CeM provides HISCO (Historical International Standard of Classification of Occupations) codes, the coding is incomplete and some codes are invalid, which is why OCCODE has been chosen as the basis for occupational classification here. Broadly speaking, my occupational groups align with the HISCO major and minor groups (e.g. 0xxxx and 1xxxx, professional occupations, 7-1xxx miners and 7-2xxx metal processors, 7-9xxx dressmakers, 7-5xxx textile workers). My ‘professional’ category also includes workers from 3xxxx clerical occupations, typists, and telegraph operators. There are few cases where a HISCO-based occupational grouping would have classified an individual in a different occupational group (agricultural labourers represent the most common edge case, but they are combined into a larger agriculture-labouring group for analysis).

Table A.3 Conditions for defining types of Registration Subdistrict

| Combined Type | Type | Conditions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Textile | Textile | ≥25% of working men and women ages 15‐64 employed in the textile industry |
| Mining | Mining | ≥30% employed in mining or metals |
| Rural | Agriculture Semi‐Rural | ≥5% employed in agriculture and population density <1 person per acre ≥5% employed in agriculture |
| Urban | Professional Transport Semi‐ Professional Other Urban | ≥7.5% employed in the professions and ≥30% employed in services ≥15% employed in transport ≥7.5% employed in the professions All other |

Source: Reid et al. (2018).
